ABSTRACT 

Public spaces such as shopping centres offer economic, social, and environmental 

benefits to society. To ensure persons can enjoy the use of these spaces without fear of in-

jury, legislation exists within the United Kingdom that requires owners to identify and miti-

gate any potential hazards. An escalator is a common feature to many urban public spaces 

such as shopping centres and airports and provides a convenient means to transition be-

tween floors. Yet, incidents can and do happen on escalators that can lead to life changing 

injuries such as amputations and/or even fatalities. Surprisingly, despite widespread adop-

tion of escalators, research into the cause of injuries is limited. The concern is such that the 

Health and Safety Executive (HSE), the regulator for health and safety legislation in Great 

Britain commissioned a shared research programme aimed at adopting a human factors ap-

proach to identify the performance influencing factors (PIFs) which could lead to escalator 

incidents. To address gaps in current understanding, this thesis aims to produce a new in-

vestigative taxonomy, designed for identifying PIFs for incidents involving members of the 

public. A sequential mixed methods approach was adopted to account for current human 

factors theoretical perspectives and ontological viewpoints to ensure multiple data sources 

are considered. 

The thesis produced a taxonomy that was successfully applied to high and low sever-

ity escalator injury data from multiple sources across the United Kingdom. Using multiple 

correspondence analysis, it was demonstrated that escalator incidents are multifactorial 

events with a combination of PIFs leading to an incident. Further, observational analysis of 

4,716 escalator users using the taxonomy provided insight to escalator near misses (a prel-

ude before an incident), again indicating such events are multifactorial. Further, the obser-

vational study provides a baseline of how persons use an escalator without incidents, a 7

novel and missing component of escalator incident research. A subsequent qualitative study 

exploring the views of those over the age of 65 as the most frequently reported injured per-

sons in escalator incidents was conducted to provide further insight on the prior studies. 

The results of this study highlighted escalators to be selected as they are convenient with a 

lack of consideration by users to being injured on escalators despite the awareness of the 

need to step accurately onto the moving surface and the challenges of age-related decline in 

coordination abilities. 

Overall, this thesis provides a new innovative taxonomy as means to investigate inci-

dents involving members of the public and applies this to the under researched area of es-

calator incidents. In so doing the thesis provides increased knowledge to escalator incidents, 

to wider academic falls literature, duty holders, and the regulator to better inform prevent 

and mitigation strategies. 8

DECLARATION 

Whilst registered as a candidate for the above degree, I have not been registered for any 

other research award. The results and conclusions embodied in this thesis are the work of 

the named candidate and have not been submitted for any other academic award.

CHAPTER: 1 INTRODUCTION 

As a Human Factors Specialist Inspector working for the Health and Safety Executive 

(HSE), my work is primarily focused on the prevention of major accident hazards that could 

lead to multiple fatalities of employees and members of the public. Whilst organisations 

must comply with health and safety law, and in so doing, keep their employees safe; health 

and safety legislation also includes members of the public, who enter work premises to use 

a facility such as shops in a shopping centre or alight a train at a station. It is therefore the 

responsibility of the duty holder (usually the owner of the work premises) to prevent all 

people from being injured, whether they be employees or members of the public. 

A challenge for duty holders is that members of the public are not situated within 

traditional archetypal organisation structures, which form the basis of most health and 

safety management systems. Specifically, members of the public are not bound by the same 

legislative requirements that an employee would be. To comply with legislation, duty 

holders are required to identify and mitigate potential hazards. However, given that 

members of the public are not employees, these circumstances lead to a void as 

commonplace methods/systems have been designed to address potential hazards with an 

employer-employee organisational structure in mind. As a case in point of such challenges, 

the focus of the current thesis is escalators, which are widely adopted for public use but as 

will be discussed, their use can lead to severe injuries and fatalities with a lack of research as 

to why these occur. The challenge for duty holders is to be able to identify hazards for 

members of the public using current investigative taxonomies designed to identify key 

performance influencing factors, which may lead to an incident. A potential shortcoming of 

extant taxonomies is their development is centred on an employer-employee organisational 

structure, and as such, a new investigative approach will be proposed. 16 

1.1 Safety and Public Spaces 

Public spaces are a vital means for human society to interact and progress, providing 

economic, social, and environmental benefits to communities (Goodsell, 2003; Francis et al. 

2012). For example, leisure and shopping centres (Musil, 2011) offer persons with the 

opportunity to engage in physical activities, and facilitate social interactions, providing health 

and economic benefits to both the individual and the wider community (Musil, 2011; 

Thomson et al., 2003). A shopping centre that is privately owned but encourages public access 

to its amenities (Karaçor, 2016), is responsible for the safety of that space in accordance with 

the Health and Safety at Work Act (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). 

In the context of managing safety in a public space, Section 3 of the Health and 

Safety at Work etc. Act (1974) infers that an employer who allows members of the public to 

enter their workplace (e.g., a shopping centre or train station) must ensure that any persons 

not in their employment, so far as reasonably practicable, do not come to any harm when 

passing through or using a common transport feature of the environment such as a staircase 

or escalator. In the event an incident occurs (e.g., a falling injury) an organisation should 

seek to identify the causes and any mitigation practices that could be put in place to prevent 

future occurrences (Health and Safety Executive, 2004, Health and Safety Executive, 2013). 

Should an organisation fail to ensure the safety of members of the public, they can face 

considerable financial and custodial sentences for those found responsible (Health and 

Safety Executive, 2013; Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). 

The circumstances described leads to health and safety practitioners adopting a 

pragmatic stance with regards to the management of health and safety issues. The 

identification of hazards and subsequent mitigation strategies are informed by evidence 

(Titler, 2008; Wang et al., 2017) an approach adopted by this thesis. By way of example, BS 17 

5395-1:2000 (British Standards Institute, 2000) is the code of practice for the design, 

construction, and maintenance of straight and winding stairs. Indeed, such industry 

standards are informed by research studies which have highlighted that increasing the 

contrast of the step edging can improve a person’s stepping clearance (Bjelica et al., 2021; 

Foster et al., 2014; Harper et al. 2022); improving the illumination of stairs increases foot 

clearance (Hamel et al. 2005; Thomas et al., 2020), whilst investigation of stair handrail use, 

height, shape, and grasp-ability has enhanced understanding of handrail design in order to 

help a person recover in the event of a fall (Dusenberry et al. 2009; Komisar et al. 2019; 

Maki et al. 1998; Reid et al., 2011). 

The failure to prevent personal injury creates a burden for the individual and wider 

economies (Chandran et al., 2010; Takala et al., 2014). For example, as will be highlighted in 

Chapter 2, falling injuries cost the UK economy £4.4 billion a year (Svedbom et al., 2013) and 

can lead to the adoption of maladaptive traits such as avoiding certain situations in a bid to 

prevent a fall (Schoene et al., 2019). Furthermore, falling injuries are more frequent in those 

over the age of 65 in both indoor (e.g., a public restroom) and outdoor (e.g., pavements or 

parks) public spaces (Schepers et al., 2017; Timsina et al., 2017). This includes the use of 

common public place features such as escalators, by which there are an estimated 2,500 – 

6,000 annual non-fatal escalator incidents in the United States 1 of which 75.0% to 84.9% 

result from a fall (McCann, 2013; O’Neil, et al., 2008) and between 43.0% (Howland et al., 

2012) and 57.7% (Lee et al., 2010) of persons injured are over the age of 65. This is a 

problem that may only increase as demographic projections indicate one in four persons in  

> 1no annual escalator statistics are readily available for injuries in the UK

18 

the UK population will be over the age of 65 in 2038, which is an increase from one in five in 

2018 (Office for National Statistics, 2019). 

1.2 Risk and Safety Management of Public Space use: Escalators 

Escalators are common features to shopping centres, train stations and airports, 

facilitating a primary means of transition from one floor to another for the convenience of 

members of the public (Howland et al., 2012; Jennings et al. 2017; Pelczarski, 2018). Yet, 

despite widespread adoption there are reports of escalator incidents having lifechanging 

consequences, such as amputations (Kates, 1968) and even death (McCann, 2013). As will 

be discussed in Chapter 2, research to explore and identify the causes of escalator incidents 

and thus control the risk of such machines is limited to a small number of papers (Howland 

et al., 2012; Pelczarski , 2018). Most current escalator literature has focussed on an 

epidemiological review of incident data, primarily taken from hospital injury reports (e.g., 

Lee et al., 2010; Schminke et al., 2013). As such, incidents are speculatively interpreted 

through a review of the frequency of recorded incident attributes (e.g., age, intoxication, 

escalator direction – ascending/descending). This research approach has led to 

contradictions between identified hazards and does little to inform safety practitioners as to 

why a factor may be pertinent (Chi et al., 2006; Wang et al., 2022) as highlighted in Chapter 

2. An alternative but under-researched approach has been to view escalator incidents using 

a human factors perspective, whereby incidents are understood to be multifactorial, 

involving a combination of performance influencing factors (PIFs) (HSE, 1999), with direct 

and indirect influences on an escalator incident occurring (Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 

2022). However, this research has primarily focused on data collected from metro stations 

(Chi et al, 2006; Wang et al., 2022) with a recognition of no uniform means to categorise 

escalator PIFs (Wang et al., 2022). 19 

The lack of knowledge of escalator incident causes and prevention is of such interest 

the Health and Safety Executive (The regulator for Health and Safety legislation in Great 

Britain 2 (GB)) commissioned an escalator incident shared research programme with six 

industry partners for which the author of this thesis led and underpins the presented work. 

The aim of the HSE’s shared research project and the basis of this thesis is to adopt a human 

factors approach to identify, categorise, and understand the PIFs (HSE, 1999) that can 

influence the occurrence of an escalator incident. In so doing, this body of work has the 

potential to inform mitigation strategies to underpin legislative compliance (Health and 

Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974) and can be used to inform escalator standards such as Global 

Essentials Safety Requirements (GESR) PD ISO/TS 8103-6:2017 (British Standards Institution, 

2018) to ensure the safety of members of the public when using escalators. 

1.2.1 Escalator Incident Analysis: a Human Factors Perspective 

The terms ergonomics and human factors are often used interchangeably as they are 

seen as equivocal of one another, and this is reflected in many text books (Shorrock & 

Williams, 2016). By way of example, the International Ergonomics Association (IEA) 

considers human factors as a synonym of ergonomics, which is defined as: 

“The definition of ergonomics (or human factors) adopted by the IEA in 2000 is the 

scientific discipline concerned with the understanding of interactions among humans and 

other elements of a system, and the profession that applies theory, principles, data, and 

methods to design in order to optimize human well-being and overall system performance.” 

(International Ergonomics Association, n.d. ) 

> 2HSE does not regulate Northern Ireland.

20 

This definition infers a focus on how a human performs an action/task and aims to 

identify and categorise those Performance Influencing Factors (PIFs) (HSE, 1999) which may 

influence the outcome (i.e., success or failure), in a bid to reduce user error and prevent injury 

(Noyes, 2002; Stanton et al., 2013; Wilson & Corlett, 1995). Application of this definition to 

the exploration of escalator incidents would entail that researchers actively identify what PIFs 

lead to a member of the public getting injured when they use an escalator. 

Whilst several human factors investigative taxonomies are available to safety 

practitioners for investigating incidents (Stanton et al., 2013), which will be discussed in 

Chapter 2, it can be argued that extant investigative taxonomies are not typically developed 

with members of the public in mind. As such they assume a more conventional employee-

employer relationship (Ball & Ball-King, 2013; Ball-King, 2022) with a traditional 

organisational structure in place (HSE, 1999; Kyriakidis et al., 2018). Yet, a member of the 

public is an outlier in this dynamic (Ball & Ball-King, 2013). They are not required to have 

adequate supervision or training, neither are they likely to follow a procedure that for 

example, informs how many items they can carry in a safe manner as they are not classed as 

an employee (Ball & Ball-King, 2013; Ball-King, 2022; Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 

1974). Nonetheless, the onus remains for an employer to investigate any incidents that 

occur, to identify and mitigate any potential risks to ensure a member of the public’s safety 

as they enter/use/pass through the public space environment (e.g., a shopping centre or 

train station) (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). As such, this provides the need to 

develop a new human factors investigative taxonomy that is specifically designed for 

investigating incidents by identifying PIFs that lead to a member of the public getting injured 

to help employers with their legal duty to manage the risks to these individuals (Health and 

Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). 21 

The development of a taxonomy will need to consider both theoretical and 

ontological human factors perspectives (Chapter 2). Specifically, this thesis will adopt a 

systems perspective to human factors, highlighted by recent escalator incident research to 

better inform escalator incident causes (Wang et al., 2022). This perspective assumes 

incidents result from a combination of PIFs, with varying degrees of influence on the 

incident (Read et al., 2021; Salmon et al., 2017). In addition, a systems perspective focuses 

on identifying when things go right as well as when things go wrong. In human factors 

ontology this is referred to as Safety-I (when things go wrong), Safety-II (when things go 

right) (Hollnagel, 2008) and Safety-III (a combination of both Safety-I & II) (Leveson, 2020). 

Considering taxonomic development approaches traditionally stem from the review of 

narrative incident reports (i.e., Safety-I) (Mitchell et al., 2015; Hale et al., 2012), which are 

sources known to have inconsistency and bias (Davies et al., 2007; Woodcock, 2008), 

additional data sources will be sought in this thesis that reflect a Safety-II ontology such as 

observational (Cocca et al., 2016, see Chapter 5) and qualitative sources (Debnath et al., 

2015, see Chapter 6). In so doing this approach will provide a more complete understanding 

of incidents, better informing possible mitigation strategies (Aven, 2022). 

To achieve the thesis’ aim of developing a new investigative taxonomy for incidents 

involving members of the public, a sequential mixed methods approach (Hong et al. 2017; 

Sandelowski et al. 2006). This approach will allow for each research study to be informed by 

the previous, building on an completing the preceding research using both quantitative (i.e., 

review of incident data - Safety-I) and qualitative (i.e., interviews - Safety-II) data sources 

(Creswell & Clark, 2017). Extant human factors perspectives tend to stem from a pragmatist, 

evidence-based viewpoint in safety (Titler, 2008; Wang et al., 2017) and mixed methods 

literature (Bishop, 2015; Heyvaert et al., 2013; Yardley & Bishop, 2015), to provide a 22 

valuable means of hazard identification and thus inform potential mitigation strategies. 

That is, this thesis will expand the under-researched area of escalator incidents providing 

greater insight as to the probable causes of escalator incidents in tandem with a means to 

investigate public space incidents and support organisations in adequately meeting 

legislative requirements (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974) 

1.2.2 Summary 

Public spaces are areas that have benefit to the individual and wider society. To 

ensure the enjoyment of these public spaces, there is a legislative requirement for duty 

holders to ensure that members of the public remain safe when passing through or when 

using features in public spaces such as an escalator. Although common to public spaces, 

escalator injuries and fatalities can and do happen. Yet, there is limited research on the 

causes and understanding of escalator incidents. Most of the available literature follows an 

epidemiological approach of identifying and speculatively discussing PIFs, which whilst 

informative, does not explain why factors are pertinent. Alternatively, although 

underdeveloped, a human factors approach has been adopted by a few researchers by 

which escalator incidents are seen as multifactorial resulting from a combination of PIFs. Yet 

no uniform means to categorise escalator incident PIFs is currently available. The HSE, 

regulator for Health and Safety Legislation in GB commissioned a shared research project to 

explore the human factors influences on escalator user safety. Whilst there are several 

human factors taxonomies to aid in identifying PIFs these have been designed with the 

traditional employer-employee organisational workplace in mind, and thus a new taxonomy 

needs to be produced for incidents involving members of the public. To develop this 

taxonomy, this research will adopt a sequential mixed methods approach considering 23 

current human factors theoretical perspectives and ontological viewpoints. In so doing, this 

taxonomy will inform both the wider academic literature, duty holders, the regulator and 

escalator standards boards about the potential causes of escalator incidents involving 

members of the public to better inform intervention and mitigation strategies. 24 

1.3 Aims of This Thesis 

PhD overall Research Aim: 

1. Develop a Human Factors taxonomy for investigating incidents involving members of 

the public using escalators. 

PhD Secondary Aims 

1. To advance current knowledge and potential mitigation strategies regarding escala-

tor incidents 

2. Apply the taxonomy to low and high-risk escalator incidents that encompass both 

hospitalised injuries and routine first-aid treatment. 

3. Critically evaluate the applicability of the taxonomy against observational coding of 

escalator usage 

4. Explore the subjective experiences of older adults when using escalators to identify 

possible factors that may mitigate against escalator incidents. 

1.4 Structure of This Thesis 

This thesis comprises of 7 Chapters containing four studies that develop, implement, 

and then evaluate a human factors taxonomy to escalator incidents. Following this 

introduction, Chapter 2 aims to provide a review of literature encapsulating current 

understanding of escalator incidents, how escalator incidents are currently investigated, the 

importance and benefits of a human factors approach to incidents investigation and finally 

previous approaches to taxonomy development. Chapter 3 presents the first study of this 

thesis, which introduces the development of the Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for 

Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) using severe incident data (i.e., leading to hospitalisation) 

reported to the HSE. Chapter 4 applies the HFIT-MP to less severe incidents by way of 

building on Chapter 3, so as not to underestimate injuries nor ignore PIFs that could lead to 

less severe escalator injuries. Chapter 5 then applies the HFIT-MP to video footage of 

escalator uses objective data that further builds upon the analysis of narrative incident 25 

reports used in Chapters 3 and 4. Chapter 6 then provides a further means to explore the 

findings from Chapters 3-5, comprising qualitative semi-structured interviews with older 

adults over the age of 65, an age-group who appeared more frequently in escalator 

incidents. Subsequently Chapter 7 provides an epilogue combining the findings from 

Chapters 3-6, highlighting contributions to literature, practical application, and future 

research avenues. 26 

CHAPTER: 2 REVIEW OF LITERATURE 

2.1 Research Context 

The following literature review will start with an overview of safety in public spaces, 

legislative requirements, and the impact of falling injuries on both the economy and the 

individual ( Section 2.1.1 ). Escalators are a common public space transportation feature yet 

as detailed in Section 2.1.2 severe and even fatal incidents can and do happen but there is 

limited research into the causes of escalator incidents. Proceeding this, Section 2.2 will 

discuss how safety in public space is currently managed including the requirement to 

investigate incidents that occur. Attention is then focussed on how escalator incidents are 

currently investigated ( Section 2.2.1 ) and how such incidents are multifactorial ( Section 

2.2.1.1 ). Building on from Section 2.1.1 that highlights a human factors approach has been 

undertaken in more recent escalator incident literature, Section 2.3 introduces the reader to 

the concept of Human Factors approach to incident investigation. The subsections provide 

further detail as to the theoretical ( Section 2.3.1 ) and ontological ( Section 2.3.2 )

perspectives which shape current human factors thinking. The final section ( 2.3.3 ) then 

explores current human factors investigative approaches in lieu of their being limited 

escalator incident research adopting such an approach. In so doing this final section sets out 

the need for producing an investigative taxonomy for incidents involving members of the 

public and how this thesis aims to achieve this. 

2.1.1 Falls in Public Spaces 

Much of everyday human behaviour takes place in public spaces such as roads, 

sidewalks (pavements), parks, and stairways (Schepers, et al., 2017). These public spaces 

provide benefits such as increased physical activity (Dunton et al, 2009; Han et al., 2014), 

positive impacts to wellbeing (Larson et al., 2016; Wood et al., 2017), facilitation of social 27 

interactions (Musil, 2011), and wider societal economic benefits (Goodsell, 2003; Francis et 

al. 2012). 

Although most human activity in public spaces takes place without incident, there is 

evidence to suggest that there are some members of the population that are at greater 

potential risk of an incident or injury than others. For instance, a review of pedestrian falls 

literature encompassing outside public spaces (e.g., pavements, parks, and stairways), 

reported that people aged 60 to 65 were found to have fallen five times more frequently than 

those under the age of 25 (Schepers et al., 2017). Similarly, in indoor spaces (e.g., a person’s 

home, public restroom, sporting facility) the proportion of people that have experienced a 

falling incident has been found to increase from 22.3% for those aged 18-44 to 47.6% for 

those aged 65 and older (Timsina et al., 2017). This injury pattern extends to the common 

public space transportation feature of an escalator (DeMers et al., 2011) discussed further in 

the proceeding section. These findings concur with wider falls literature, which highlights that 

28% to 35% of those over the age of 65 are likely to fall once a year, a statistic that increases 

with age and rises to between 32% and 42% for those aged over 75 (Kenny et al., 2017; Masud 

& Morris, 2001). Moreover, considering the older adult demographic (aged 65 and over), 

females appear to be at greater risk, with 61.9% of females experiencing a fall indoors 

compared to 39.8% of males (Timsina et al., 2017). 

In the UK, legislation and regulations exist to ensure the safety of members of the 

public as they move through a person’s workplace (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 

1974; The Management of Health and Safety at Work Regulations, 1999). A workplace may 

also be a public space, for example a train station or a shopping centre. This legislation and 

subsequent regulations require duty holders (i.e. legal entities (owners) of such premises, 

commonly an employer, though not always) to identify and manage any potential hazards 28 

(something that can cause adverse effects) and subsequent risks (the likelihood a hazard will 

cause adverse effects) to members of the public to as low as is reasonably practicable 

(Health and Safety Executive, n.d.a; Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974; The 

Management of Health and Safety at Work Regulations, 1999). In the UK, Section 3 of the 

Health, and Safety at Work etc. Act etc. (1974) states: 

‘It shall be the duty of every employer to conduct his undertaking in such a way as to ensure, 

so far as is reasonably practicable, that persons not in his employment who may be affected 

thereby are not thereby exposed to risks to their health or safety. ’

Therefore, if an organisation has several falling incidents/injuries reported, in 

particular with those over the age of 65 whilst using an escalator it would suggest that the 

hazards and risks are not being controlled and action is required to investigate and 

understand the causes of such occurrences (Delikhoon et al., 2022; Leveson, 2020) before 

enforcement action is taken (Health and Safety Executive, 2013) which can lead to 

substantial fines, and/or imprisonment (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). 

To add context beyond legislative compliance, Public Health England 3 (PHE) have 

estimated that there are around 255,000 annual fall-related hospital admissions for persons 

over 65 in the UK (Public Health England, 2017). Exploring hospital admissions data over a 5-

year period (01 st April 2016 to the 31 st of March 2021), indicates that there has been an 

average of 41,950 hospital admissions in England as a result of a fall from step or stair (a 

common feature in a public space), and falls have been in the top 10 causes for attending  

> 3A UK Government department originally responsible for protecting and improving the UK’s health and
> wellbeing, and reduce inequalities in health (Public Health England, n.d) replaced by the UK Health Security
> Agency and Office for Health Improvement and Disparities in 2021.

29 

hospital in England (NHS Digital, 2017; 2018; 2019; 2020; 2021). Over the stated 5-year 

period, 91% of falls from a step or stair were emergency admissions (i.e., unplanned), the 

mean length of hospital stay was four days, the mean age of the injured person was 61 years 

old, and around 60% of patients were female. The estimated financial cost of falls to the 

National Health Service (NHS) is £2.3 billion a year (National Institute for Health and Care 

Excellence (NICE), 2013), with the wider cost to the UK estimated around £4.4 billion 

(Svedbom et al., 2013). One of the possible reasons for this high figure is due to the vast 

expenditure associated with supporting those persons that have a fall. For instance, it is 

estimated that around £1.1 billion is spent on social scare and about £2 billion spent on the 

treatment and rehabilitation associated with hip fractures (Public Health England, 2017). 

These findings are a cause for national concern as demographic predictions indicate that the 

number of persons aged 65 and over in the UK is increasing from one in five in 2018 to one in 

four by 2038 (Office for National Statistics, 2019). Moreover, by 2068, it is estimated that the 

number of persons over the age of 65 will be 20.4 million, which could reflect 26.4% of the 

total UK population (Office for National Statistics, 2019). 

Furthermore, it is not just the financial cost nor physical injury of a fall that is of 

concern. Research has suggested that having a fall can give rise to a fear of falling (FoF) 

(Legters, 2002), which can lead to a reduced quality of life (QoL) as individuals avoid certain 

physical activities such as walking and climbing of stairs (Sleney et al., 2014) or restrict their 

participation in social activities in a bid to mitigate another fall occurring (Hughes et al, 2015; 

Jung, 2008). A recent systematic literature review of 30 studies by Schoene et al. (2019) 

examining the results of over 29,000 persons over the age of 60 from 16 countries regarding: 

(i) Fall; (ii) FoF; and (iii) QoL, suggests a more complex relationship exists. Schoene et al. (2019) 

suggests fall and FoF to be independent in predicting a person’s QoL. That is to say, someone 30 

can still be anxious about falling but not have experienced a fall and it is this anxiety that can 

impact their QoL as they adapt their behaviours in a preventative manner to reduce the 

likelihood of a fall. This behavioural adaption can be both advantageous in terms of keeping 

someone safe (i.e., not putting oneself in a risky situation) but also can lead to inactivity 

subsequently increasing falls risk through deconditioning (Goswami, 2017), and can become 

maladaptive when such anxiety leads to undesirable behaviours in the context of moving 

through public environments (Schoene et al., 2019). For example, Ellmers et al. (2020) found 

older adults who are anxious about falling will see a detrimental impact on their movement. 

Participants in this study who were anxious about falling focused on the immediate 1-2 steps 

in front of them (a step being the distance from the foot strike of one foot to the foot strike 

of the opposite foot) (Chambers & Sutherland, 2002), ensuring they maintained a maximum 

stepping accuracy at the expense of attending to the wider environment leading to increase 

in stepping errors when stepping onto a target beyond this focal 1-2 step period. This may 

lead to challenges when they encounter a common transportation feature such as an 

escalator where a high degree of foot placement accuracy is required to alleviate a fall (Hsu 

et al., 2015, Hunt et al., 2023). Hsu et al. (2015) provide a baseline analysis of a person getting 

onto a moving platform, finding that anticipatory locomotor adjustment is required at least a 

stride before embarking the moving surface for the action to be successful. Recent 

biomechanical work by Hunt et al., (2023) found that older adults who are required to step 

onto a moving surface such as that of an escalator, are at greater risk of fall compared to 

younger adults due to reduced action capabilities in the older adults. If a person is instead 

anxious about falling, according to Ellmers et al. (2020) they will be focusing on their 

immediate steps, rather than gazing ahead to anticipate the embarkation of the moving 

escalator surface. Further, Ellmers et al. (2022) conducted a qualitative study, interviewing 17 31 

older adults (mean age of 79) exploring their perceptions and worries about falling. Ellmers 

et al’s (2022) findings suggest falling anxiety develops in response at an individual’s 

recognition of their fallibility to have a fall, be that having experienced a fall themselves or 

aware of others that have. Ellmers et al. (2022) suggest that anxiety about falling is linked to 

a person’s perceived control. If someone perceives themselves to have a high level of control 

over their ability to prevent a fall, they will engage in protective behavioural adaptations such 

as focusing on foot placement to ensure accuracy. Conversely if they feel they have limited 

control, anxiety takes over which may be leading to over cautious gait or failures to plan 

stepping actions. Therefore, considering escalator use as common public space feature, if a 

person feels they have limited control over the escalators’ already moving surface, they may 

find their anxiety increases, in turn impacting their ability to successfully embark the moving 

surface (Hsu et al., 2015) 

It is important to highlight the above findings as it helps demonstrate that the 

prevention of injuries, such as falls in public spaces can meet legislative requirements and be 

beneficial to both the wider economy and the individual. 

2.1.2 Escalator Incidents 

As mentioned, escalators are a common feature in urban public spaces such as 

shopping centres and airports (DeMers et al., 2011; Howland et al., 2012). There are an 

estimated 800 thousand installed worldwide (Elevatori, 2018), providing a convenient means 

of transport between floors (Bodendorf et al., 2014), moving an estimated 3.5 billion persons 

daily (Elevatori, 2018). However, escalator use is not without some risk; a review of US 

Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC) data by McCann (2013) identified 39 escalator 

related deaths over a 13-year period in between 1997 and 2010, 74.4% of which were 32 

attributed to a fall. Furthermore, US Hospital admission data indicated there to be 2,500 – 

6,000 non-fatal escalator incidents reported annually, and between 75.0% and 84.9% of these 

incidents were the result of falls when using the escalator (McCann, 2013; O’Neil, et al., 2008). 

Although, a recent review of National Electronic Injury Surveillance System data from the US 

CPSC (Caswell et al., 2021) suggests overall escalator related incidents may be reducing in the 

US from 1778 in 2009-2012 to 1591 in 2013-2016, and then to 1110 in 2017-2019, it is not 

clear why this may be the case. Caswell and colleagues (2021) suggested that the reduction 

may be due to increased awareness of escalator risks, but it could just as be that fewer 

incidents are being reported to the CPSC (Robinson, 2017). 

Outside the US, information is limited regarding national rates of escalator-related 

incidents with research instead focusing on localised reporting such as urban transport 

systems (Chi et al., 2006; Li et al., 2021) or injury data from a single hospital (Lee et al., 2010; 

Schminke et al., 2013) (see Table 2.1). At present, in-depth escalator incident research in the 

United Kingdom (UK) is limited to one Health and Safety Executive (HSE) (the regulator for 

Health and Safety legislation) research report (Page & Hough, 1989). Yet, consistent with the 

US, falls are the most frequent form of escalator incident, ranging from 77.0% to 97.6% of 

incidents (Table 2.1). In addition, injury data (including that of the US) suggests that between 

43.0% and 56.7% of those that fall are over 65 years of age (Table 2.1). 33

Given that escalators are common to many public spaces (Chi et al., 2006; Howland et 

al., 2012; Lee et al., 2010) it is surprising only limited research has examined escalator 

incidents in detail (Chi et al., 2006; Page & Hough, 1989, Xing et al. 2019; Li et al., 2021), 

particularly given that fatalities can occur (Caswell et al., 2021; Schminke et al., 2013; McCann, 

2013; Wells et al., 1986). Alternatively, it could be viewed that the number of persons injured 

is perhaps minimal compared to the estimated 3.5 billion daily global users (Elevatori, 2018). 

Yet, escalator incidents can lead to severe lacerations (Collins, 1969) and/or fractures 

(Murphy & Moore, 1992) leading to amputations (Kates, 1968), which may produce a burden 

for the individual (i.e., an increase in fear of falls leading to maladaptive practices, Hughes et 

al, 2015) and wider economies (Chandran et al., 2010; Takala et al., 2014) including a country’s 

healthcare system (Svedbom et al., 2013). 

As mentioned in Section 2.1.1 Falls in public spaces , in the UK there is a legal 

requirement to prevent injuries to members of the public or face prosecution, leading to fines 

and/or imprisonment (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). The concern of a lack 

understanding and thus failure to prevent escalator injuries is such that the Health and Safety 

Executive (HSE) the regulator for Health and Safety Legislation in Great Britain commissioned 

a shared research project with the aim to identify and understand performance influencing 

factors (PIFs) (HSE, 1999) that can lead to an escalator incident occurring. The current 

programme of research will seek to inform escalator incident understanding by providing a 

means to investigate and understand the causes of escalator incidents to better inform injury 

prevention. Before going into the approach to be adopted, the next section will outline 

current approaches to managing safety in public spaces, followed by a section regarding how 

escalator incidents are currently investigated to better inform the rationale for the presented 

work. 35 

2.2 Safety Management for Public Spaces: Escalators 

To aid a duty holder in the application of their legal responsibility and by extension 

ensure the safety of persons entering a working environment. The regulator (Health and 

Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974), HSE, provides approved codes of practice, general/sector 

specific guidance and research reports to aid in the identification and management of 

hazards and risks (Health and Safety Executive, n.d.b). Published safety management 

literature suggests there are several approaches for health and safety practitioners to 

investigate incidents with the aim of each being to understand the causes and provide 

insight into prevention (Katsakiori et al., 2009; Sklet, 2004; Stanton et al., 2013). In a 

comparison of well used and researched methods for incident investigation, Sklet (2004) 

suggested that the selection of which technique to use should be based on the incident and 

the methods should provide some form of graphical representation, facilitate 

communication, and help to identify information gaps in an investigation. For example, if an 

investigator were looking at potential higher order influences such as the impact of 

government policy and how decisions lead to an incident, they should look to use the 

AcciMap methodology (Figure 2.1) as this requires the review and impact of decisions made 

from government at the top end, to the influences of the task being undertaken at the 

other, in the form of a graphical representation (Stanton et al., 2013). 36 

> Figure 2.1:
> Risk Management Framework and AcciMap Method, Adapted From Rasmussen (1997).

Katsakiori et al. (2009) also provide a review of accident causation models and incident 

investigation and suggest how thinking has moved from there being one immediate cause of 

an incident to the recognition of multiple interacting causes. Akin to Sklet (2004), Katsakiori 

et al. (2009), suggest the selection of which method to take should be based on the needs of 

the investigation. Irrespective of the approach selected the focus is to identify, understand 

and control risk in a bid to prevent incidents and is usually integral to a much wider safety 

management system (Li & Guldenmund, 2018). 

In the context of falls risks in public spaces (Schepers et al., 2017; Timsina et al., 

2017), the duty holder would be required to identify and manage the hazards and 

subsequent risks that could influence a fall in this space. Such hazards may only come to 

light after an incident has occurred and thus highlight a need for a safety control measure 

(Health and Safety Executive, 2013). For example, an escalator could be considered a falling 

hazard to older adults (over the age of 65) as this population appears more frequently in 

injury data than other age groups (Table 2.1). Whilst there are several escalator safety 37 

standards available (Filippone et al., 2012) their focus is on escalator construction, 

installation, and maintenance based on anthropometric measurements (British Standards 

Institute, 2017; British Standards Institute, 2021), designed to meet legislative requirements 

(The Workplace (Health, Safety and Welfare) Regulations, 1992). Currently, limited 

consideration is given to the mitigation of passenger related factors that may prevent an 

incident beyond the inclusion of four directive safety signs as a control measure: (i) small 

children shall be held firmly; (ii) dogs shall be carried; (iii) use the handrail; and (iv) push 

chairs not permitted (British Standards Institute, 2017) (Figure 2.2). 

> Figure 2.2:
> Example of Safety Signs Required by BS EN 115-1:2017 (British Standards Institute, 2017).

This is not to say a standardised approach to escalator safety is not useful. Chew et 

al. (2021) demonstrated that the lack of standardised escalator safety signs in Malaysia is an 

issue that needs addressing, calling for a more tailored message to both children and older 

adults who they identify as occur in more incidents. The concern is to make sure that safety 

standards are effective to managing escalator user safety by preventing injuries. This has 

been recognised by the British Standards Institute who have developed and continue to 

update a Global Essentials Safety Requirements (GESR) PD ISO/TS 8103-6:2017 (British 38 

Standards Institute, 2018) that uses information from independent research to inform safe 

escalator use which the current programme of work can inform. 

As escalator research is limited (Howland et al., 2012; Pelczarski , 2018), it is possible 

that some escalator hazards are under-represented in extant data whilst others may be 

over-represented. For example, failure to hold the handrail has been attributed to 10.3-

20.0% of incidents in some literature (Chi et al., 2006; Li et al., 2021; Xing et al., 2019) and 

the risk appears such a concern a warning sign is required to be displayed (Figure 2.2) by BS 

EN 115-1:2017 (British Standards Institute, 2017). In contrast, carrying out other tasks when 

using the escalator, such as carrying luggage or looking after children, has been attributed to 

19.6%-32.0% of incidents (Chi et al., 2006; Li et al., 2021; Xing et al., 2019). However, this 

latter finding only has limited reference in British standards (i.e., to ensure small children 

are held firmly: BS EN 115-1:2017 British Standards Institute, 2017). Whilst well intentioned, 

this safety sign could even be counterproductive as an adult focuses on the small child’s 

escalator use and not their own escalator use, potentially leading to a fall. For instance, 

Page and Hough (1989) identified that 215 injured persons were traveling with a 

companion, 51 of those were accompanied by children. Yet, it is not clear why traveling with 

a person may be influential to escalator injuries, thus illustrating a proactive need to identify 

the hazards associated with escalator falls (Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2022; Xie & Liu, 

2019) which the current thesis seeks to address. Absence of effective hazard identification 

methods and risk reduction measures has the potential to further complicate an increasing 

public health issue for older adults in particular, with associated implications for the 

economy, and wider society (Public Health England, 2020) as well as a breach of legislative 

requirements (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). 39 

One possible premise could be to use stair safety literature, for which there is plenty 

to plug the research gap (Jacobs, 2016). For example, one intervention that could be used 

by industry health and safety practitioners is to encourage handrail use, a safety feature of 

stairs to aid fall prevention (Maki et al., 1998; Nemire et al., 2016). Indeed, a lack of handrail 

use has been cited in escalator injury literature as a potential major cause of escalator 

incidents (Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 2019). However, unlike stairs, the handrail is also 

moving on an escalator. Therefore, a person using an escalator may find their attentional 

demand divided between stepping onto the moving escalator (Hsu et al., 2015; Hunt et al., 

2023) and grasping the moving handrail (Hesse & Deubel, 2011). Moreover, age related 

declines in mobility and co-ordination (Kenny et al., 2017), may be a particular challenge for 

those over the age of 65 and therefore the suitability of the handrail as a primary safety 

intervention could be questioned. Further differences in safety features between stairs and 

escalators includes the step height. Specifically, an escalator is manufactured with a step 

height of 24cm (British Standards Institute, 2017). In contrast, the safe step height for a 

staircase should be no higher than 17.8cm (Jacobs, 2016). The British Standard for escalator 

construction and instillation states that an escalator is not designed to be used as a staircase 

and further recommends they are not considered as such for emergency exit procedures 

(British Standards Institute, 2017). Therefore, whilst on first consideration an escalator may 

seem no more than a moving staircase, the example differences outlined mean that whilst 

similar, they are two distinct entities and as such, dedicated research is needed to examine 

the causes of escalator injuries to better inform interventions and guidelines that can be 

used by industry. Subsequently, this thesis will intend to provide a means to investigate and 

understand escalator incidents ( Chapter 3 ) with current escalator incident investigative 

approaches detailed in the proceeding section. 40 

2.2.1 Investigating Public Space Incidents: Escalators 

Previous escalator incident research has predominantly reported escalator incidents 

in one of two ways (Xie & Liu, 2019; Wang et al., 2020): 1) via type/outcome of the incident; 

or 2) via a human factors approach. The first is typically epidemiological, where type of 

escalator incidents (i.e., fall, entrapment or other) are broken down into factors that may 

have contributed to an incident occurring. For example, several articles have described the 

location, type and severity of injury sustained during an escalator incident, such as 

lacerations, soft tissue injuries, and fractures (Algin et al. 2019; Campbell, 1973; Caswell et 

al. 2021; Howland et al., 2012; Kates, 1968; Lee et al., 2010; Murphy & Moore, 1992; O’Neil 

et al., 2008; Page & Hough, 1989; Schminke et al., 2013; Wells et al., 1986). In addition, 

these epidemiological articles report supplementary factors recorded at the time of incident 

which may be relevant. Some of the most consistently reported factors are presented in 

Table 2.2. 41 

Table 2.2 

Consistently Reported Escalator Incident Factors
