Whilst these studies offer a means to depict and understand escalator injuries, 

arguably these works go little beyond identification and there can be some speculative 

interpretation, leading to contradictions in the literature when identifying escalator hazards. 

For example, Lee et al. (2010) and Page and Hough (1989) identified getting on to the 

escalator as an issue in 14% - 24.2% of escalator incidents respectively. However, Page and 

Hough further highlighted that 49.0% of incidents happened during the traveling phase of 

the escalator (i.e., successfully boarded and then standing still). Murphy and Moore (1992) 

indicated that both stepping on and off are likely to cause an incident (10% and 14% of 

incidents respectively). Similarly, O’Neil et al. (2008) highlighted that stepping on or off an 

escalator as a potential factor in 14% of injuries but no distinction was made as to which 

was more prevalent. This illustrates that whilst embarkation/traveling/disembarkation on an 

escalator are hazardous phases, it is not overtly clear what the cause is, whether there is an 

issue with the escalator design (Dhanasekaran et al., 2016; Pelczarski, 2018), or whether the 

incident is caused by how the user interacts with the escalators’ moving steps (Hsu et al., 

2015; Hunt et al., 2023). 

A recent study by Ayres (2019) went beyond just reviewing of incident reports and 

instead reviewed 100 escalator injuries that were posted to social media assigning one 

cause to each incident (e.g., falling whilst sliding on the escalator). The author then 

compared this with 100 different National Electronic Injury Surveillance System escalator 

incident reports that they also assigned one cause. Ayres argued the video data provides 

greater detail to the incident compared to the narrative report, highlighting that of the 78 

incident reports that indicated a fall occurred little if any information was provided as to 

how it happened. This is in comparison to the video data by which there were 11 potential 

causes for a fall (e.g., falling whilst traveling in the wrong direction). Yet, whilst promising no 43 

other current incident literature has sought to use escalator video data to review incidents. 

Furthermore, the work by Ayres (2019) is limited as although it gives greater detail 

regarding a falling incident it appears somewhat reductionist to assume the fall is due to 

one cause alone (Reason, 1990; Sklet, 2004). 

A second approach that has been taken in escalator incident literature is that of a 

human factors approach, which has emphasised that an incident is not likely to be the cause 

of one factor in isolation but a series of factors influencing the formation of an incident 

(Reason, 1990; Sklet, 2004). ‘Human factors’ is the scientific discipline that seeks to 

understand how a human interacts with a system to optimise wellbeing and performance 

(International Ergonomics Association, n.d.). The focus is on how a human performs an 

action/task with the aim to identify and categorise those ‘Performance Influencing Factors’ 

(PIFs) (HSE, 1999) (sometimes referred to so Performance Shaping Factors (Pan & Wu, 

2018)), which may influence the outcome (i.e., success or failure), in a bid to reduce user 

error and prevent injury (Noyes, 2002; Stanton et al., 2013; Wilson & Corlett, 1995). 

Some escalator incident authors have adopted a human factors approach. For 

instance, Chi et al. (2006) reviewed 194 escalator incident reports for the Metro Rapid 

System in Taiwan, adopting Drury and Brill’s (1983) human factors classification system 

(actors, props, the scene, and the action) to review and then break-down escalator incident 

reports to distinguish the major cause of an incident (e.g. not holding the handrail, carrying 

out other tasks, unhealthy passengers etc.) and associated incident type (i.e. fall or 

entrapment). Chi et al. (2006) reported that injured persons over the age of 65 were 

significantly associated with; (i) traveling with company; (ii) use of ascending escalators; (iii) 

suffering head injuries; and (iv) requiring an ambulance. This however should not be 

inferred as a specific escalator incident scenario for an older user i.e., incidents for those 44 

over 65 are more frequent when traveling with friends on the ascending escalator leading to 

head injuries and an ambulance called. The authors are examining the relationship between 

two variables independently to other factors and to infer these four factors are related 

without further statistical analysis does not provide an accurate interpretation of the 

escalator hazards that need addressing (Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al. 2022). This is also an 

assumption made by other escalator incident researchers (Xing et al., 2019; Li et al., 2021) 

emulating the work of Chi et al. (2006). The proceeding section explores multifactorial 

supposition of escalator incidents in more detail accentuating the need to select the right 

statistical approach to account for multifactorial relationships between PIFs. 

2.2.1.1 Escalator Incidents are Multifactorial 

Using an escalator involves a person getting onto and then off a moving surface (Hsu 

et al., 2015). During this human-machine interaction, it is likely that several PIFs (HSE, 1999) 

may lead to an escalator incident occurring rather than one single cause (Al-Sharif, 2006; 

Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2022; Xie & Liu, 2019). Such suggestion is congruent with 

wider incident investigation perspectives, which have highlighted that most major accidents 

are almost never the result of one single cause, but the result of multiple, interrelated 

factors (Delkihoon et al. 2020; Katsakioir et al., 2009; Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020; Reason, 

1990; Sklet, 2004). By way of example, Ackermans et al. (2019) identified several cumulating 

factors could lead to a stair fall, rather than one parameter in isolation. Biomechanical 

analyses revealed that participants at low risk of a fall had a higher maximal centre of mass 

(CoM) angular acceleration (indicating a reduced ability to control the body against gravity 

on descent), high levels of cadence (indicating a higher speed when descending stairs), but a 

smaller required coefficient of friction (Ackermans et al., 2019). Similarly, in a review of 45 

stairway falls and stair negotiation, Jacobs (2016) identified that features of the person, 

task, and environment were influential in stairway falls and suggested that interventions 

need to encompass multi-components, harnessing each of these areas to reduce fall risk. 

For example, modifying the environment (e.g., stair structure), behaviours of users (e.g., 

encouraging use of the handrail) and/or improving individual abilities (e.g., increasing 

physical function). 

In accordance with a multifactorial perspective, Al-Sharif (2006) developed the 

Passenger Accident prevention model, which highlighted (i) Escalator design; (ii) Passenger 

behaviours; and (iii) Maintenance, inspection, and operation of the escalator (Figure 2.3). 

> Figure 2.3:
> Passenger Accident Prevention Model, Taken From Al-Sharif (2006).

Al-Sharif (2006) interpreted that passenger accidents resulted from any one of these 

three factors either individually or as a combination. Although the model is a beneficial 

depiction of a multifactorial approach, there is a lack of any transparent method for how the 

model was developed, lack of link between established literature, and there is currently 

limited empirical evidence to substantiate this model. Further, given that almost half of 

escalator falls involve those over the age of 65 (Table 2.1) it is probable that this model may 

be missing a significant PIF. There is only limited reference to an escalator users age with 

the author only mentioning that teenager’s behaviour of jumping from one escalator to 46 

another can lead to an incident. It is important to understand how and why age may be a 

factor in escalator incidents given it is often reported in escalator injury literature (Table 2.1) 

and falls (the most common type of incident on escalator) risk has been shown to increase 

with age (Etman et al., 2012). It is possible that changes in age-related physiological mobility 

(Kenny et al., 2017) reduces a person’s ability to board the moving escalator steps (Hsu et 

al., 2015; Hunt et al., 2023) but without exploration of potential PIFs it is not clear what, if 

any, other factors are influencing this supposed scenario. 

Xie and Liu (2019) adopted an interpretive structural modelling and decision-making 

trial and evaluation laboratory method to explore escalator incidents. This method involved 

utilizing a matrix and graphical representation to establish the importance and relationships 

between incident factors (i.e., PIFs) in a bid to distinguish the cause-and-effect patterns 

leading to an escalator incident. Data mining of 231 Chinese news reports - and cross 

referencing this analysis with available escalator incident literature - the authors identified 

22 factors that could influence an escalator incident. Structuring these factors into cause 

and effect, the authors developed a five-hierarchy structure (level five being the most 

important) for reference in preventing future escalator incidents: (i) correcting passenger 

behaviour; (ii) management of the environment around the escalator; (iii) optimising 

escalator settings, components, and maintenance; (iv) standardising safety rules and 

strengthening supervision; and (v) implement safety education and creating a safety 

climate. Wang et al. (2020) have argued that whilst the work of Xie and Liu (2019) is a step 

in the right direction for a more holistic and deeper understanding of escalator incidents, 

the method adopted is too subjective, relying on the subjective interpretations of 

investigators to decide what is the most important PIF, which may introduce unnecessary 

bias to the risk assessment process (Malterud, 2001). 47 

To address the concerns highlighted in the previous paragraph, Wang et al. (2020) 

adopted an approach similar Xie and Liu’s (2019), as they developed a hierarchical, 

multifactorial model for examining escalator incidents, exploring 327 escalator incidents in 

the Beijing metro transit stations. The authors identified both critical and non-critical risk 

passenger behaviours and other hazards that were related to risky passenger behaviour 

(i.e., PIFs for escalator incidents). Rather than relying on subjective reasoning to understand 

how these PIFs interacted (Xie & Liu, 2019), Wang et al. (2020) used statistical analyses to 

explore the topology of the structure of an escalator incident using the following three 

parameters: (i) degree distribution (used to indicate the strength of a hazard with other 

hazards); (ii) clustering coefficient (the degree to which hazards cluster together); and (iii) 

betweenness centrality (identifies hazards that form bridges to other hazards). Wang et al. 

suggested that when a series of critical and non-critical events unfolded in a specific order, 

they ‘cascade’ to an escalator incident occurring. For example, the authors found that 

“Carrying of bulk items” was a key hazard and bridge (i.e., causally linked) to other hazards: 

“objects fallings down”; “stepping off the escalator” and “quarrels with others” in the data. 

The authors suggested that by preventing the carrying of bulky items on escalators and 

encouraging passengers to use the lift, would in turn be efficient in reducing these other 

escalator hazards from occurring. 

In the respective studies of Wang et al. (2020) and Xie and Liu (2019), both articles 

appeared to assume a direct causal relationship between PIFs (i.e., X will lead to Y). 

However, Wang et al. (2022) have subsequently argued that this view may be limiting the 

understanding of escalator incidents. Wang et al. (2022) highlighted that there are many 

PIFs which can impact an escalator incident, not all of which will have a direct causal impact 

on an incident occurring. To address this potential shortcoming, Wang et al. (2022) 48 

proposed a generic method for the modelling of escalator incidents based on the three-tier 

taxonomy proposed by Al-Sharif (2006) and included the ‘escalator riding environment’ 

highlighted by Wang et al. (2020) as a fourth area of influence. The proposed interaction 

model comprised of four areas: (i) engineering design; (ii) maintenance; (iii) riding 

environment; and (iv) passenger behaviours. This model proposed a multi-interaction-based 

event comprising of several PIFs leading to an incident. Using Lasso-Logistic Regression (LRR) 

to overcome issues with multicollinearity between variables (anticipated in an interaction-

based approach) and the high number of PIFs but low observation count (described as 

typical escalator incident data by the authors), they found evidence to support the use of 

the four-way taxonomy with at least one PIF from each of the four areas able to predict 

injury type. This, they suggested, addressed the shortcomings of previous escalator research 

that has focused on there being a direct causal relationship between PIFs (Wang et al., 2020; 

Xie & Liu, 2019) and research that has examined the association between two PIFs and not 

the influence of multiple PIFs on escalator incident formation (Chi et al. 2006; Xing et al, 

2019; Li et al., 2021). 

Whilst a LRR appears a promising method for a multi-interaction in-depth escalator 

incident investigation there were still several PIFs that were removed as part of the model 

validation process with a bias-variance trade off, which reduces the number of PIFs when 

compensating for multicollinearity (Abdulhafedh, 2022). Wang et al. (2022) recognise the 

lack of effective guidance for LRR parameter selection and model fitting may be impacting 

their final model. This in turn could mean that escalator incident research is not getting the 

deeper understanding of contributing escalator incident factors that Wang et al. (2022) call 

for. 49 

An alternative approach could be to use Multiple Correspondence Analysis (MCA). 

MCA is less well-known method than traditional regression techniques (Lolle 2008; 

Panagiotakos & Pitsavos, 2004) and is designed to handle categorical data (Das et al. 2018) 

that is prominently reported in escalator incident research (Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 2019; 

Wang et al., 2022). Unlike traditional regression techniques MCA does not make any 

assumptions surrounding the data such as multicollinearity of variables (Das & Sun; 2015; 

Das et al., 2018). It is different from the parametric estimation of LRR as the objective is to 

investigate associations between several factors not just the associations between predictor 

variables and single response of intertest (i.e., Injury type, in the case of Wang et al. 2022) 

(Das et al., 2018). MCA is an exploratory geometric data analysis technique that allows the 

mapping and interpretation of multiple variables (in this case PIFs). Each variable is 

represented as a data point which can then be interpreted using graphical representations 

methods such as hierarchical cluster analysis (Chauvin et al. 2013; Costantino et al., 2016). 

By way of example, Facchinetti et al. (2013) use MCA to group characteristics of falls 

patients with Human T-cell lymphotropic virus type (HTLV) I-associated myelopathy/tropical 

spastic paraparesis (HAM/TSP), a mobility disabling disease. Using MCA on questionnaire 

responses from 36 HAM/TSP patients exploring potential PIFs to falls, Facchinetti et al., 

(2013) identify fallers were characterised as males; less than 60 years old; exercising three 

or more times a week; having had fallen two or more times in the last year; community level 

ambulation (walking ability) and a HTLV-I proviral load of <6.6 copies per 100 cells. This 

combination of variables provide insight to the prevalence of falls in HAM/TSP patients for 

which falls are frequently reported by these patients, the reasons why was unknown 

(Facchinetti et al., 2013). Therefore, MCA provides a more exploratory yet detailed 50 

examination of PIFs, their influence and congruence with escalator incidents and will be 

adopted in the current thesis to explore escalator incident PIFs in Chapters 3-5. 

A further limitation of current multicomponent and multi-interactional escalator 

incident research is that it has used data from Metro Stations (Xie & Liu; 2019; Wang et al., 

2020; Wang et al., 2022). It is possible that the analysis thus far is metro-specific and thus 

other PIFs may be unaccounted for that could influence incidents in shopping centres (Page 

& Hough, 1989) or airports (Howland et al. 2012). As such this thesis (Chapter 3-5) will use 

data from several sources comprising of different sectors to ensure no key PIFs are missed. 

In a similar vein, most epidemiological style escalator incident research uses data from 

hospital admissions (Lee et al., 2010; O’Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et al., 2013) and thus may 

be occluding PIFs for less severe incidents which occur but are recorded by organisations 

managing the escalator and thus may provide wider insight into the causes of escalator 

incidents (DeMers et al. 2011). Therefore, this thesis will focus beyond metro station 

escalator incidents and explore both severe and less severe incidents to increase 

understanding of incidents (Chapters 3 & 4). 

Furthermore, there is an absence of a uniformed method for recording escalator 

incidents in extant escalator health and safety research, which may occlude the 

identification of important PIFs. For instance, Wang et al. (2020) highlighted that of the 799 

incidents initially identified in their analysis, 59.1% were unusable as the incident 

descriptions were not present or only reported one hazard. This is a recognised issue in the 

wider health and safety literature when analysing incident report data (Davies et al., 2007; 

Woodcock, 2008), even when the reporting systems are from the same organisation 

(Rossignol, 2015), and is a commonly raised concern by escalator incident researchers 

(Ruibal et al., 2015). Wang et al. (2022) highlighted that no such method for investigating 51 

escalator incidents exists for subway stations, citing that their method could be used more 

widely in escalator incident research, yet as this is a recent development there is a lack of 

empirical evidence to substantiate this suggestion. Further, Wang et al’s (2022) four-way 

interaction taxonomy is primarily based on the work of Al-Sharif (2006) (three of the four 

areas) which lacks empirical evidence to substantiate its design and as such may be missing 

potential key PIFs. The importance of having an accurate method to investigate escalator 

incidents has a wider impact for preventative measures. For example, there have been 

recent attempts to develop escalator safety monitoring systems that use a combination of 

artificial intelligence and real time escalator video footage to intervene when key PIFs are 

identified, stopping the escalator and/or warning the escalator operator (Jiao et la. 2022; 

Osipov et al. 2022). However, such systems are only as useful as the initial identification 

parameter set. For example, Jiao et al. (2022) focussed their system to identify three injury 

triggers: (i) loss of balance; (ii) not holding the handrail; and (iii) carrying of large items. 

Whilst all have been highlighted in previous escalator research as PIFs (Chi et al., 2006; 

Wang et al., 2020, Xing et al., 2019), it is not clear from current research if the PIFs Jiao et al. 

(2022) selected were the most influential, as aforementioned escalator incident research 

has only recently started to take a multi-interaction, multi-component approach. 

A possible solution to address current limitations in extant escalator incident 

research and adopted by this thesis is the use of a human factors investigative approach. 

Though the discipline of human factors is not specifically mentioned in the multi-interaction, 

multi-component analyses of escalator incidents (Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2022; Xie & 

Liu; 2019;) the work is in accordance with that of the discipline’s ethos, to understand the 

impact of PIFs on a person undertaking a task/action in a bid to reduce user error and 

prevent injury (International Ergonomics Association, n.d.; Noyes, 2002; Stanton et al., 2013; 52 

Wilson & Corlett, 1995). There are many different human factors investigative approaches 

that could be taken (Stanton et al., 2013) and the next section outlines the human factors 

accident perspectives, ontological considerations, and methods for incident investigation. 

2.3 A Human Factors Approach to Incident Investigations 

Human failure has often been attributed as the cause of major incidents such as Three 

Mile Island, Chernobyl, or Piper Alpha (Meshkati et al., 1991; Swuste et al., 2018). Yet, human 

failure also occurs in less publicised incidents such as traffic accidents (Zhang et al., 2019), 

medical errors (Mitchell et al., 2015), construction fatalities (Hale et al., 2012) or falling 

incidents (Davis, 1983). Human factors researchers have sought to understand those PIFs that 

both positively and/or negatively influence human performance that led to an incident (HSE, 

1999; Pan & Wu, 2020). In so doing, there is recognition that incidents are rarely due to one 

PIF in isolation but rather a combination of PIFs (Delikhoon et al. 2022; Katsakiori et al., 2009; 

Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020; Reason, 1990; Sklet, 2004). The proceeding sections consider the 

current theoretical and ontological perspectives and incident investigation methods in 

Human Factors. Taken together, these will shape the proposed investigative method for 

investigating escalator incidents, outlined in the last section. 

2.3.1 Theoretical Accident Perspectives 

In current human factors discourse there are three theoretical perspectives to 

accident causation models (Read et al., 2021; Uğurlu et al., 2018). The first and perhaps 

simplest perspective is that accidents are the result of consecutive actions/inactions by a 

person which leads to an error-based cause-effect relationship and thus an incident occurring 

(Dekker, 2002). For example, as mentioned previously Wang et al. (2020) suggest there to be 53 

direct casual relationships with a person carrying a bulky item on the escalator and said item 

then falling down the escalator, cascading onto other passengers. 

A second approach is an interactionist perspective (Read et al., 2021). Like the 

consecutive perspective, error is seen as the main cause of an accident, however, this 

approach recognises that there are contributory PIFs that can influence an incident occurring. 

This perspective is best represented in the work of James Reason (1990) and Reason et al. 

(2006) who put forth the Swiss Cheese Model (Figure 2.4). This model seeks to conceptualise 

that human errors are not the product of one major cause, rather a combination of: (i) hazards 

(i.e. things that have the potential to cause harm); (ii) active failures (e.g., someone not 

completing a task as intended); (iii) latent conditions (e.g., a design oversite that was missed 

until an incident occurred); which consecutively led to a (iv) loss (i.e. incident). 

> Figure 2.4:
> Swiss Cheese Model, Adapted From Reason et al. (2006)

Reason’s model postulates that an incident occurs as the results of a goldilocks 

moment where those barriers (pieces of cheese) designed to prevent injury fail (i.e., have 

holes) in their ability to control risk. If we were to take escalators as an example, the hazard 

would be the escalator, an active failure may be someone not successfully embarking the 

moving surface (Hsu et al., 2015), a latent failure could be that the moving surface was not 

optimised for decline in the action capability of older users (Hunt et al., 2023) and ultimately 

Hazards 

Losses 

Latent 

Failures 

Active 

Failures 54 

this leads to an incident. Although Reason’s model is commonly utilised across industry and 

safety practitioner toolkits (Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020; Underwood & Waterson, 2013), it has 

been critiqued for being too linear, with focus on cause-effect, not identifying potential 

contributory relationships between PIFs or wider influences such as 

Regulatory/Governmental legislative impact that may influence more complex incidents 

(Hollnagel, 2004; Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020; Leveson, 2004). 

This leads onto the final perspective, a systems approach to accident formation (Read 

et al., 2021; Salmon et al., 2017). A systems perspective focuses on the interaction between 

the human and the technological components (the system) (Read et al. 2021). This 

perspective assumes, incidents are not the result of a linear cause-effect relationship with 

focus solely on the operator, but the result of non-linear, non-deterministic interaction(s) 

between/within components (PIFs) of the system (Read et al., 2021; Salmon et al., 2017). For 

example, Leveson (2004) proposed a systems incident model that considers the potential 

impact US Congress has on incidents. Leveson’s (2004) model highlighted that US Congress 

passes laws and establishes regulatory bodies, these decisions in turn can influence safety 

and thus potentially have an impact an incident occurring. In the context of escalator 

incidents, current escalator injury literature proposes escalators to be multifactorial (Al-

Sharif, 2006; Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2022; Xie & Liu, 2019). Wang et al. (2022) 

specifically highlights the need for a systems perspective when exploring escalator incidents, 

recognising incidents are not the result of linear cause-effect relationships (e.g., not holding 

the handrail leads to a fall) rather a multi-factorial and multi-interactional based event 

comprising of several PIFs. Therefore, in line with current escalator injury literature thinking 

this thesis will consider escalators incidents to be multi-factorial and multi-interactional. But 55 

just focusing on incidents alone could be seen as restrictive (Hollnagel, 2008), the next section 

discusses the need to look at when things go wrong but also when things go right. 

2.3.2 Ontological Perspectives: Safety-I, Safety-II & Safety-III 

The systems perspective considered in the previous section does not just focus on 

when something goes wrong as is suggested by the consecutive and interactionalist 

perspectives (Read et al., 2021), but there is an additional focus on examining ‘normal’ 

operating performance (Hollnagel, 2008). Ontologically, consecutive and interactionalist 

approaches have been argued to reflect a “Safety-I” view (Read et al., 2021), which is 

concerned with learning from incidents when things go wrong. Procedurally, the prerequisite 

is for an incident to have occurred before the compartmentalisation of the incident into 

different facets (i.e., PIFs), and then an examination of how the PIFs may contribute to the 

incident before the final aim of correcting the PIFs (Hollnagle, 2008; Hollnagle et al., 2015; 

Read et al., 2021). By way of illustration, James Reason’s model (Figure 2.4) can be seen as 

being a Safety-I perspective (Read et al., 2021), breaking down an incident into active and 

latent failures to identify and mitigate the causes. 

“Safety-II” has been proposed as complementary to Safety-I, with a focus on when 

things go right (Hollnagel, 2008). The ontological stance of Safety-II is the belief that by 

understanding how safety practices are coordinated successfully and by acknowledging that 

a person’s performance will be variable, it is possible to infer, or construct causes proactively 

rather than finding them retrospectively following an incident (Hollnagle, 2008; Hollnagle et 

al., 2015). For example, Tselentis et al. (2019) examined data of driving behaviours whilst 

using smartphone technology in a bid to understand those behaviours that might 

differentiate safe and less safe driving. Data from a sample of 56 drivers were collected from 56 

the OSEVEN insurance telematics and driving behaviour platform database over a 7-month 

period. Speeding, mobile phone use whilst driving, number and severity of harsh events (i.e., 

breaking, acceleration, cornering) were analysed in a bid to produce a baseline of safe driver 

behaviour. The authors identified that drivers who were less safe violated speed limits 

between 20% and 32% of the time compared to safer drivers, who would only do so 6.5% of 

the time. The authors suggested that their findings could be used to proactively warn drivers 

when their behaviours become less safe. Furthermore, the study of Tselentis et al., (2019) is 

an illustrative example of Safety-II, as no actual road traffic accidents occurred, but the study 

data could be used to proactively identify driver behaviours – such as the violation of speed 

limits – that could put themselves and other road users at risk of an accident. 

Rather than being competing approaches, it has been proposed that Safety-I and 

Safety-II can be viewed as complementary to one another to help reduce risk (Hollnagle et al., 

2015). For example, in a video surveillance study, Cocca et al. (2016) aimed to use objective 

video recordings of workers identify both safe and unsafe (referred to as at-risk behaviours 

by the authors) behaviours at a heat treatment company. Specifically, in this study, Cocca and 

colleagues defined unsafe behaviours as actions that did not conform to the organisation’s 

operational procedures/methods and could cause harm or injury. Conversely safe behaviours 

were defined as the opposite, namely conforming to operational procedures/methods. Using 

video recording of workers day to day activities, the authors were able to provide a baseline 

of safe and unsafe behaviours in the workplace. For example, each month there were 

fluctuations in unsafe behaviours, which reached a peak of 50.3% in July (4 months into the 

7-month experiment), which the authors suggested could be due to the summer shutdown 

of the factory during August and employees paying less attention to safety. Subsequently, 

there was an increase in safety behaviours from 58.8% to 68.2% between September and 57 

October, which the authors suggested could be due measures put in place upon the plant 

restart (training sessions and procedure revisions). However, the authors acknowledged that 

a greater longitudinal design would be required to assess the sustainability of the observed 

impact of the intervention. Nevertheless, the authors highlighted the benefits of using video 

surveillance, which can lead to a more proactive means of safety management rather than 

waiting for an incident to happen. The work by Cocca et al., (2016) helps to demonstrate 

where Safety-I and Safety-II perspectives can be used together to inform safety management, 

looking at both the unsafe and safe actions of employees at the heat treatment plant to get 

a more detailed picture of what is going on. 

An additional approach used in incident investigation literature is to interview key 

stakeholders to explore experiences and perceptions of incidents in the workplace, which can 

provide insight to unsafe behaviours and what can be done to control/prevent such 

behaviours. For example, Debnath et al. (2015) interviewed 66 road construction and 

maintenance workers in Australia to supplement the limited available data on work zone 

crashes. The results of these interviews indicated perceived hazards of roadwork arose from 

diver factors (e.g., speeding, distraction, frustration), environmental factors (e.g., poor 

weather conditions, limited work space, obscured vision) and work and equipment factors 

(e.g., lack of appropriate communications, limited clearance from machinery). The authors 

highlighted that those wet weather-related hazards such as poor visibility, slippery surfaces, 

limited skid resistance and increased stopping distances were collectively mentioned by more 

participants than any other hazard. These findings helped to demonstrate the additional level 

of safety insight offered by the qualitative approach given that the frequency of the 

highlighted results did not always appear as prominently in other published literature, where 

speeding tends to be most prominent (Allpress & Leland Jr, 2010). Further, participants 58 

highlighted that measures that were aimed at increasing compliance would be better than 

simply introducing additional safety measures. For example, participants highlighted that 

police presence was very effective at getting drivers to maintain a speed limit but once 

removed speeding would resume. This method helps to go beyond Safety-I of just looking at 

incident reports alone (Hanson et al., 2011) and encapsulates a Safety-II premises as it 

provides greater detail as to potentially unresearched hazards (environmental hazards) as 

well as what safety measures are perceived to be the most effective in the workplace (police 

enforcement). 

A recent development is the concept of Safety-III by Leveson (2020), which was 

introduced to address the criticism of Safety-I by Hollnagle (2008). Leveson (2020) suggests 

that Safety-III, comprises both reactive (Safety-I) and proactive (Safety-II) ideologies to 

managing safety. This stance is that accidents are the result of inadequate control of hazards; 

there is not a linear cause-effect relationship but rather the entire safety system must be 

designed to prevent hazards, with investigations centred about why the system structure did 

not prevent a loss. From this view, concentration should not just be oriented to preventing 

incidents (Safety-I), but focus should also be on learning from incidents and audit how the 

system is performing (Safety-II). It is possible that in their criticism, Leveson’s (2020) account 

of Safety-III echoes Hollnagel’s (2008) suggestion that Safety-I and Safety-II are 

complementary, and therefore, Safety-III primarily highlights the dynamic relationship 

between the two alternative safety views and so could reflect an intermediary between the 

two. In a discussion article of all three ontological frameworks, Aven (2022) suggested that 

irrespective of stance, the key focus of human factors approaches should be on risk, and 

safety can be seen as an absence of unacceptable risks. Therefore, Aven suggested to 59 

effectively manage risk, consideration to both Safety-II and Safety-III needs to be given as they 

build on the more linear Safety-I approach. 

In the context of escalator incidents, the review of ontological stances considered 

above implies that the examination of incidents involving members of the public should focus 

on the examination of when things go right (i.e., when someone uses the escalator without 

incident) and when things go wrong (i.e., an incident such as a fall). In so doing, this would fall 

in to a pragmatic approach advocated in safety literature, that scientific inquiry and education 

should consider multiple approaches (i.e., Safety-III) to increase knowledge about how to 

inform and improve safety (Dolan et al., 2022; Klockner et al., 2021). 

Therefore, this thesis will explore previous incident reports of escalator incidents to 

help identify baseline factors occurring when things go wrong (i.e., Safety-I, for example Chi 

et al., 2006) but to supplement this with more proactive (i.e., Safety-II) means such as an 

observational video approach, as used by Cocca et al. (2016), and a qualitative approach 

(Debnath et al., 2015) to explore both safe and unsafe escalator behaviours. In so doing the 

thesis will adopt a pragmatic mixed methods approach. Pragmatism, attributed to William 

James (1904) places itself between the empiricist view that knowledge is acquired through 

observation and sensory experience and that of the rationalist that knowledge is attained 

through reasoning and reflection (Dolan et al., 2022). In terms of this thesis this would mean 

examining both quantitative data sources (i.e., escalator incident reports; video coding) and 

qualitative data sources (i.e., interviews with escalator users) combined to enhance 

understanding (Creswell & Clark, 2017) of escalator incidents. The next section discusses the 

commonly used human factors investigative methods available to safety practitioners, which 

can help to inform such research approaches and further develops the approach this thesis 

will take. 60 

2.3.3 Human Factors Incident Investigation Methodologies 

The focus of this review is on systems thinking incident investigation methods. As 

discussed, this perspective is the most current thinking in human factors discourse and best 

reflects a combination of Safety-I, Safety-II and thus Safety-III ontological perspectives 

(Leveson, 2020; Read et al. 2021; Salmon et al. 2017). 

In a systematic review of systems incident investigation methods, comprising a total 

of 73 peer reviewed studies, Hulme et al. (2019) identified four systems incident analysis 

methods that currently dominate the literature: AcciMaps (Rasmussen, 1997); (ii) the Human 

Factors Analysis and classifications System (HFACS) (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2001); (iii) 

Systems Theoretical Accident Model and Processes (STAMP) and associated Casual Analysis 

(Leveson, 2004; 2012); and (iv) Functional Resonance Analysis Method (FRAM) (Hollnagel, 

2004). In the next paragraphs, each model will be briefly illustrated, alongside an example of 

their application in the investigative literature. Indeed, as will be further emphasised, the 

application of these four methods to the study of escalator incidents is complicated by the 

fact these approaches are exemplary of safety management perspectives that do not 

adequately account for the actions of members of the public, focussing rather on employer-

employee relations (Ball & Ball-King, 2013). 

AcciMaps, HFACS and STAMP (Leveson, 2004; Rasmussen, 1997; Wiegmann & 

Shappell, 2001) provide a classification system for an investigator to use to retrospectively 

(i.e., Safety-I) organise and collate their incident data to understand how an incident could 

have occurred (Figures 2.1, 2.5 and 2.6). For example, Newman and Good (2015) analysed 

27 US road freight transport crashes using thematic analysis combined with the AcciMaps 

method. This study reported that driver fatigue was a contributory factor in 14 of the 

reviewed incidents and was further influenced by the lack of higher order fatigue risk 61 

management systems in place from organisations, further compounded by the lack of 

regulation to prevent driving during high-risk fatigue hours of between 00:00 and 05:59 

when 8 of the 14 incidents occurred. In a further example, Chauvin et al (2013) used a 

maritime derivative of HFACS (HFACS-Coll), adopting multiple-correspondence analysis to 

detect patterns of contributory factors across 27 maritime vessel collisions that may not 

have a direct causal impact but cumulatively influence an incident occurring. Chauvin et al.’s 

(2013) results highlighted that vessel collisions happened as an accumulation of HFACS-Coll 

layers. For example, one pattern of vessel collisions resulted from the precondition deficits 

(poor visibility; inattention; instruments misused or not used) combined with the 

organisational influence (incomplete safety management system) and leadership influence 

(a new level added by Chauvin et al. (2013)) of inappropriately planned ship operations (e.g., 

failure to plan crew scheduling, pairing, rest). 

> Figure 2.5 :
> HFACS Framework, Adapted From Wiegmann and Shappell (2001)

Comparable to HFACS and AcciMaps, STAMP (Leveson, 2004; 2012) (Figure 2.6) 

provides a classification structure to examine incidents, however, this method can be applied 

reactively using Casual Analysis using Systems Theory (CAST) (Safety-I) or proactively using 62 

Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA) (Safety-II) (Leveson; 2019; Leveson & Thomas; 

2018). These analysis methods provide a framework for investigators to use depending on if 

they are investigating reactively to an incident or looking at a proactive means of risk 

identification (Table 2.3). 

> Figure 2.6:
> STAMP Model of Control Taken From Leveson (2004)

63 

> Table 2.3
> STAMP Analysis Frameworks CAST (Safety-I) and STPA (Safety-II)

Düzgün and Leveson, (2018) used STAMP and CAST (Table 2.3) to analyse the Soma 

Mine Disaster, which was an underground coal mine fire that claimed the lives of 301 persons 

in 2014. Following the five steps of CAST (Table 2.3) to assess the incident the researchers 

identified Socio-technical factors in terms of poor safety culture and improper decision 

making. Inadequate control systems were identified across the systems hierarchical structure. 

For example, the legislation designed to protect workers was not accompanied with any 

standards, protocol, or guidelines, meaning the operators did not know what ‘good’ was 

meant to look like. Ultimately the authors were able to highlight the need to improve the 

culture at the mine to move away from a reliance on productivity and balance this with safety. 

Lee et al. (2020) used STAMP and STPA (Table 2.3) to identify unsafe control actions in nuclear 

facility operations. Using the four steps to orientate their investigation, they Examined 51 

operational control actions identifying 127 unsafe control actions that could lead to loss

(CAST) 

Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis 

(STPA)

the incident, the hazards, events,

scenarios. These were subsequently reviewed by operators to alter procedures and introduce 

mitigation strategies. 

In terms of the applicability of these three classification methods to escalator incident 

research, one of the main challenges is that a member of the public does not neatly fit into 

the traditional approaches for safety management (Ball & Ball-King, 2013; Ball-King, 2021). 

AcciMaps, HFACS, and STAMP methods use classification systems which adopt an 

organisational-oriented perspective. For example, AcciMaps has a section regarding ‘Staff’, 

whilst STAMP has an element to capture ‘Operations Management – work instructions’ and 

HFACS has a section for ‘Unsafe supervision’ none of which fit naturally with the safety 

management of a member of the public for whom such elements will have no meaning as 

they are not an employee (Ball & Ball-King; 2013; Ball-King, 2021). Further, existing human 

factors classifications systems have been criticised for being over complex, requiring specialist 

knowledge to understand their application. For example, O’Connor and Walker (2011) used 

the Department of Defence Human Factors Analysis and Classification System (a derivative of 

HFACS used by the US department of defence) with 204 military aviation safety officer 

students to analyse two aviation mishaps and found that the Kappa level of agreement was 

only acceptable for 56.9% of the categories across the two incidents. The authors highlighted 

that the classification system required refinement, with overlapping categories leading to the 

low levels of coder agreement. Therefore, trying to retrofit AcciMaps, HFACS, and STAMP 

classification systems to escalator incidents would not appear to be a suitable means of 

incident analysis and thus the production of an alternative classification system would be 

warranted. 

FRAM is the final method identified by Hulme et al.’s (2019) review, which can be used 

for incident investigation in both a retrospective (Safety-I) but also proactive (Safety-II) 65 

manner to identify where potential issues may exist (Hollnagel, 2012; Riccardo et al. 2018). In 

contrast to adopting a classification structure with several levels as with AcciMaps, HFACS and 

STAMP, FRAM identifies and evaluates the relationships between functions (i.e., tasks, 

activities, or components) of a system examining the resonance (disparity) between 

behaviour that is expected (i.e., normal behaviour) and that which is not (i.e., abnormal 

behaviour). Figure 2.7 illustrates an example FRAM Schematic (Hollnagel, 2004; 2012; 

Hollnagel & Goteman, 2004). 

> Figure 2.7
> Example FRAM Schematic Adapted From Hollnagel (2004, 2012)

Each hexagon represents a system function (e.g., stepping on to the escalator). In 

addition, six FRAM aspects of this function are depicted and considered: (i) I – refers to the 

input a function uses or transforms (e.g., grasping for the handrail may impact successfully 

stepping onto the escalator); (ii) O – refers to the output of a function produces (e.g., 

successfully embarking the escalator); (iii) P – refers to the preconditions that are required to 

perform its function (e.g., person decides to choose the escalator over alternatives such as 

stairs or lifts); (iv) R – refers to the resources that a function needs or uses for its output (e.g., 

person has physical capability to board the moving steps); (v) T – refers to the influence time 66 

has on a function, be this a limitation or a resource (e.g. person is running late for a 

train/plane); (vi) C – refers to control in terms of what regulates a function from producing an 

output such as regulations (e.g., common to stand to the right when embarking an escalator 

on the London underground). Alm and Woltjer (2010) implemented the FRAM method to a 

case where surgical materials were left in a patient’s abdomen during a surgical procedure in 

a hospital in Sweden. The FRAM method was compared with an investigation that had already 

been conducted by the patient safety unit that had utilised a more traditional Safety-I of 

looking for PIFs that caused the error. The FRAM method was able to identify variability in the 

suturing process that contributed to functional resonance and ultimately led to the system 

failing and materials left in the patient. However, the authors stated it was not possible to 

identify exactly how the functional resonance emerged due to the complexity and how 

interwoven ‘functions’ are in the operating room. In comparison with the patient safety unit’s 

initial investigation, the FRAM method was able to provide higher level recommendations 

such as improvement of culture to improve safety compared to the previous analysis, which 

resulted in a focus on procedural, front line level improvements. 

FRAM has the potential to offer a more feasible method for exploring escalator 

incidents as this approach does not rely on the existence of any archetypal organisational 

structure. However, this method is dependent on extant understanding of what ‘normal’ and 

‘abnormal’ behaviours look like. Yet a critique of FRAM is that no clear ‘generic’ definition for 

‘normal’ vs ‘abnormal’ is provided to guide researchers (Salehi et al., 2020). Further, as 

escalator incident literature is yet to conclusively address what is ‘normal’ and ‘abnormal’ in 

terms of escalator behaviours FRAM would not appear to be the most suitable approach at 

this stage of escalator injury research. 67 

In current escalator incident literature only, Wang et al. (2022) have undertaken a 

systems perspective to retrospectively review (i.e., Safety-I) metro station escalator incidents, 

providing a classification system primarily based on a previous model produced by Al-Sharif 

(2006) (Figure 2.3, Section 2.2.1 ). 

> Figure 2.8:
> Escalator Accident Mechanism Model Taken From Wang et al. (2022).
> Note : Task Driven Accident Process Analysis (TDAPA); Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA)

Utilising a method comparable to STPA (Leveson, 2019; Leveson & Thomas, 2018) and 

Task Driven Accident Process Analysis (TDAPA) (Drury & Brill, 1983), Wang et al. (2022) 

highlighted some of the PIFs that may have that may have indirectly influenced an incident. 

Though less well used, TDAPA is like STPA as it requires the investigator to answer a series of 

questions about the incident to identify PIFs that could have caused the incident to occur. 

Wang et al. (2022) modified TDAPA to correspond with STPA and thus provide systems 

perspective analysis of escalator incidents. Figure 2.8 demonstrates the adopted approach 

where each question of TDPA is answered using the generic model (Figure 2.9) to identify PIFs 

that may have led to an escalator incident, and this is then mapped across to STPA. 68 

> Figure 2.9:
> Generic Model for Formation of Escalator Incidents Taken From Wang et al. (2022).
> Note : Metro Rail Transport Stations (MRTS)

Whilst Wang et al. (2022) adopted a systems approach to explore escalator incidents, 

examining the direct and indirect relationships of PIFs, the approach is arguably limited due 

to the utilisation of Al-Sharif’s (2006) classification system, which as discussed ( 2.2.1 Escalator 

incidents are multifactorial ), fails to adequately account for potential PIFs, such as a person’s 

age. Therefore, to address this limitation, the current thesis will set out to develop a new 

taxonomy with its own classification system that is oriented to use for members of the public 

who do not fit with the traditional archetypal organisational structure. Following the 

recommendations of Sklet (2004) and Katsakiori et al. (2009), the choice of the investigative 

method should be based on the needs the incident and needs of the investigation. As current 

escalator injury literature escalator is not a single case study of an incident such as the Soma 

Mine Disaster (Düzgün & Leveson, 2018) but rather several incidents recorded at 

organisational level reporting systems (Chi et al., 2006) or national level databases (O’Neil et 69 

al., 2008) it is essential that a method of categorising and exploring PIFs with such data would 

be a suitable and sensible approach (Salmon et al., 2012). As limited literature is available for 

escalator incidents, the taxonomy will need to be flexible to incorporate new variables that 

may need to be added as more data is generated. Therefore, consistent with established 

literature, a similar developmental approach will be applied to review escalator incident 

reports using existing academic literature (Al-Sharif, 2006; Chi et al., 2006). Additionally, the 

systematic approach of a taxonomic method would provide the ability to conduct measures 

of inter-rater reliability providing confidence in the approach used (McBurney & White, 2007; 

Ritchie & Lewis, 2006). 

Thus, though a new taxonomy is proposed to retrospectively review escalator 

incidents, this would predominantly satisfy the criterion of a Safety-I perspective, that is, 

when things have gone wrong. As discussed ( 2.3.2 Ontological perspectives: Safety-I, Safety-

II & Safety-III ), there is a need to use data sources beyond incident reports so that focus is 

not just on when things go wrong (Safety-I) but also when things go right (Safety-II & Safety-

III) (Hollnagel et al., 2008). It would therefore be prudent to supplement incident reports 

(Chapter 3 and 4) with video surveillance (Chapter 5; Cocca et al., 2016) as well as interviews 

(Chapter 6; Dabneth et al., 2015) with escalator users as means to identify those behaviours 

that more predominately lead to an incident and those which may be more typical of 

‘normal’ behaviours i.e. does holding the handrail have such a big impact to an incident 

occurring (Chi et al., 2006) or is this a red herring within the incident data. 

This thesis will adopt a sequential mixed methods approach (Hong et al. 2017; 

Sandelowski et al. 2006). A sequential mixed methods approach is beneficial as it will allow 

for a pragmatic deductive approach by which each chapter of this thesis builds on the 

development and refinement of the proposed taxonomy to assess its applicability to escalator 70 

incidents using both quantitative (i.e., review of incident data - Safety-I) qualitative data (i.e., 

interviews - Safety-II) (Creswell & Clark, 2017; Hong et al., 2017). 

Specifically, the traditional development of incident taxonomies stems from a Safety-

I, quantitative review of incident reports, looking for common PIFs that subsequently make 

up the basis for the taxonomy (Hale et al., 2012; Itoh et al., 2009; Mitchell et al., 2015; 2016; 

Wiegmann & Shappell 2001). For example, Shappell and Wiegmann (1997) and Wiegmann 

and Shappell (2001) developed and refined the Human Factors Accident Classifications 

System, one of the most popular human factors methodologies (Hulme et al., 2019) by initially 

reviewing 300 US Naval aviation accidents to produce a four-level structure containing 19 

contributory PIFs that may influence an incident occurring. This will be the basis of Chapter 3 

which entails the development of the proposed taxonomy. Yet, considering limitations of 

current escalator incident research in terms of the focus on severe incident data (see, Section 

2.2.1 ) there is a need to assess the applicability of taxonomy to data sources not reliant on 

severe incidents as such Chapter 4 will seek to build on the work of initial development of the 

taxonomy in Chapter 3. 

This thesis will adopt a Safety-III perspective by which it goes beyond traditional 

taxonomy development Safety-I approaches, encompassing a Safety-II ontological 

perspective by applying the taxonomy to observational footage of escalators users in Chapter 

5 to both overcome the limitations of narrative incident reports (see, Section 2.2.1 ) and to 

provide an understanding of how persons use an escalator without incident (i.e. when things 

go right). Further, a qualitative approach will then be adopted in Chapter 6 to explore the 

views of those escalator users most a risk of an injury by means to explore the findings of the 

previous Chapters 3-5 and assess the impact this data may have on the taxonomy. 71 

2.4 Summary 

Public spaces are important to human society. Legislation exists to ensure the safety 

of persons using public spaces which in turn prevents injury to the person and thus reduces 

the potential economic and individual burden such injuries have on societies. Yet the 

common place public space feature of an escalator sees annual injuries and fatalities, 

despite their being several safety standards aimed at preventing such occurrences. Further, 

there is a lack of research as to the causes of escalator incidents which is of such concern 

the regulator for health and safety legislation in Great Britain, HSE, commissioned a shared 

research programme to identify and understand the PIFs that may be leading to escalator 

incidents in more detail. The majority of current escalator injury research has focused on 

the outcome of the incident not exploring the relationships within and between identified 

PIFs in detail. However, a second approach, that of a human factors approach has been 

undertaken by a handful of escalator incidents researchers. These researchers recognise 

escalator incidents to be multifactorial with both direct and indirect relationships between 

PIFS that may lead to an escalator incident. No uniform means is available to identify and 

thus explore escalator PIFs. Therefore, this thesis expands on current human factors 

approaches to escalator incidents. In so doing it acknowledges human factors theoretical 

and ontological perspectives as well as the issues that existing investigative human factors 

approaches have been designed with an archetypal organisational structure in mind which 

does not translate to a member of the public. The proceeding chapters will outline the 

development and subsequent applicability of a new taxonomy designed specifically for 

investigating incident involving members of the public. 72 

FOREWORD TO CHAPTER 3 

The literature review in Chapter 2 highlighted that escalator research is limited, with 

a focus primarily on the identification and discussion of Performance Influencing Factors 

(PIFs) with no explanation as to why said factors may be pertinent to an incident occurring. 

A new, yet under-researched strand has been to adopt a human factors approach by which 

escalator incidents are seen as multifactorial. Yet currently no uniform means to categorise 

and thus explore escalator incidents from a human factors perspective exists. Whilst there 

are several human factors taxonomies available to researchers, these are designed with the 

traditional organisational structure in mind where an employee-employer relationship 

exists, which does not translate to how a member of the public interacts with an 

environment. The literature described in Chapter 2 highlights how taxonomies are 

traditionally developed from a Safety-I (i.e., learning from when things go wrong) approach 

of reviewing incident data. Subsequently, Chapter 3 introduces the development of the 

Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP). The taxonomy is 

designed to address a gap where existing taxonomies fall short without the need for an 

employee-employer relationship to be present. The HFIT-MP is then applied to severe 

escalator incident data provided by the Health and Safety Executive to identify and explore 

relationships between PIFs that could lead to a member the public getting injured when 

using an escalator. 73 

CHAPTER: 3 A NEW HUMAN FACTORS INCIDENT TAXONOMY FOR MEMBERS OF THE 

PUBLIC (HFIT-MP): AN INVESTIGATION OF ESCALATOR INCIDENTS 

3.1 Abstract 

Background: 

Escalators are used in shopping centres, airports, and train stations, yet their use is 

not without risk as injuries, even fatalities, can occur during use. This research takes a hu-

man factors approach to: 1) produce a new Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members 

of the Public (HFIT-MP); and 2) apply the taxonomy to a dataset of serious escalator inci-

dents to explore relationships between the factors and gain insights into escalator incidents. 

Methods: 

The HFIT-MP was developed using a combination of human factors literature, Health 

and Safety Executive (HSE) guidance, and subject matter experts. Narrative escalator inci-

dent reports spanning 31st January 2015 to 31st January 2016 were obtained from HSE and 

coded according to the HFIT-MP. Inter-rater agreement was measured using Cohen’s Kappa 

for a sample of incidents. The full incident dataset was investigated using frequency distri-

butions and Multiple Correspondence Analysis (MCA) coupled with cluster analysis to iden-

tify factors that tended to occur together. 

Results: 

The final HFIT-MP consisted of four overarching themes and 25 factors. Double cod-

ing of 197 of the 403 escalator incidents using the HFIT-MP demonstrated substantial to al-

most perfect levels of agreement (all factors achieved Kappa > 0.60). Analysis of all 403 es-

calator injury reports indicated that 93% were falls. MCA identified six groups of factors that 

tended to occur together; for example, falls occurred more frequently with those 65 or over, 

those who lost footing, getting onto the escalator, not making any unexpected movements, 

the escalator descending, and travelling alone. 74 

Conclusion: 

Results provide health and safety practitioners with possible target groups (e.g., 

age), actions (e.g., getting onto the escalator) and locations (e.g., descending escalator) to 

inform interventions. Results also support the need to consider the interaction of multiple 

factors among incidents involving members of the public. Further application of the HFIT-

MP to different sources and modes of data, such as video footage, could further refine the 

HFIT-MP. 

3.2 Introduction 

Escalators are commonly found in most shopping centres, train stations, and airports 

and used by people to move through these environments (Howland et al., 2012; Lee et al., 

2010). However, their usage is not without some risk; a review of US Consumer Product 

Safety Commission (CPSC) data by McCann (2013) identified 39 escalator related deaths 

over a 13-year period between 1997 and 2010. Moreover, US Hospital admissions data 

indicates that there are 2,500 – 6,000 non-fatal escalator incidents reported annually, 75.0% 

- 84.9% of these incidents are falls, and 3% - 20% are due to entrapments (McCann, 2013; 

O’Neil et al., 2008). By comparison, in Great Britain (GB) detailed escalator incident research 

is limited. By way of example, a research report (Page & Hough, 1989), was produced 30 

years ago by the Health and Safety Executive (HSE), the main regulator for health and safety 

legislation. Although there is no current central repository of escalator incidents in GB, any 

incident which involves a member of the public within a workplace, which is severe enough 

to necessitate hospital treatment, should be reported to the appropriate enforcing authority 

through the HSE online portal. Subsequently, severe escalator incidents are recorded in 

HSE’s Reporting of Injuries, Diseases and Dangerous Occurrences Regulations 2013 75 

(RIDDOR) database (The Reporting of Injuries, Diseases and Dangerous Occurrences 

Regulations, 2013). The present study will therefore seek to extend current understanding 

of the factors that contribute to escalator incidents through analysis of the RIDDOR 

database. 

The research examining the causes of escalator-related incidents is limited (Chi et al., 

2006; Page & Hough, 1989, Xing et al., 2019). Using an escalator involves a person getting 

onto and then off a moving surface (Hsu, et al., 2015). During this behaviour, it is likely that 

several performance influencing factors (PIFs) (HSE, 1999) could contribute to an escalator 

incident occurring (Al-Sharif, 2006; Basir et al., 2018). To explore the potential PIFs that lead 

to an incident, the current research adopts a human factors approach to produce an 

investigative taxonomy for escalator incidents involving members of the public. A human 

factors approach focuses on how a human performs an action/task and aims to identify and 

categorise the PIFs which may influence the outcome of the action/task in a bid to reduce 

user error and prevent injury (Noyes, 2002; Stanton et al., 2013; Wilson & Corlett, 1995). 

Previous escalator incident research has adopted a human factors approach (Al-Sharif, 2006; 

Chi et al., 2006),yet their application has been limited as they have not considered a 

person’s age as a PIF (Al-Sharif, 2006) or they have not considered that there may be 

multiple PIFs that lead to an incident occurring (Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 2019). 

There are several investigative human factors taxonomies available to incident 

investigators (Stanton et al., 2013). However, extant taxonomies have been built around the 

premise of there being a conventional organisational structure (HSE, 1999; Kyriakidis et al., 

2018). Such taxonomy design does not adequately capture how members of the public may 

behave when using an escalator, as they are not required to be trained, supervised, or 

adhere to specific procedures as outlined by an organisation. Further, existing taxonomies 76 

can be over complex (O’Connor & Walker, 2011) and require specialist knowledge to 

understand the terminology being used, meaning that application outside of a specialist 

setting could be limited. 

In summary, research investigating the potential causes of escalator incidents is 

limited, with extant literature (Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 2019) focusing on identifying the 

primary factor(s) that may lead to an incident and not any potential combinations of these 

factors. Human factors approaches have been proposed to offer a method with which to 

explore escalator incidents, but existing taxonomies (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2001) are 

limited as they do not offer sufficient consideration of how to investigate incidents, which 

solely involve a member of the public. To the address these gaps in the literature, the 

present research has two distinct aims: (i) to develop a new human factors taxonomy for the 

investigation of escalator incidents involving members of the public; and (ii) apply the 

taxonomy to the HSE RIDDOR dataset of serious escalator incidents to explore relationships 

between the factors of the taxonomy and gain insights into escalator incidents. 

3.3 Method 

3.3.1 Participants 

A retrospective review was conducted on all recorded escalator incidents (i.e., 

events that were identified using the keyword “escalator”), that had been reported to HSE 

between 31st January 2015 and 31st January 2016 and held in their RIDDOR database. All 

records were anonymised before being provided to the researchers. Each incident report 

included the date, age, and sex of the injured person, and a short description of the inci-

dent. The level of detail in each report varied as some only provided a brief narrative and 

the injured person’s sex. All returned incidents ( N = 439) were reviewed and cases that were 77 

deemed to be outside the scope of the study (i.e., involved an incident near an escalator but 

not involving use of the escalator) were removed, as were any duplicated reports. Subse-

quently, a sample of 403 non-fatal, serious escalator-related incidents, taken from 154 Local 

Authorities (LA) were analysed as part of this study. The study received approval from the 

University of Portsmouth ethics committee [ref: 0491-9C9F-3B86-C01E-FAEB-A9E2-A73E-

77CB] ( Section 9.1 Appendix 1 ). 

3.3.2 Procedure 

3.3.2.1 Development of the Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public 

(HFIT-MP) 

Reviewing incident reports and extant literature have been adopted in the 

development of incident taxonomies across domains, including shipping incidents (Chauvin 

et al., 2013), crane incidents (Shepherd et al., 2000), medical incidents (Mitchell et al., 

2015), and amusement park ride incidents (Woodcock, 2008). Therefore, this study took a 

similar approach by using academic literature but extended it by using both expert 

knowledge and HSE’s investigative taxonomy (HSE, 1999). Subsequently, the lead author 

produced the outline for the taxonomy integrating: (i) findings from current escalator injury 

research (Al-Sharif, 2006; Chi et al., O’Neil et al., 2008); (ii) falls literature regarding stairway 

negotiation (Jacobs, 2016), and risk factors associated with falls (Hamacher et al., 2011); (iii) 

expert knowledge from an HSE Senior Ergonomics and Human Factors Specialist (LY) and an 

HSE Senior Falls Prevention Specialist (RS); and (iv) two existing human factors taxonomy 

approaches (HSE’s industry guidance (HSE, 1999), and the HFACS (Stanton et al., 2013; 

Wiegmann & Shappell, 2001). The unique inclusion of HSE specialist knowledge, which drew 

upon their experiences of investigating health and safety incidents, and their work with 78 

organisations outside the human factors discipline helped to ensure the terminology used in 

the taxonomy is understandable, a critique of previous works (Taib et al., 2011). 

The taxonomy consisted of four overarching themes: (1) Individual factors such a 

person’s age or sex; (2) Task factors relating to how an escalator user may have been behav-

ing at the time that they were injured, such as getting on or off the escalator or not holding 

the handrail; (3) Environmental factors relating to the escalator such as whether the escala-

tor was ascending or descending, or whether the escalator was operating correctly; and (4) 

Outcome factors , which reflect the impact of the incident on the person.. Figure 3.1 shows 

the factors overlapping; this is intentional as it is likely that while some factors may be inde-

pendently influential, more typically there will be a combination of factors that lead to an 

outcome. 

> Figure 3.1:
> Themes of the Proposed Taxonomy for Escalator Incidents

Following the approach of Mitchell et al. (2015) and other taxonomy-led investiga-

tions (Olsen & Williamson, 2017), the descriptions of the 403 incidents were reviewed by 79 

three researchers (PB, LY, RB) who identified all reported components of an escalator inci-

dent, which then became factors under the four themes. A hypothetical example would be 

if the incident narrative stated: “a lady fell whilst getting onto the ascending escalator”. The 

components recorded would be that the person’s sex was female (individual factor), she fell 

(outcome factor), she was getting on to the escalator (task factor) and it was the ascending 

escalator (environmental factor). Where a person was described as using a walking aid, such 

as a walking stick, these individuals were coded as having mobility issues. A combined list of 

all the factors identified by all three researchers across all incident reports was produced. 

This was reviewed by all three researchers separately, before they discussed the list to come 

to a consensus. There was disagreement between the researchers regarding the factors loss 

of footing and loss of balance, which were considered as possibly being separate codes for 

the same factor and used interchangeably in the narrative reports. As literature on escalator 

injuries is limited, it was agreed the two separate factors remained within the taxonomy. Fi-

nally, the lead author re-coded all 403 escalator incidents using the final HFIT-MP to provide 

the final coded dataset. 

3.3.3 Data Analysis 

3.3.3.1 Inter-rater reliability of the HFIT-MP 

Cohen’s Kappa was used to assess the inter-rater reliability of coding escalator inci-

dents according to the final HFIT-MP (McHugh, 2012). An independent coder who had not 

been involved in the development of the taxonomy coded approximately half ( N = 197) of 

the escalator incident reports. This coding was then compared to the final coded dataset 

produced by the lead researcher. A sample size of 197 would achieve a 95% confidence in-

terval with a width of .279 if the value of Kappa was 0.5 (i.e., moderate agreement) and a 80 

width of 0.168 if the value of Kappa was 0.7 or 0.9 (i.e., substantial, or almost perfect agree-

ment), assuming that the proportion of incidents classified identically by both coders was 

0.8 (PASS 2020, 2020). 

3.3.3.2 Exploratory Analysis of Factors among Escalator Incidents 

The occurrence of factors among escalator incidents was summarised using frequency 

distributions. Multiple Correspondence Analyses (MCA) (Panagiotakos & Pitsavos, 2004) was 

used to explore the positive relationships across the factors of the HFIT-MP and has been 

used in previous human factors incident investigations (Chauvin et al., 2013). Factors were 

screened for inclusion into the MCA using Fisher’s Exact test for all pairwise comparisons. P-

values were adjusted for multiple comparisons using Bonferroni correction. Only those fac-

tors that had a statistically significant association (at adjusted p<0.05) with at least one 

other factor were included in the MCA, which helps restrict noise in the MCA and is similar 

to the approach used by Ginis et al. (2010) to facilitate interpretation. 

To determine the number of dimensions for the MCA, the total inertia (variance) for 

each dimension was examined with dimensions accounting for 10% or more of the variance 

retained. To aid interpretation of the results, agglomerative hierarchical cluster analysis 

was used on the dimensional coordinates of the factor categories to group them together 

(Costantino et al., 2016). Complete-linkage cluster analysis was used because this leads to 

less chaining than other methods (Mamun et al., 2016). The results are presented in a den-

drogram, and the Duda-Hart Index was consulted to help decide how many groups should 

be created (Everitt & Rabe-Hesketh, 2006). The strength of association for factor categories 

within the same group was assessed using the Euclidean distance between the origin and 

the centroid of the group. This was turned into a score from 1 (weakest association) to 4 81 

(strongest association) by scaling the Euclidean distance. All statistical analyses were under-

taken using Stata V16.0 statistical software. 

3.4 Results 

3.4.1 Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) 

There were 25 factors included in the final HFIT-MP (see Figure 3.2). Twenty three of 

the factors include an ‘unknown’ category, with 12 instances of factors having just a ‘yes’ 

and ‘unknown’ option. The inclusion of ‘unknown’ was deliberate to highlight that the 

incident reports did not contain all of the information needed to accurately code all factors, 

and to avoid forcing the coder to make assumptions about the incident. Whilst most are 

self-explanatory, ‘unexpected movement’ requires further clarification. A passenger moves 

when stepping onto or off the moving escalator steps and so this is expected movement. 

However, they should not be moving (i.e. reaching for a falling item) or walking once 

boarded as escalators are not intended to be used as a staircase (British Standards Institute, 

2017) and so this would be unexpected movement. 82 

Figure 3.2: 

Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Member of the Public (HFIT-MP) for Escalator Incidents. Included are The Four Top-Level Themes of the Taxonomy and the Corre-

sponding Factors That are Within Each Theme. 83

Table 3.1 shows the percentage agreement between the two coders and the esti-

mated Cohen’s Kappa. All 23 factors (where Kappa could be estimated) had a Kappa of 0.60 

or above, indicating substantial agreement between coders; although some confidence in-

tervals were wide (e.g., distracted using the escalator). Ten factors had a Kappa of at least 

0.80, indicating almost perfect agreement between coders for these factors.

3.4.3.5 Multiple correspondence Analysis and Hierarchical Clustering 

Twelve of the 25 factors had a statistically significant association with at least one 

other factor during the pairwise screening process. These twelve factors were included in 

the MCA. The scree plot from the MCA showed a drop in the principal inertias after the first 

four dimensions (Figure 3.3). 

> Figure 3.3
> Scree Plot of Principal Inertias After Multiple Correspondence Analysis

The first dimension accounted for 21.3% of the total inertia (variance), the second 

dimension accounted for 17.9%, and the third for 12.9%. Each dimension after that ac-88 

counted for less than 10% of the total inertia. The first three dimensions were therefore re-

tained for use in the hierarchical cluster analysis. To identify the best group solution, the 

Duda-Hart Index was inspected. An eight-group solution had one of the highest Je(2)/Je(1) 

values and a low pseudo-T-squared value followed by an increase in the pseudo-T-squared 

value (Everitt & Rabe-Hesketh, 2006) (Figure 3.4, Table 3.6), suggesting this was a reasona-

ble number of groups.
