3.5 Discussion 

Despite research evidence demonstrating that escalator-related incidents can result 

in fatalities (McCann, 2013), investigation of the potential causes is limited, with extant liter-

ature (Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 2019) only focusing on identifying the primary factor(s) 

that may lead to an incident and not the potential combinations of these factors. The aims 

of this research were to: 1) develop a new human factors taxonomy for the investigation of 

escalator incidents involving members of the public; and 2) to apply the taxonomy to the 

HSE RIDDOR dataset of serious escalator incidents to reveal relationships between the fac-

tors of the taxonomy, and subsequently gain new understanding into the factors that con-

tribute towards escalator incidents. The following discussion is split into two sections, first 

evaluating the HFIT-MP in respect of its design and second, the applicability of the HFIT-MP 

to escalator incidents. 

3.5.1 Development of the HFIT-MP 

This study has developed a new taxonomy that enables reliable coding of escalator in-

cidents to help researchers identify performance influence factors (PIFs). In line with previ-

ous incident taxonomy approaches (Shepherd et al., 2000; Chauvin et al., 2013), four key 

themes were established as a basis for the HFIT-MP: individual, task, environment, and out-

come. Yet, unlike previous approaches the HFIT-MP was designed to accommodate the be-

haviours of members of the public, which are not constrained by conventional organisa-

tional structures that previous taxonomies have been designed around (e.g., the Human 

Factors Analysis and Classification System, HFACS (Wiegmann & Shappell, 2001)). Further, 

the HFIT-MP was designed in an ‘open’ manner (i.e., four top level themes as opposed to 94 

specifics) in order to ensure that factors relevant to an escalator incident were not inadvert-

ently missed (Baysari et al., 2009; Salmon et al., 2012), and to enable prospective research-

ers to make adaptations to fit the taxonomy due to a limited level of information available in 

incident reports ( Mitchell et al. , 2015 ). 

The HFIT-MP was refined using escalator incidents that were serious enough to have 

been reported to the HSE and as such will have necessitated hospital treatment for the in-

jured person. Despite this, there were several factors that had minimal responses within es-

calator incident reports. For example, in 74% of incidents, it was unknown whether a person 

was traveling with a companion or not. The large proportion of ‘unknowns’ in the dataset 

demonstrated the inconsistent and incomplete nature of narrative reporting of incidents 

(Davies et al., 2007; Rossignol, 2015; Woodcock; 2008). The current results therefore rein-

force the need for a uniform way of reporting not only escalator incidents but incidents 

more generally (Rossignol, 2015), to adequately identify common factors, which may facili-

tate the development interventions to reduce incidents from occurring. The HFIT-MP could 

form the basis of such a reporting system and extend to other incidents involving members 

of the public such as stair way falls (e.g., Jacobs, 2016). Moreover, low frequency factors 

were included within the HFIT-MP due to the fact that, although infrequent, they may be 

underrepresented in the current dataset or may contribute to an incident when considered 

in combination with other factors (Reason, 1990). 

There is potential for bias to be introduced during the development of any taxonomy 

(Mays & Pope, 1995). To ensure rigour and to keep biases to a minimum, the taxonomy was 

developed through a triangulation of various sources such as academic literature (e.g., 

Wiegmann & Shappell, 2001), HSE guidance (HSE, 1999) and subject experts in ergonomics, 

human factors and falls prevention. Further the dataset analysed was not limited to one 95 

specific organisation but comprised data collected from 154 local authorities across GB, 

highlighting that the taxonomy and associated findings are likely to be generalisable to the 

wider population of escalator incidents. Further the results of the inter-rater agreement 

analysis highlighted a good degree of consistency between two coders with factors having 

substantial to almost perfect levels of agreement. To improve these scores further addi-

tional training or a specific training course could be provided for users to practice imple-

menting the taxonomy (Goodell et al., 2016). 

3.5.2 Application of the HFIT-MP 

MCA followed by hierarchical cluster analysis were used to identify and then explore 

the relationships between factors involved in serious escalator incidents. Comparable to es-

calator incident research in Taiwan, Hong Kong, the US, China, and GB (Chi et al., 2006; Lee 

at al., 2010; Howland et al., 2012; O’Neil et al., 2008; Page & Hough, 1989; Xing et al. 2019) 

the results demonstrated that most incidents in the HSE dataset resulted from a person fall-

ing whilst using the escalator (93%) with entrapment incidents being the second most fre-

quent (5%). This finding suggests a potential uniformity regarding the most prevalent type 

of escalator incidents, irrespective of geographical location. As falls were the most fre-

quently recorded outcome, this incident occurred together with many other factors, but 

with relatively weak associations (compared to other groups identified). Specifically, the 

MCA indicated that fall incidents were frequently reported together with factors including 

the individual being aged 65 years or older, getting onto the escalator, the escalator being a 

descending direction of travel, the individual was not moving (i.e., they stepped onto the es-

calator then remained stationary), and traveling alone and lost their balance (i.e., a stumble, 

misstep). Explanation for this combination of factors can be discerned from literature 96 

demonstrating that an older person’s perceptual-motor control can be compromised by a 

combination of: reduced mobility due to age-related physiological changes (Kenny et al., 

2017), the disorientation experienced when using descending escalators (Cohn & Lasley, 

1990), and the complex demand of stepping onto a moving surface (Hsu et al., 2015). De-

clines in perceptual-motor control associated with ageing may lead to a loss of foot place-

ment accuracy (Roerdink et al., 2021) or loss of balance (Zhang et al., 2020), ultimately lead-

ing to a fall. Interventions that aim to reduce escalator falls may target older adult popula-

tions that appear to reflect the most at-risk demographic (Hoogendijk et al., 2019). Such an 

intervention could look to reduce the burden on an older person stepping onto a moving 

surface, perhaps using different markings on escalator steps (Jacobs, 2016) or changes to 

the speed of the moving surface (Hsu et al., 2015). 

To note, previous falls research has indicated females aged 65 and older are at 

greater risk of a fall compared to males (Timsina et al., 2017) and comparable to previous 

escalator incident research (Howland et al., 2012, Page & Hough, 1989), my research identi-

fies a higher proportion of those injured were female (62%). However, as no statistically sig-

nificant association was observed with sex and any of the HFIT-MP factors, this was not in-

cluded in the MCA (Ginis et al., 2010) meaning that beyond the observation of frequency, it 

is difficult to make assumption as to the role of sex in escalator incidents. One potential ex-

planation is that this is a limitation of the large proportion of ‘unknowns’ within the data set 

meaning there is no significant association observed with sex and the other PIFs. As such, 

other data sources should be considered as will be discussed in the proceeding section. 

Entrapments accounted for just 5% of incidents and the MCA suggested that they 

were frequently reported together with adults aged 25 - 34. Whilst the low percentage of 

entrapment injuries is consistent with extant literature (Chi et al., 2006; Lee et al., 2010), 97 

previous findings suggested that children under the age of 5 age most frequently encoun-

tered entrapment injuries. In the current study, the strong association between adults aged 

25 - 34 and entrapments were not frequently associated with other factors, most likely due 

to the fact that the number of entrapments in the current dataset were too few or they 

were more strongly related to other factors and so appeared in different groupings in the 

MCA. Conversely, it could be that there are additional influential factors not currently in-

cluded in the HFIT-MP such as risk-taking behaviour amongst younger persons resulting in 

behaviours that lead to entrapments (Albert et al., 2013). Future research could focus on 

identifying individual attitudes and perceptions to the risks associated with escalator use to 

better understand this finding. 

The MCA also identified other factor combinations that tended to occur together in 

escalator incident reports; although they did not have an affiliation with a particular incident 

type (i.e., a fall or entrapment). These relationships offer useful insights into patterns of inci-

dents for health and safety professionals. For example, one group included the factors of 

individuals aged 11-17 or 45-54 years, getting off the escalator, and the emergency stop not 

being pressed. Together these factors could suggest locomotor adaptation issues when dis-

embarking from the moving escalator to the stationary landing surface, leading to the emer-

gency stop being activated. Current research in this area is limited to the embarkation of 

moving surfaces (Hsu et al. 2015) and so future research could focus on the transition from 

moving to stationary surfaces. In addition, another factor group suggested that injuries in-

volving those aged less than 10 also tended to include unexpected movement (e.g., walking) 

and the escalator not operating normally (i.e., switched off). This finding is likely due to the 

fact that the step height on an escalator is 24cm, which is greater than the 17.8cm height 

deemed safe for stair locomotion (Jacobs, 2016). Therefore, an intervention could focus on 98 

discouraging escalator users (in particular younger children) from using switched off escala-

tors as staircases, as they are not designed for such purpose (British Standards Institute, 

2017). Alternatively, a mechanical redesign of an escalator to make them more user-friendly 

for walking could be considered. 

3.5.3 Future Directions 

The data analysed in the current study consisted of serious escalator incidents re-

ported to the HSE where the injured individual required hospital treatment. As there is no 

other central repository for escalator incidents within GB, the HSE database and this analysis 

provides invaluable understanding of a range of incidents that occurred across multiple or-

ganisations and geographical locations. Furthermore, it is pertinent to acknowledge that less 

serious escalator incidents can occur (DeMers et al., 2011) and the factors that contribute to 

these incidents may not be equivalent to the incidents identified in the current study. Given 

that accidents that do not require hospital treatment are likely to take place and be rec-

orded by respective organisations through their own internal health and safety reporting 

procedures, future work could aim to examine a wider sample of escalator incidents to build 

a better understanding of the multi-faceted nature of such accidents (Howland et al., 2012). 

Furthermore, examination of a wider sample of incidents would enable a further level of rig-

our to be applied to the HFIT-MP developed in the current study. 

In addition to using other data sources, it would be prudent to examine escalator inci-

dents using data collected through other means than narrative incident reports. By way of 

example, Robinovitch et al. (2013) recorded and coded closed-circuit television (CCTV) foot-

age of fall incidents in care home settings as a method to systematically quantify the causes 

of falls in older adults. Such methodology could be applied to escalator incidents to provide 99 

further understanding on the reasons why escalator incidents may happen and to help shed 

light on some of the ‘unknown’ factors that were named in some of the RIDDOR incident re-

ports examined in the current study. With such suggestion in mind, the HFIT-MP developed 

in the current study provides a framework for the systematic coding of video data to exam-

ine the human factors associated with escalator incidents. 

3.5.4 Conclusion 

Escalator incidents are seldom the result of one causal factor, rather a cumulation of 

PIFs. To examine the multi-faceted nature of escalator incidents, the present research devel-

oped a new taxonomy - the Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public 

(HFIT-MP) - using a combination of academic literature, subject matter expertise and two 

existing HF taxonomies. The applicability of this taxonomy was assessed by applying it to the 

HSE dataset of GB escalator incidents and was found to have reasonable inter-rater reliabil-

ity. Subsequent analysis revealed potential combinations of factors that are frequently re-

ported together when an escalator incident occurs. For example, fall incidents frequently 

occurred with persons over the age of 65, traveling alone, losing footing, getting onto the 

escalator, and the escalator descending. This analysis provides researchers and health and 

safety practitioners alike, with new understanding on the pertinent factors that may con-

tribute to escalator incidents, which can be utilised in the development of interventions. 

This research also highlights the need to improve narrative reporting of incidents as indi-

cated by the high proportion of missing information for identified PIFs. Future work would 

benefit from using the HFIT-MP on different escalator injury datasets and the use of footage 

of incidents to further enhance understanding on the factors contributing to escalator inci-

dents. 100 

FOREWORD TO CHAPTER 4 

The results of Chapter 3 demonstrate the utility of the designed Human Factors Inci-

dent Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) to categorise and explore severe esca-

lator incidents. The findings indicated falls to be the most common type of injury, resulting 

from potentially multiple PIFs. However, it is likely that less severe incidents happen on es-

calators which require attention so as not to underestimate injuries nor ignore PIFs that 

could lead to less severe injuries. Subsequently, Chapter 4 employs the use of the HFIT-MP 

to 1,966 escalator incident reports provided by industry members of the HSE’s escalator 

shared research group. Chapter 4 again falls under the Safety-I (i.e., review of when things 

go wrong) premise to review incident data for pertinent PIFs. In so doing, the aim of Chapter 

4 is to provide a validation of the designed taxonomy and its applicability to escalator inci-

dents that do not just lead to hospitalisation. 101 

CHAPTER: 4 A HUMAN FACTORS ANALYSIS OF ESCALATOR INCIDENTS INVOLVING 

MEMBERS OF THE PUBLIC: AN EXAMINATION OF THE UNITED KINGDOM’S RETAIL AND 

TRAVEL SECTORS. 

4.1 Abstract 

Background: 

Current understanding of escalator injuries stems from data associated with 

hospitalisations. Nonetheless, less severe incidents occur, meaning existing research may 

underestimate injuries and mask patterns of behaviour associated with an injury. 

Recognising this issue, this study used the Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members 

of the Public (HFIT-MP) to examine escalator injures that had ‘less severe’ outcomes taken 

from United Kingdom’s (U.K.) retail and travel sectors. 

Methods: 

1966 ‘less severe’ escalator incidents were obtained from six (U.K.) retail and travel 

sector organisations and coded according to the HFIT-MP. Cohen’s Kappa was used for inter-

rater agreement and Multiple Correspondence Analysis (MCA) coupled with Bi-plots to 

identify performance influencing factors (PIFs) that occurred together. 

Results: 

84% of ‘less severe’ incidents were falls on escalators. 24 of 26 of HIFIT-MP factors 

achieved a Kappa greater than 0.60. MCA and Bi-plots demonstrated that several PIFs 

tended to occur with 'less severe' incidents. For example, falls occurred more frequently 

with those aged 45-84, facing the correct direction of travel, losing their balance, carrying 

more than one item, not holding the handrail, using the ascending escalator, the emergency 

stop being pressed, with General Practitioner treatment sought. 102 

Conclusion: 

Like previous research, less severe escalator incidents were predominantly due to 

falls and were associated with a combination of PIFs. This analysis provides great insight for 

health and safety practitioners when designing interventions to reduce escalator injuries. 

The strong reliability scores support the use of the HFIT-MP to other escalator injury data, 

beyond that used in its development. 

4.2 Introduction 

Escalators offer a means of travel between floors (Bodendorf et al., 2014) and are 

common in most urbanised environments (Chi et al., 2006; Howland et al., 2012; Lee et al., 

2010). Although escalators are common, there were 39 recorded escalator-related deaths in 

the United States between 1997 to 2010 (McCann, 2013). Furthermore, non-fatal incidents 

reported to US hospitals indicate escalator injuries range from 2,500 to 6,000 annually 

(McCann, 2013; O’Neil et al., 2008). Although escalator research in the UK is limited, Chap-

ter 3 found 93% of the 403 severe incidents reported to the Health and Safety Executive 

(HSE) between Jan 2015 and Jan 2016 were falls that required hospital treatment. Unilater-

ally, falls account for most escalator incidents, with other researchers reporting falls to ac-

count for 77.0% to 84.9% of all escalator incidents (McCann, 2013; O’Neil et al., 2008). 

During the examination of the causes of severe escalator incidents to members of 

the public, Chapter 3 argues that several performance influencing factors (PIFs) (HSE, 1999) 

may contribute to an escalator incident. To test this hypothesis, I developed the Human Fac-

tors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) (Figure 4.1), which enables the 

identification of PIFs associated with escalator incidents. PIFs are categorised into four over-

arching themes: 1) Individual factors (e.g., age, gender, mobility issues); 2) Task factors (e.g., 103 

getting onto an escalator, loss of footing, carrying items); 3) Environmental factors (e.g., es-

calator direction); and 4) Outcome factors (e.g., type of incident, such as a fall or entrap-

ment). Using this taxonomy, Chapter 3 found that severe falling incidents on escalators oc-

curred more frequently with those aged 65 or over, getting onto a descending escalator, 

alone, and losing their footing. Making any unexpected movements (i.e., running or jumping 

on the escalator), however, did not appear to contribute to such incidents. 

> Figure 4.1:
> Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP)

A potential limitation in the approach of Chapter 3 and other escalator injury re-

search (O’Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et al., 2013), is that the HFIT-MP was developed using 

data related to hospital admissions, which in turn may underestimate injuries and ignore 

the patterns of behaviour leading to less severe injuries (DeMers et al., 2011). It is likely that 

less severe injuries that happen on escalators are recorded by the organisations managing 

the escalator (DeMers et al.,2011; Woodcock, 2008). Drupsteen and Guldenmund (2014) 

suggested that the initial investigation by an organisation’s safety manager may focus on 

the overt cause of an incident (e.g., ‘rushing on the escalator’, Chi et al., 2006), as opposed 

to a deeper analysis of the root causes of an incident (Stemn et al., 2018). Therefore, it 104 

would be prudent to explore organisational incident reports with the human factors 

approach of Chapter 3 to assess if the type of incidents (e.g., falls or entrapments) and any 

associated PIFs of less severe escalator injuries differ from those which are more severe. In 

addition, this provides further opportunity to validate the HFIT-MP using an alternative data 

set to that of Chapter 3. 

In summary, research investigating the potential causes of escalator incidents has 

identified falls to be one of the main causes of escalator injury (McCann, 2013; O’Neil et al., 

2008). Recent research using the HFIT-MP investigative taxonomy suggests an escalator fall 

is associated with several PIFs (Chapter 3) as opposed to one major cause (Chi et al., 2006). 

Yet this and other escalator incident research is limited to severe incident data sets (Chap-

ter 3; O’Neil et al., 2008), potentially underestimating injuries and patterns of behaviour 

that lead to an escalator incident. To address these gaps in the literature, the present re-

search has two distinct aims: (i) to use the HFIT-MP developed in Chapter 3), to identify and 

explore the PIFs frequently associated with escalator incidents that did not lead to hospitali-

sation; and (ii) to assess the applicability of the HFIT-MP to data sources other than those 

associated with severe injury. 

4.3 Method 

4.3.1 Procedure 

Data for this study were obtained by recruiting organisations from an escalator 

research steering group, chaired by the HSE. The steering group was comprised of members 

from organisations in the retail and travel sectors within the United Kingdom. A total of 

1,966 anonymised escalator incident reports dated between November 2013 and June 2017 

that did not lead to hospitalisation were provided to the researchers; 1,448 incidents came 

from four retail organisations (shopping centres/retail stores) and 518 came from two travel 105 

organisations (airport and train stations). The incident reports varied in the level of detail 

provided, but as a minimum, all reports included a narrative incident description. Ethical 

approval for this study was granted by the University of Portsmouth ethics committee 

(Reference: SFEC 2018-066B) ( Section 9.2 Appendix 2 ). All participating organisations of the 

HSE steering group were sent an information sheet describing the study and requesting to 

review any incident data they may have regarding escalators injuries. Organisational 

consent was provided by all six participating stakeholders. 

4.3.2 Application of the HFIT-MP 

Following the approach of Chapter 3 and other taxonomy led investigations (Mitchell 

et al., 2015; Olsen & Williamson, 2017) the lead author (PB) and a human factors specialist 

(NH) independently reviewed the 1,966 incident descriptions, reading through each to then 

discuss and agree any additional factors and categories that may not have been previously 

identified in the HFIT-MP. Each escalator incident description was then read, coded, and 

classified using the modified HFIT-MP by the lead author. If a factor was not mentioned in 

the incident description it was coded as ‘unknown’, as opposed to assuming that the poten-

tial factor was not present. For example, if intoxication was not mentioned in the report, 

this factor was coded as ‘unknown’ rather than assuming ‘no’. This prevents potentially in-

troducing bias through any assumptions made about whether the factor was present or not 

(Altman & Bland, 2007). Having a specific ‘unknown’ category rather than treating this as 

missing data also maximises the number of incidents included in the analysis. 106 

4.3.3 Data Analysis 

4.3.3.1 Inter-rater reliability of the HFIT-MP 

Cohen’s Kappa was used to assess the inter-rater reliability of the coding of less 

severe escalator incidents using the HFIT-MP (McHugh, 2012; Olsen & Williamson, 2015, 

2017). An independent coder, not involved in the review of this dataset, was provided with 

334 incidents from one organisation. The Cohen’s Kappa excluded three factors (‘Age’, 

‘Gender’ and ‘Location on the escalator’) because this specific information was recorded in 

this organisation’s incident report form and therefore, these factors did not vary. Cohen’s 

Kappa values ≤0 indicated no agreement, 0.01-0.20 indicated none to slight, 0.21-0.40 

indicated fair, 0.41-0.60 indicated moderate, 0.61-0.80 indicated substantial, and 0.81-1.00 

indicate almost perfect agreement (McHugh, 2012). 

4.3.3.2 Exploratory Analysis of Less Severe Escalator Incidents 

Following the methods described Chapter 3, an initial pairwise comparison analysis 

was used to screen factors before using Multiple Correspondence Analysis (MCA) 

(Panagiotakos & Pitsavos, 2004). This resulted in the factor ‘hesitancy’ being omitted as it 

had no significant association with any other factor and thus its removal helped to reduce 

the noise in interpretations of the MCA (Ginis et al., 2010). To determine the number of 

dimensions for the MCA, the total inertia (variance) for each dimension was examined with 

dimensions accounting for 10% or more of the variance retained. Chapter 3 uses 

agglomerative hierarchical cluster analysis to interpret the results of the MCA and find 

structure in the data (Chauvin et al., 2013). However, the current study was specifically 

interested in the type of incident (fall, entrapment, cut/graze, or an unclear incident type) 

and so a different method to interpret the MCA results, which places emphasis on exploring 

factor categories associated with the different type of incident was used. Following the 107 

recommendations of Torres-Salinas et al. (2013), the results of the MCA were plotted on a 

biplot to assist in data interpretation of multivariate data to aid decision-makers, in this case 

health and safety managers. In this method, two factor categories are considered positively 

associated if they lie on the same vector from the origin, negatively associated if they are in 

opposite directions from the origin, and there is no association if their vectors are at 90° 

from one another (Kamalja & Khanger, 2017). As such, a vector was drawn between the 

origin and each ‘type of incident’. Each vector was then used to plot areas within 15°, 15°-

30° and 30°-45°. Although the choice of cut-offs is subjective, these area-plots aid 

interpretation since those factor categories lying within the 15° area would have a much 

smaller distance between the vector and category indicating a stronger positive association 

(Kamalja & Khanger, 2017). All statistical analyses were undertaken using IBM SPSS V23 and 

Stata V16.01 statistical software. 

4.4 Results 

4.4.1 Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of The Public (HFIT-MP) 

All 25 factors from the HFIT-MP were confirmed. Four new factors were identified 

based on the incident report data collected in the present study: one new ‘Outcome’ factor 

(‘Treatment received’); one new ‘Task’ factor (‘Facing the wrong direction of travel’); and 

two ‘Environmental’ factors (‘What was the location of the incident on the escalator?’ and 

‘Was a barrier in place at the escalator entrance/exit?’) (Figure 4.2). Additionally, a 

‘cut/grazes’ option was added to the ‘Type of incident’ factor as the narrative reports 

suggested a person had received a cut/graze but were not specifically attributed by the 

investigator to a ‘fall’ or an ‘entrapment’, for example catching their hand on something 

sharp on the handrail. 108 

Figure 4.2: 

Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) Including Factor and Category Breakdown .

Note. * and bold indicates a new factor/addition for this data set. 109 

4.4.2 Inter-rater Reliability 

Table 4.1 shows the percentage agreement between the two coders and the 

estimated Cohen’s Kappa of one organisation’s incident reports ( N = 334). Of the factors, 

where Cohen’s Kappa could be estimated, 24 of 26 factors had a Cohen’s Kappa of 0.61 or 

above, indicating substantial agreement between coders; although some confidence 

intervals were wide (e.g., playing on the escalator). Seventeen factors had a Cohen’s Kappa 

of at least 0.81 indicating almost perfect agreement between coders for these factors. The 

two factors which scored less than 0.60 (‘Hesitant using the escalator’ and ‘Distracted using 

the escalator;) still had over 95% agreement between the coders.

4.4.3 Descriptive Data

paramedic assistance . However, 16.7% of persons ( N = 329) either did not need or refused 

any medical assistance. The emergency stop was reported as being pressed in 8.1% ( N =

4.4.3.4 Environmental factors

were not (7.3% vs 2.3%); the bottom of the escalator had 11.2% occurrences of incidents (N 

= 262); in 10.7% (N = 211) of incidents a functioning lift was available as an alternative; and

4.4.3.5 Multiple Correspondence Analysis 

MCA was used to explore the positive associations across the factors. Exploring the 

total inertia (variance) for each dimension, it was decided that a 2-dimensional model would 

be adequate as the first dimension accounted for 36.45% of the total inertia (variance), the 

second dimension accounted for 11.18% and subsequent dimensions after that accounted 

for less than 10% of the total inertia.

4.5 Discussion 

Escalator safety research is limited and has focussed primarily on severe escalator 

incident data sets (O’Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et al., 2013). This focus in the literature on 

severe incidents may lead to an underestimation of the possible factors and patterns of 

behaviour that lead to a wider number of reported injuries, including those that do not lead 

to hospitalisation (DeMers et al., 2011). To address these gaps, the aims of the current 

research were to: (i) to use the HFIT-MP developed in Chapter 3, to identify and explore the 

PIFs frequently associated with escalator incidents that did not lead to hospitalisation; and 

(ii) to assess the applicability of the HFIT-MP to data sources other than those associated 

with severe injury. 122 

4.5.1 Application of the HFIT-MP to Less Severe Escalator Incident Data 

Comparable to analyses of ‘severe’ escalator incidents (Chapter 3; Schminke et al., 

2013), less severe escalator incidents are primarily falls on escalator (84%), and a small 

number of entrapments (8%). In addition, the current study found a small number of ‘cuts 

and grazes’ (3%) on escalators not specifically attributable to a ‘fall’ or an ‘entrapment’ 

within the narrative incident reports. This finding combined with ‘first aid’ or a ‘refusal/no 

treatment’ as most frequent types of treatment provided reflects the lesser severity of this 

data set. Of note is the high proportion of missing data in this data set as represented by the 

‘unknown’ factor categories. The inclusion of the unknown factor categories was to reduce 

the potential for bias resulting from excluding data because of missing data (Altman & 

Bland, 2007). The results of this study illustrate the point made in Chapter 3 and other 

researchers (Davies et al., 2007; Woodcock, 2008) regarding the inconsistency of narrative 

incident reports, even when the reports are made within the same organisation, following 

the same organisational reporting procedures (Rossignol, 2015). Ultimately caution is 

advised in interpretation, although some factor categories were infrequent (e.g., handrail 

use) they were still included in the analysis as it is possible, they are underrepresented in 

the current data set. This may be due to the inconsistencies in narrative reports (Davies et 

al., 2007; Woodcock; 2008; Rossignol, 2015) or perhaps a limitation of the reporting 

procedure with the investigator just looking for the most overt cause (Dodshon & Hassall, 

2017) and therefore potential PIFs to an incident should not be discounted (HSE, 1999; 

Reason, 1990). Subsequently this is a point for future research to explore as discussed in the 

corresponding section. 123 

4.5.2 Falling Incidents 

The results of the MCA indicated that, among less severe escalator incidents, falls 

were most frequently associated with persons who were 45-84, facing the correct direction 

of travel, losing their balance, carrying more than one item, not holding the handrail, using 

the ascending escalator, the emergency stop being pressed, and GP treatment being sought. 

A possible explanation for the cumulation of these factors is that carrying multiple items 

may lead to a dual task situation by which a person is concentrating on both carrying several 

items and trying to successfully navigate the moving escalator surface, dividing attention 

between the two tasks impacting a person’s posture and / or gait which could lead to a fall 

occurring (Bayot et al., 2018). A review of anterior load carriage on gait parameters by 

Simpkins et al. (2022) demonstrate carrying a forward load (e.g., a shopping bag/product) 

that is 10% over a person’s bodyweight (a plausible proxy for multiple items) has been 

shown to reduce step length. Comparable results have been observed in carrying oxygen 

tanks on the backs of fire fighters in which heavier tanks had greater impact on a 

firefighter’s gait, increasing falling risks (Park et al., 2010). The increased physical demand 

caused by carrying multiple and/or heavy objects combined with the age-related reduction 

in an individual’s action capabilities may invite maladaptive behaviours, such as reduced 

foot placement accuracy (Roerdink et al., 2021), impacting a person’s success when getting 

onto the moving escalator surface (Hsu et al. 2015). Further, carrying more than one item 

may have led to people not holding the handrail which could help arrest a fall (Maki et al., 

1998) resulting in the emergency stop being engaged and the resulting injury leading to GP 

treatment. Yet as biomechanical research on escalator use is limited in the literature, future 

research could seek to address the nature of the relationship between carried items and 

successful escalator use. 124 

To prevent fall incidents, a suitable intervention based on the current findings may 

be to follow stair safety guidelines (Jacobs, 2016) and previous escalator incident research 

(Li et al., 2021) that suggests instructing escalator passengers (in particular, older 

passengers) to refrain from carrying multiple items and to hold the handrail (Maki et al., 

1998; Nemire et al., 2016). Such instruction may help to alleviate a fall (Dusenberry et al., 

2009; Jacobs, 2016; Maki et al., 1998); However, unlike stairs, the escalator handrail is a 

moving object/target that a person is required to reach and grasp. Therefore, if someone 

was at the start of an escalator, their attentional demand would be divided between 

stepping onto the moving escalator (Hsu et al., 2015), carrying multiple items (Roberts et al., 

2018; Yang, Ban et al., 2022), and grasping the moving handrail (Hesse & Deubel, 2011). 

Further, age-related decline in mobility and co-ordination (Kenny, Romero-Ortuno & Kumar, 

2017) may make the overall demand of getting on to an escalator particularly challenging 

and hence increase the potential for an incident to occur. Alternatively, if it is assumed 

someone had successfully boarded the escalator and then had an incident the handrail 

movement would be equivalent to the movement of the escalator treads potentially 

reducing any attentional demand as the handrail would be perceived stationary. Therefore, 

the issue may be that the design of an escalator handrail is such that it does not help a 

passenger who stumbles or loses balance to arrest a fall that has begun (Dusenberry et al., 

2009; Maki et al., 1998). Consequently, more in-depth research on the impact of escalator 

handrail use and the influence of carrying multiple items on escalator users is required 

before suggesting increased handrail use would reduce falling incidents. 

Chapter 3 reported that more severe falls occurred more frequently on the 

descending escalator; in complement to this, the current analysis suggests less severe falls 

occur more frequently on the ascending escalator. A plausible explanation for the difference 125 

in direction and injury severity could be that when a person falls on the escalator it is 

typically when embarking the moving surface (Chapter 3; Hsu et al., 2015). For an ascending 

escalator, this would result in a fall from a low height, which is potentially less severe 

(Freeman et al., 2014). In contrast, a fall on a descending escalator could result in a fall from 

a much greater height as the user falls down the remaining treads of the escalator, leading 

to the person sustaining more severe injuries and requiring hospital treatment (Chapter 3; 

Howland et al., 2012). This interpretation, however, should be treated with caution as 

escalator direction, falling distances and injury specifics on escalators has received limited 

attention in extant literature with Xing et al. (2020) finding no difference between escalator 

direction and injury severity for injured metro passengers in China. 

Like Chapter 3, sex does not appear to be pertinent to falls despite past escalator 

and wider falls research highlighting that females and those aged 65 and older are at greater 

risk of a fall compared to males (Timsina et al., 2017; Howland et al., 2012, Page & Hough, 

1989). In the current research, females are more frequently associated with ‘unclear’ 

incidents and ‘unknown’ factors. This may be an artifact of the data because of the 

inconsistent reporting practices as discussed in the ‘unclear’ incident section. 

4.5.3 Entrapment Incidents 

As reported in previous escalator research, entrapment incidents were found to be 

less frequent than falls (Chapter 3; Xing et al., 2020). Entrapments were most frequently 

(within 15° of the vector) associated with items of clothing being caught on the descending 

escalator. Chi et al. (2006) highlighted that ‘improper outfits’ are positively associated with 

escalator entrapments; however, they only identified five instances out of 194 reports 

where clothing led to an entrapment. Research has also suggested that specific types of 

footwear such as rubber shoes with extended lips (Tatla et al., 2001) or rubber clogs (Lim et 126 

al., 2010; Ong et al., 2016) can lead to entrapments. Lim et al. (2010) identified that rubber 

clogs tend to be caught within the moving treadplate as they are loose and flimsy. 

Furthermore, although entrapments are not as prevalent as falls, Bress et al. (2018) 

highlighted that foot and toe entrapments (which they attributed to unsuitable footwear) 

on escalators are increasing, from around 2% to 3% of annual escalator incidents. A possible 

intervention may be to warn users of the risk of specific types of footwear (i.e., rubber clogs 

or shoes with extended lips) via an additional mandatory sign to the current four required 

by escalator construction standards (British Standards Institute, 2017). Alternatively, or in 

addition, signs could be used to encourage an alternative means of travel (i.e., lift or stairs) 

for at risk passengers. Framing of such messages could consider Jennings et al’s (2017) 

review of stair versus escalator interventions, which highlights the need to use: images; text; 

time and fitness-based motivational messages; and a variety of signage sizes, to increase 

uptake by passengers. However, further research into this would be required as the aim of 

Jennings et al’s (2017) review is on the promotion of physical activity rather than promotion 

of user safety. Furthermore, the impact on escalator user behaviour of such additional signs 

would need to be explored in future work. 

4.5.4 Cut/Graze Incidents 

Cut/graze injuries were most frequently associated with those aged 6-24, playing on 

an escalator, and the escalator not functioning normally (i.e., switched off). The height for 

each escalator step is 24cm (British Standards Institute, 2017), which is much higher than 

the 17.8cm deemed safe for a static staircase (Jacobs, 2016). A static escalator may be 

incorrectly perceived as comprising step heights that are equivalent to a staircase, and in 

turn, persons may catch their feet / legs on the treads whilst playing, leading to a cut/graze. 127 

The notion of playing on the escalator could be of particular issue for younger passengers 

(aged 6-24) who may be predisposed to active risk-taking behaviour as part of their 

development (Spear, 2000). Additionally, persons using stationary escalator may find 

themselves influenced by the ‘broken escalator phenomenon’ (Reynolds & Bronstein; 2003; 

2004). This phenomenon results in a person boarding a stationary surface inappropriately 

fast and having a large sway in posture resulting from their incorrect prior judgement that 

the surface would be moving. Although this effect appears to degrade with practice, it is 

possible that the initial error may lead to an injury on the static escalator treads. 

As means of intervention, escalators that are static could be prevented from being 

used as a staircase. The current escalator standard highlights that an escalator is not 

designed to be used as a staircase and it is recommended that they are not used as such or 

as an emergency exit (British Standards Institute, 2017). However, some organisations do 

consider escalators to be used in part of their evacuation procedures, with modelling 

suggesting the suspension of escalators as an evacuation method could increase evacuation 

times in an emergency (Yang, Zhang et al., 2022). Therefore, it is perhaps the design of the 

escalator that needs to be revisited with future researchers focusing on making the steps in 

line with safer stair dimensions (Jacobs, 2016). Yet, even if escalators were redesigned 

Shiwakoti et al’s (2017) survey of 1271 Australian train passengers suggests escalators are 

not perceived to be viable exits in an emergency and so any such alterations to design 

would need to be coupled with an information campaign to raise awareness and look to 

influence perceptions of escalators as a means of emergency exit. 

Additionally, it is also possible to suggest that there may have been mechanical 

issues with the escalators leading to the observed problems, highlighting the need to ensure 

regular maintenance (Bardyshev et al., 2017). 128 

4.5.5 Unclear Incidents 

4.2% of all incidents were classed as ‘unclear’ due to a lack of clarity from the data in 

the incident report as to the type of incident that occurred. The results of the MCA indicate 

that ‘unclear incidents’ were most positively associated with mostly unknown factor 

categories. These findings are congruent with other narrative report-led incident research, 

that such data sources can often be inconsistent and/or incomplete (Chapter 3; Davies et 

al., 2007; Rossignol, 2015; Woodcock, 2008). Several researchers have argued for a 

consistent approach to the reporting of escalator incidents (Chapter 3; Ruibal et al., 2015; 

Xing et al., 2020). The revised HFIT-MP presented in this paper, could form the basis of such 

a reporting system. 

4.5.6 Evaluation HFIT-MP on Less Severe Incidents Data 

All factors previously reported in Chapter 3 for severe escalator incidents were 

present in this ‘less severe’ data set. In addition, four new factors were added because of 

greater incident detail in the industry incident reports. These new factors did not alter the 

overall structure of the HFIT-MP (i.e., Individual, Task, Environment & Outcome) and 

although not all factors were as frequent as each other, the results of the MCA help support 

the suitability of the HFIT-MP. Consistent with Chapter 3 the results of the present study, 

highlight that an escalator incident, even with a less severe outcome, is associated with a 

combination of PIFs (Al-Sharif, 2006; Basir et al., 2018) rather than one main causal factor 

(Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 2019). This finding is important for a health and safety 

professional as it provides greater insight when designing safety interventions. Consulting 

the frequency count of PIFs alone would just inform a practitioner that the ascending 129 

escalator (the most frequent incident location for less severe incidents) alone was 

important, but this does not indicate who or why a person may be most at risk in using this 

direction of escalator. 

Cohen’s Kappa was used to assess the reliability of two independent researchers 

coding incident report data using HFIT-MP framework (McHugh, 2012; Olsen & Williamson, 

2015, 2017). Most coded factors scored moderate to almost perfect associations of 

agreement. However, low Cohen’s Kappa scores for ‘Hesitant using the escalator’ and 

‘Distracted using the escalator’ suggests that these factors may not be adequately defined 

for the investigator to make a successful judgement if the PIF was present (Olsen & 

Williamson, 2015; 2017; Yang et al., 2015). However, there were few recorded instances of 

these factors within the organisational sample provided to the independent coder (N = 1 

and N = 20, respectively). As such, any disagreement could have disproportionately 

impacted on the reliability statistic. To address this in future work, it may be necessary to 

provide clearer examples of what is and is not meant by ‘hesitation’ and ‘distraction’. 

Additionally, longer periods of training for taxonomy users to practice the HFIT-MP may also 

be beneficial for those less frequent factors (Goodell et al., 2016). 

4.5.7 Future Research 

Comparable to previous research on narrative incident reports (Mitchell et al.,2015; 

Rossignol, 2015; Woodcock, 2008), there was variation in the quality of information 

regarding each incident, even when reports were compiled by the same organisation 

(Rossignol, 2015). One possible suggestion would be to use the HFIT-MP as the basis of an 

escalator incident reporting system. Feedback from shared research stakeholders from 

which the current study’s data were obtained, suggested that adaptations could be made to 130 

existing frameworks to encompass factors of the HFIT-MP in reporting systems. In addition, 

future research could look to examine the use of terminology in incident reporting systems 

with health and safety managers to gather their understanding of what is being recorded 

and design a uniform manner of reporting escalator incidents helping to identify potentially 

contributory PIFs in an incident as opposed to just looking for the most overt cause 

(Dodshon & Hassall, 2017). This could be extended to explore the use of the HFIT-MP in 

other incidents involving members of the public such as stair way falls to assess its 

applicability outside the escalator domain. 

A further alternative avenue would be to use the HFIT-MP on video-based data. A 

video-based analysis of escalator incidents would have the benefit of using objective 

recordings of escalator incidents and may provide additional data that can be used to assess 

how an incident has happened, reducing the need to code factors as ‘unknown’ (Malterud, 

2001; Rossignol, 2015). This point is emphasised in work by Ruibal et al. (2015), whose case 

study of the ‘Transports Metropolitans De Barcelona’ highlighted that most escalator 

incidents (N = 1879) were due to falls (49%) or slips (39%, which can also be regarded as a 

fall) on treads/pallets/belts or on landings, yet no causal factors are attributed to these falls 

Moreover, Robinovitch et al. (2013) highlighted the lack of available objective evidence to 

understand why falls occur in care homes. These authors examined incident footage of 

persons falling in care homes, using a previously designed taxonomy (Yang et al., 2013), 

allowing them to identify incorrect weight shifting as the most frequent cause of falling with 

more incidents during standing and transferring compared to when residents were walking. 

This approach applied to escalator incidents could provide more clarity as to what is 

happening and where on the escalator, which may include behaviours that do not lead to an 131 

incident, and in turn could be used to categorise any ‘unsafe’ behaviours (Cocca et al., 

2016). 

4.5.8 Conclusion 

Previous escalator incident research has focused on the use of severe incident data 

sets such as hospital admissions (O’Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et al., 2013) or incidents 

severe enough to be reported to a governmental health and safety regulator (Chapter 3), 

which may underestimate injuries and mask patterns of behaviour leading to less severe 

injuries (DeMers et al., 2011). Comparable to severe escalator incidents, falls were found to 

be the most frequent type of escalator incident. In congruence with Chapter 3, falls were 

associated with a combination of PIFs rather than one main attributing cause. This current 

study provides greater insight for health and safety practitioners when designing 

interventions to reduce escalator-related injuries. Furthermore, the successful use of the 

HFIT-MP on these data and strong reliability scores for most factors demonstrates its 

applicability to further escalator-injury data sources, other than the severe injury data used 

to develop it. However, further work needs to be done to reduce ‘unknown’ responses 

within the data before potentially using the HFIT-MP as a basis for escalator incident 

reporting. Future research should consider observational analysis to overcome the lack of 

objectivity and variability in detail of narrative incident reports. 132 

FOREWORD TO CHAPTER 5 

The results of Chapters 3 and 4 highlight the utility of the Human Factors Incident 

Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) to identify and explore performance 

influencing factors (PIFs) in both severe and less severe escalator incident reports. The use 

of the less severe data set provided additional PIFs for the HFIT-MP due to the increased 

richness of the data contained within the reports. However as highlighted in Chapter 2, 

these narrative incidents reports are known for their inconsistencies as demonstrated by 

high proportions of unknowns within both data sets of Chapters 3 and 4. To counter this, 

Chapter 5 will adopt an observational video coding approach of escalator users to examine 

potential escalator incidents and near-misses. The observational approach provides an 

objective data set not subject to the biases of those reporting or recording the incident. A 

near-miss is an event that has the potential to lead to an incident and is overlooked in 

current literature, with focus instead on when things went wrong (i.e., a Safety-I ontological 

approach). Subsequently, this Chapter incorporates a Safety-II ontological perspective (i.e., 

examining when things go right) as no actual harm has occurred. Moreover, this approach 

allows for the review of escalator use to produce a baseline of successful use, currently 

missing from academic literature. 133 

CHAPTER: 5 VIDEO CAPTURE TO IDENTIFY THE RISK FACTORS RELATING TO ESCALATOR 

INJURIES: A MULTI-SITE OBSERVATIONAL STUDY 

5.1 Abstract 

Background: 

Examination of falls and accidents during escalator use have heavily relied on the 

analysis of narrative incident reports to explore the causes of incidents. Incident report data 

can be ambiguous or potentially missing or mis-interpreted pieces of information, even 

within the same organisation. I aimed to address this limitation be analysing video footage 

escalator use in natural settings using the Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members 

of the Public (HFIT-MP), to better understand the factors associated with near misses. 

Methods: 

2 to 4 cameras were custom mounted and set up to ensure a sufficient field of view 

of the escalator run up and off, at 4 different organisations in the UK. Each camera recorded 

48h of video footage for subsequent analysis. Following a feasibility study to determine a 

sampling strategy for coding, 65 x 15-minute segments were randomly selected for coding. 

A near miss was defined as overt changes in postural stability that could have led to a fall. 

The subsequent analysis included the use of pairwise comparisons, robust Poisson regres-

sion and Multiple Correspondence Analysis (MCA) to explore the predictors of near misses. 

Results: 

I captured video recording of 4,716 escalator users’ journeys. Findings indicated that, 

irrespective of locale, there were 151 near misses, providing an estimated near miss rate of 

3.2% (95% confidence interval [CI] 2.1-7.1%) of escalator journeys. There were no observed 

incidents. Subsequent analysis using Robust Poisson regression suggested a hesitant person 

was six times more likely to have a near miss than someone who was not hesitant (Preva-

lence Ratio [PR]=6.12, 95%CI=4.08-9.18). Playing on the escalator (PR=5.40), having mobility 134 

issues (PR=2.20), no unexpected movement on an escalator (i.e., step on then remain sta-

tionary) (PR=2.19), being distracted (PR=2.09) and not using the handrail (PR=2.07), were 

also significantly associated with a near miss. Age was significantly associated with near 

misses showing a U-shape trend, with those aged <10 or 75+ having over twice the near 

misses of those aged 25-30 years. 

Conclusion: 

This study suggests that causes of escalator near misses are multifaceted, and the 

analysis provides a valuable observation for typical escalator passenger behaviour that does 

not lead to an incident. Hesitation appeared to be a key predictor of a near miss, future 

work should look to explore the reasons for hesitation in escalator users and how hesitation 

relates to incident occurrence and thus successful escalator use. 

5.2 Introduction 

The prevention of falls in elderly adults has been highlighted as a public health prior-

ity (Robinovitch et al., 2013), not least because of the unintentional injuries (e.g., hip and 

wrist fractures: Jaglal et al., 2005) that can occur in elderly people following a fall. In re-

sponse to such priorities, literature has begun to introduce new methods aimed at examin-

ing the settings (e.g., long-term residential care: Robinovitch et al., 2013) and causes (e.g., 

location of impact: Komisar et al., 2022), of how and why falls occur in older adults. Obser-

vational coding of video footage represents one method that has received particular atten-

tion in contemporary literature and has begun to reveal the mechanisms that lead to falls in 

elderly adults (for an overview of methodological approaches, see Woolrych et al., 2015). In 

contrast to reliance on interviews/incident reports (Johnson et al., 2016; Timsina et al. 2017) 135 

or the use of laboratory-based simulations with healthy adults (Moreira et al., 2018; Schiff-

man et al., 2006) the observational study of behaviour from video can generate objective 

evidence to understand why falls occur (Robinovitch et al., 2013). 

In a seminal study, Robinovitch et al. (2013) provided evidence of the mechanisms of 

falls following the observational study of video capture in a long-term care environment. 

Robinovitch and colleagues reported that the most common causes of falls in elderly adults 

were due to inadequate weight shifting and tripping, whilst the most common activities re-

sulting in falls were walking, standing quietly, and sitting down. Comparable to the long-

term care environment, a further public setting that has been found to be the location of 

falls impacting upon elderly adults is escalators in retail and travel environments (Chi et al., 

2006; Page & Hough, 1989). For example, Chapter 3 reported that 93% of the 403 escalator 

incidents reported to Great Britain’s 4 Health and Safety Executive (HSE) between January 

2015 and January 2016 were due to a fall. Similarly, Chapter 4 identified that 84% of 1,966 

less severe (i.e., not requiring hospitalisation) incidents recorded between November 2013 

and June 2017 from six organisations representing retail and travel sectors in United King-

dom (UK) were as a consequence of a fall. 

In the investigation of severe and less severe escalator incidents, Chapters 3 and 4 

developed and refined the HFIT-MP, which is an investigative taxonomy that can act as the 

basis for investigating the environments and causes of injuries. The HFIT-MP utilised multi-

ple correspondence analysis (Panagiotakos & Pitsavos, 2004), which enabled Chapters 3 and 

4 to demonstrate that falling in both severe and less severe escalator incidents were due to 

a combination of performance influencing factors (PIFs). For example, in the case of severe  

> 4The Health and Safety Executive only regulates, England, Scotland, and Wales. Norther Ireland has its own
> regulator.

136 

incidents, combined PIFs include people 65 or over, who lost their footing getting onto the 

descending escalator, not making any unexpected movements, and travelling alone rather 

than one factor (e.g., not holding the handrail) acting in isolation (Chi et al., 2006; Xing et al., 

2019). Despite generating new understanding on escalator incidents, the works in Chapters 

3 and 4 were based on the analysis of narrative incident report data, which are known to 

lack objectivity (Woodcock, 2008), leading to inconsistent reporting practices (Mitchell et 

al., 2015) and a high proportion of unknown PIFs. Therefore, the challenges encountered in 

the analysis of incident report data in retail and transport settings resonates with the wider 

falls literature, including the previously described literature in residential care environments 

(e.g., Robinovitch et al., 2013; Woolrych et al., 2015). 

In a first effort to move beyond narrative incident report data of escalators, Ayres 

(2019) coded video footage of 100 escalator injuries that were posted to social media. These 

authors identified PIFs that contributed to the incident before comparing them with 100 dif-

ferent National Electronic Injury Surveillance System escalator incident reports. In so doing, 

Ayres argued the video data provides greater detail to the incident not observed in the nar-

rative report. For example, video footage showed 11 falling incidents resulting from some-

one lying down across horizontal handrails going in opposite directions to cause the person 

to rotate. Yet, this was not a factor identified in the reviewed incident reports. Further, 

Ayres (2019) did not consider there to be a multifactorial component to incidents, rather 

they, attributed one PIF to an incident; nor did they adopt a rigorous coding criterion to cap-

ture the mechanisms of incidents. Thus, to advance current understanding in the literature 

on falls, it would be prudent to adopt a more comprehensive coding method that considers 

incidents to be multifactorial such as the HFIT-MP developed previously in Chapters 3 and 4. 137 

There have been notable advances in the study of falls in both care home environ-

ments and public spaces such as retail and transport settings, leading to the utilisation of 

novel video capture methods (Robinovitch et al., 2013) and investigative taxonomies (Chap-

ter 3). However, one significant consideration that has currently been overlooked in the falls 

literature is the study of both near misses and observed incidents. A near miss can be de-

fined as an event that had the potential to lead to an incident occurring (McSween & Mo-

ran, 2017). Importantly, in the safety literature, research has shown that high numbers of 

lower severity incidents (i.e., near misses) can lead to higher numbers of fatalities (Moore et 

al. 2020; Yorio & Moore, 2018). Therefore, by exploring near misses, it would be possible to 

identify PIFs that have the potential to lead to a fall or escalator specific incident (e.g., en-

trapment) occurring, which in turn can aid safety professionals to manage the risk of escala-

tor use before an incident occurs (Gnoni & Saleh, 2017; Gnoni et al., 2022). Indeed, the pro-

posal to examine near misses that may lead to an incident in tandem with exploration of in-

cidents, is in-keeping with contemporary perspectives in the safety literature (Hollnagel, 

2008). 

In summary, escalator injuries have the potential for life changing consequences with 

falls being the most common type of escalator incident (Chapter 3 and 4; McCann, 2013). 

The cause of escalator incidents are multi-factorial (Chapter 3 and 4); however, current es-

calator research is frequently based on analysis of narrative incidents reports, which can of-

ten present scientists with incomplete data (Rossignol, 2015; Woodcock, 2008). Prior re-

search suggests the use of video footage of accidents and falls may be beneficial to identify-

ing PIFs leading to an escalator incident (Ayres, 2019; Robinovitch et al., 2013). Indeed, it 

would be prudent to explore escalator near misses, recognising escalator incidents whilst 

severe are not common occurrences. The present research therefore has two distinct aims: 138 

(i) to use the HFIT-MP developed in Chapter 3, to identify and explore the PIFs that may lead 

to an escalator incident or ‘near miss’; and (ii) to assess the applicability of the HFIT-MP to 

data obtained via video capture as opposed to narrative incident reports. 

To achieve these aims, a unique and extensive multi-site sample of 4,716 users were 

coded, representing the largest study of its kind to date. First, an initial feasibility study was 

conducted based on 3 hours of footage taken from two of the participating organisations to 

develop and refine the use of the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3) on the video capture data, and to in-

form the amount of video footage that would need to be reviewed in the full analysis. To 

ensure reliability, an independent coder was trained on the use of the HFIT-MP before cod-

ing a segment of the final sample data, which was compared against my coding using Co-

hen’s Kappa. Subsequent stepwise regressions and multiple correspondence analysis were 

then used to aid the interpretation of the observational findings and inform new under-

standing on near-miss incidents that contribute to falls in public settings. 

5.3 Method 

5.3.1 Procedure 

Following institutional ethical approval (Reference: SFEC 2018-066B) ( Section 9.2 

Appendix 2 ) organisations from an escalator incident steering group, chaired by the Health 

and Safety Executive (HSE), were approached for their permission to film on their respective 

premises. The steering group consisted of six members representing four retail (shops, retail 

outlets) and two travel organisations/bodies (rail and aviation) within the UK. Four of the six 

organisations took part in the study: two from the retail sector and two from the travel 

sector. Escalators were identified by each respective organisation with locations chosen due 

to historically high levels of recorded incidents. 139 

5.3.2 Observational Analysis of Video Footage Using the HFIT-MP 

Comparable to extant methods examining walking behaviours of members of the 

public (Gallup, Chong et al., 2012; Gallup, Hale et al., 2012), portable cameras were covertly 

set up, one at the top and one at the bottom of an escalator, which would allow a field of 

view that ensured an escalator user’s full journey was captured (McCarney et al., 2007). The 

cameras used were GoPro 5s, set at a recording rate of 25 frames per second. The cameras 

were mounted next to existing CCTV cameras using ceiling mounts with additional battery 

power supplies for long periods of recording. Cameras recorded over an 8-hour period (the 

maximum length of the cameras’ memory cards). In total this provided the researchers with 

48 hours of escalator footage to review. All escalators were in working order (as checked by 

premises’ operators) and all were operating at 0.5 m/s. For two of the organisations, sepa-

rate cameras had to be set up to record the ascending and descending escalators as these 

were separated by a staircase. Further, for one of the organisations it was only possible to 

record a double (i.e., side by side) descending escalator. In addition, varying safety features 

were in place across the respective organisations that participated in the study (Table 5.1). 

Figure 5.1 provides an example of the safety alterations taken from different escalators as 

not to identify the organisations used in this study.

> Organisations’ Escalator Safety Alterations

> only markings.

(A ) (B) 

Previous work conducted in Chapters 3 and 4 has introduced a validated human fac-

tors coding criterion, the HFIT-MP (Figure 5.2), which allows researchers to code escalator 

incidents in terms of four overarching themes: (1) ‘ Individual’ factors (e.g., age or sex); (2) 

‘Task’ factors (e.g., getting on or off the escalator or holding the handrail); (3) ‘ Environmen-

tal’ factors (e.g., ascending or descending or if the escalator was operating correctly); and 

(4) ‘ Outcome’ factors (e.g., type of incident or treatment received). 

> Figure 5.2:
> Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP)

141 

The behaviours of all escalator users irrespective of whether they had an incident or 

not were coded using the HFIT-MP to provide an understanding of what the typical behav-

iours of escalator users look like (Ayres, 2019) and allow the researcher to contrast PIFs of 

those who have an incident and those who do not. If an incident was observed, the person’s 

actions were coded up until the point that the incident happened. This enabled examination 

of the antecedent PIFs leading to the incident, informing the investigation, and thus leading 

to potential proactive intervention strategies (McSween & Moran, 2017). 

5.3.3 Feasibility Study 

A feasibility study was conducted to investigate the time it would take to code the 

video footage, informing sampling strategy for the full study. The feasibility study was also 

used to refine the HFIT-MP for the use on video data. One hundred and twenty minutes of 

footage from one organisation and sixty minutes from another organisation were coded us-

ing the HFIT-MP (Chapters 3 and 4). The researchers, Philip Beards (PB) and Robert Shaw 

(RS) coded 643 escalator users in these three hours, an average of one user every 17 sec-

onds, coding 26 of the 29 factors identified in Chapters 3 and 4. It was not possible to code 

the Outcome factor ‘Type of Incident’ as no incidents (i.e., fall, entrapment, cut/graze) were 

observed during the feasibility study. Thus, it was apparent that even if incidents occurred in 

the full video footage study, the incident rate would likely be low. Consequently, it was de-

cided that the focus of coding would be on exploring factors associated with near misses re-

garding falls on escalators, as falling incidents appear to be the most numerous (Chapters 3 

and 4; McCann, 2013). A falling near miss was defined as overt changes in postural stability 

that could be indicative of a fall (Muhammad et al., 2010) and as such, a new Outcome 

factor “incident classification” (near miss or no near miss) was introduced. For example, a 142 

person using the escalator may stumble getting on as demonstrated by a sudden grasping 

for the handrail or a sudden if not erratic adjustment of foot placement to regain balance. In 

total, 21 near misses were observed in the feasibility study, providing an average near miss 

rate of 3.3% (95% confidence interval [CI] 2.0-4.9%). 

The Environmental factor “Escalator location” was combined with the Task factor 

“Getting on or off the escalator” as the video footage provided more detailed information to 

record at the point an escalator near miss occurred. Specifically, a new Environmental fac-

tor, “Journey stage” encompassing: getting on, transition 1 (change from flat surface to 

staircase), transition 2 (change from staircase to flat surface) and getting off. As summarised 

in Figure 5.3, five new factors were identified during the feasibility study: one new ‘Task’ 

factor coded how long an individual held onto the handrail – that is, did they hold the hand-

rail for the ‘full journey’ or only part of the journey; and Four ‘Environment’ factors were 

added to code for the ‘number of flat steps on the escalator before transition 1’, ‘the colour 

of the comb plate’, ‘the level of demarcation on the escalator’ and if the person was ob-

served at ‘peak or off-peak time periods’ (this information was provided by the organisa-

tion). Furthermore, the use of video meant that factors such as: age, mobility issues, health 

conditions, intoxication and hesitation were limited to observer judgment as there was no 

means to ask persons or review an associated report. Observers (PB and RS) estimated age 

by any distinguishing characteristics (e.g. older appearance), mobility issues were estimated 

by any observable walking aid, health conditions were estimated by any observable charac-

teristics (i.e., carrying of a medical item such as a colostomy bag), intoxication was esti-

mated if a user was carrying and drinking any open alcohol, and hesitation was character-

ised by long pauses and/or tentative stepping when boarding the escalator. To add a level of 143 

robustness to the data and reduce any observer bias when coding (Cocca et al.,2016) an in-

dependent coder not involved in the feasibility work was asked to code a proportion of the 

full study. 144 

Figure 5.3: 

Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) Factor and Category Breakdown for Observational Data. 

Note. * and bold indicates a new factor for this data set. 145 

A sample size calculation was undertaken to inform the amount of the 48 hours of 

video footage that should be coded for the full study. The calculation was based on how 

many escalator users would need to be coded, assuming a near-miss rate of 5% (the upper 

limit from the feasibility study). The calculation suggested a sample size of 47 people from 

each site would produce a two-sided 95% confidence interval with width equal to 15 per-

centage points (Hintze, 2014). To aid coding and to gain a rigorous representation of the full 

footage, the video was divided into 15-minute periods that would contain multiple people. 

The sample size was therefore adjusted for this cluster sample, using the design effect 

(Hintze, 2014) assuming that people used the escalators at a rate of 72.5 people per hour 

(the lowest usage rate observed in the feasibility study) and that the potential clustering of 

behaviour by time-period would be low, with an intraclass correlation coefficient of 0.05. 

The adjustment suggested that a minimum sample size of 75-minutes (5 x 15-minute peri-

ods) from each site would estimate a 5% near miss rate (for each site) with the width of the 

95% confidence equal to 15 percentage points. The 15-minute time periods were randomly 

sampled to prevent bias. This was done for each site buy using a random number generator 

in Microsoft Excel, assigning each 15-minute segment a number, and then sorting all seg-

ments into ascending order, taking the first five segments as a minimum. Due to some sites 

having a smaller number of users than initially anticipated from the feasibility study, a total 

of 675 minutes of footage were coded. Moreover, due to Organisation 3 having a large gap 

between the ascending and descending escalator, recordings of the respective escalators 

were performed separately due to the angle required. Thus, in this case, a higher amount of 

footage was required to code both ascending and descending users (Table 5.2). 146         

> Table 5.2
> Amount of Escalator Footage Coded and Number of Users
> Amount of Footage

5.3.4 Inter-rater Reliability of the HFIT-MP 

Interrater reliability of the coding of escalator video footage using the HFIT-MP was 

estimated using Cohen’s Kappa. Kappa values ≤0 indicate no agreement, 0.01-0.20 indicate 

none to slight, 0.21-0.40 indicate fair, 0.41-0.60 indicate moderate, 0.61-0.80 indicate sub-

stantial, and 0.81-1.00 indicate almost perfect agreement (McHugh, 2012). An independent 

coder not involved in the review of the data was given a one-hour training session using un-

used clips . Training followed the process recommended by Goodell et al. (2016) where the 

independent coder sat with PB and RS whilst they demonstrated how coding was to be done 

using the HFIT-MP. The independent coder was then given one 15-minute clip, already 

coded by the lead researcher, and asked to code this using the HFIT-MP. Disagreements were 

discussed, where it was felt agreement could not be met the result was retained and Cohen’s Kappa 

analysis conducted using PB scoring and that of the independent coder. 

5.3.5 Analysis of Escalator use and Near Misses 

All statistical analyses were undertaken using Stata V16.0 statistical software. Fre-

quency distributions were used to summarise the occurrence of factors within the footage. 

Crude associations between factors and Incident classification (i.e., near miss or non-near 

miss) were tested using Fisher’s exact test or the Chi-squared test (if assumptions were not 

violated). Poisson regression with robust standard errors was used to investigate the HFIT-

MP factors associated with the prevalence of near misses among escalator users (Zou, 

2004). Specifically, since a clustered sampling strategy (i.e., randomly selected 15-minute 147 

time periods) was used to sample video footage for coding, cluster robust standard errors 

were used to consider potential intragroup correlation for individuals within the same time 

period. This was an exploratory analysis; as such, a forward stepwise procedure was used to 

select factors for inclusion to the final multivariable model with the p value set at 0.10, so as 

not to exclude any potentially influential factors. Seven of the HFIT-MP factors: 1) Loss of 

footing; 2) Loss of balance; 3) Journey stage; 4) Collision with another user; 5) Collision with 

object; 6) Carried item caught; and 7) Clothing caught, were not included as they could not 

be coded for non-near misses. For example, a person who had lost their footing (i.e., a stum-

bling of feet) was always coded as having a near miss. As the ‘Age’ category only had ten 

persons judged to be younger than five and only three persons judged to older than 85, 

were combined into the next relevant age group for analysis purposes. 

To control for potential confounding variables, the ‘Organisation’ factor was always 

included in the Poisson regression. ‘Health issues’ were excluded as there was only one case 

where a person appeared to have a health issue, and this could lead to spurious results. 

‘Placement of any step markings’ and ‘Number of flat steps before the transitions’, were 

also removed because of collinearity with the ‘Organisation’ factor. 

MCA was used as an exploratory analysis method (Panagiotakos & Pitsavos, 2004) to 

examine the relationships within and between factors among all escalator users (see Chap-

ter 3). All factors of the HFIT-MP were included in the MCA apart from those seven factors 

that could not be coded for non-near misses. Additionally, the HFIT-MP factors: 1) Intoxica-

tion, 2) Escalator in normal operation, 3) Lift in normal operation, 4) Treatment received and 

5) Emergency stop pressed were excluded as these only had one response category (i.e., Yes 

or No) and MCA is not able to compute these due to lack of variation. Furthermore, the 148 

‘Outcome’ factor – ‘Incident Classification’ was excluded as I wished to explore the popula-

tion of escalator users as a whole. To determine the number of dimensions for the MCA, the 

total inertia (variance) for each dimension was examined with dimensions accounting for 

10% or more of the variance retained. Agglomerative hierarchical complete-linkage cluster 

analysis was used on the dimensional coordinates of the HFIT-MP factor categories to group 

them together (Costantino et al., 2016), and the Duda-Hart Index was consulted to help de-

cide how many groups should be created (Everitt & Rabe-Hesketh, 2006). The strength of 

association for factor categories within the same group was assessed using the Euclidean 

distance between the origin and the centroid of the group, which was turned into a score 

from 1 (weakest association) to 4 (strongest association). 

5.4 Results 

In total, 45, 15-minute video segments were coded across the four sites. Keeping 

with the sample size calculation, a minimum of five 15-minutes recordings were sampled 

from each organisation, although additional coding time was available to allow the re-

searcher to code more than the required minimum of five segments as suggested by the 

sampling strategy. Organisation 1 had 6 segments coded, Organisation 2 had 10 segments 

coded; Organisation 3 had 20 segments coded and Organisation 4 had 9 segments coded 

(Table 5.2). 

5.4.1 Inter-rater reliability 

Of the 33 factors, it was not possible to estimate Kappa scores for 19 of them due to 

both researchers responding the same way (i.e., no variation) (Table 5.3). For example, no 

persons with mobility issues were observed in the footage by either coder, and so Kappa 149 

cannot be estimated as there are too few rating categories. Of the 14 factors where Kappa 

could be estimated, sex scored perfect agreement with a Kappa value of 1 (95% CI 0.726-

1.274) and twelve factors scored Kappa values of between .646 and .921, indicating substan-

tial to almost perfect levels of agreement. One factor, losing balance, had an estimated 

Kappa of zero even though it achieved a percentage agreement of 98%. This was because 

one researcher always coded this factor as ‘no’ but the second researcher coded one person 

as ‘yes’, and so this one disagreement had a disproportionate effect on Kappa. Although dis-

cussed, the second researcher felt strongly that what was observed was a loss of balance 

and so the result was retained.
