5.4.2 Descriptive Data 

5.4.2.1 Outcome Factors 

Of the 4716 users observed using an escalator, 3.2% ( N = 151, 95% CI 2.1-7.1%) had a 

near miss which was almost identical to the 3.3% ( N = 21, 95% CI 2.0-4.9%) observed in the 

feasibility study ( Section 5.4.3 ). As there were no falls, entrapments or cuts/grazes ob-

served, there was no need to code for the type of treatment provided and the emergency 

stop was never pressed. 151 

5.4.2.2 Individual Factors 

Examining all observed escalator users ( N = 4,716) the most frequent users were esti-

mated to be between the ages of 25-34 (23.3%), and there was an almost equal split of male 

and female users (50.9% vs 49.1%, respectively). All users were coded as showing no signs of 

intoxication (100%) and most appeared to have no identifiable mobility (98.5%) or physical 

health issues (99.9%), and for most there was no hesitation when using the escalator 

(98.6%). 

Pairwise comparisons between users who had a near miss ( N = 151) and those who 

did not ( N = 4565), highlight a statistically significant difference ( p<0.05) in regard to: age, 

mobility issues, health issues and hesitation. Exploring the proportion of observations be-

tween those who had a near miss and those who did not. Near misses were higher in those 

aged 6-10 or 75-84, those with mobility issues, those with health issues, and those who hesi-

tated using the escalator. Although not significant, there was an indication that females had 

a slightly higher number of near misses (Table 5.4).

5.4.2.3 Task Factors 

Examination of all observed escalator users (N = 4716) revealed that not all factors of 

the HFIT-MP could code for non-near misses, as highlighted in Table 5.5. The most frequent 

observed tasks that could be coded for both a near miss and non-near miss included: not 

making unexpected movements (57.7%), not boarding or facing the wrong direction of esca-

lator travel (99.9% & 94.6%, respectively), using the handrail (58.4%) but not for the full 

journey (64.1%), travelling alone (77.4%), carrying at least one item (50.4%), not playing on 

the escalator (99.8%) nor distracted (64.5%). In respect of those seven categories that could 

only be coded for a near miss, most near misses ( N = 151) involved: a loss of footing (N = 

132, 87.4%) or occurred when getting on to the escalator (N = 75, 49.7%) (Table 5.5). 

Pairwise comparison between users who had a near miss and those who did not, 

highlighted a statistically significant difference ( p<0.05) in regard to: making unexpected 

movements on the escalator, use of the handrail, traveling with companions, playing on the 

escalator, and distracted when using the escalator. Exploring the proportions of observa-

tions, near misses were higher in those who did not make unexpected movements (i.e., they 153 

remained stationary), those who did not use the handrail, those who travelled with compan-

ions, those playing on the escalator and those distracted when using the escalator. 154

5.4.2.4 Environmental Factors 

Examining all observed escalator users ( N = 4716) the most frequent environmental cat-

egories observed were (Table 5.6): use of the descending escalator (59.8%), escalators in 

normal operation (100%), lift in operation (100%), no barriers used on escalator en-

trances/exits (72.9%), the use of nose only markings on escalator steps (74.5%), 3 flat steps 

before the escalator transitioned into a stair case (57.4%), the use of a yellow comb plate 

(89.6%), and most users were traveling at peak time periods (75.9%). Pairwise comparisons 

between users who had a near miss and those who did not revealed a statistically significant 

difference (p<0.05) in regard to: the placement of markings and the number of flat steps be-

fore the transition. Exploring the proportion of observations, near misses were higher for 

those using an escalator with nose only markings or 3 flat steps before the transition (see 

Table 5.6).

5.4.3 Stepwise Poisson Regression 

Table 5.7 shows the final robust Poisson regression model for the HFIT-MP factors 

that were statistically significantly associated with a near miss whilst controlling for organi-

sational differences to account for any unforeseen confounding factors. The forward step-

wise selection method resulted in seven factors from the HFIT-MP plus the covariate ‘Organ-

isation’ included in the final model (Table 5.7). Both the deviance and Pearson goodness-of-

fit tests were not statistically significant (both p>0.99), and so there was no evidence against 

the final robust Poisson model. 

The factors in the final regression model suggest that those hesitating, those playing 

on escalators, those not using the handrail, those not making unexpected movements, 

those distracted using the escalator, and those with mobility issues were statistically more 

likely to have a near miss ( p < .05). Age was also statistically significantly, associated with 

near misses showing a U-shape relationship; those at younger (i.e., < 10 years) and older 

ages (i.e., 65+ years) tended to have a higher prevalence of near misses than others. 158

5.4.4 Multiple Correspondence Analysis and Hierarchical Clustering 

MCA was used to explore the positive associations across factors of the HFIT-MP. Us-

ing the scree plot from the MCA, it was decided that a two-dimensional model would be ad-

equate as the first dimension accounted for 39.94% of the total inertia (variance), the sec-

ond dimension accounted for 20.97% and subsequent dimensions after that accounted for 

less than 10% of the total inertia (Julia et al., 2016). Figure 5.4a shows a dendrogram result-

ing from the hierarchical cluster analysis. To identify the best group solution, the Duda-Hart 

Index (Table 5.8) was inspected. A 10-group solution had one of the highest Je(2)/Je(1) val-

ues and a low pseudo-T-squared value followed by a much larger increase in the pseudo-T-

squared value (Everitt & Rabe-Hesketh, 2006) (Figure 5.4b), suggesting this was a reasona-

ble number of groups.

cluster analysis, ordered according to the strength of association. For example, having de-

marcation lines on the nose and around the step, and having a red comb plate were factors 161 

that occurred together frequently, and this was a strong association (Group 3). This associa-

tion was because only one organisation used a red comb plate and this style of step mark-

ings. Being hesitant and getting an item of clothing caught (Group 2) occurred frequently to-

gether, but this was a slightly weaker association compared to Group 3 and Group 10. 

The regression analysis identified factors that were independently associated with a 

near miss. Therefore, results of the regression analysis can help to identify groups of partic-

ular interest when considering near misses. Hesitancy had the largest prevalence ratio (Ta-

ble 5.7), and the results of the MCA suggested that hesitancy occurred most frequently with 

those getting an item of clothing caught (Group 2, Figure 5.5). The next highest prevalence 

ratio was for playing on the escalator (Table 5.7). The results of the MCA (Group 5, Figure 

5.5) suggested that playing on the escalator occurred more frequently with persons using 

the descending escalator, losing their footing, and using the escalator at peak time. The 

third, fourth and fifth highest prevalence ratios were for persons aged less than 10, persons 

aged over 75 and persons with mobility issues (Table 5.7). In the MCA, all three occurred 

more frequently together in addition to those boarding in the wrong direction, using the 

handrail for the full journey, traveling with companions, not carrying any items and the es-

calator having two flat steps before the transition (Group 1, Figure 5.5). The sixth highest 

prevalence ratio was not making unexpected movements (Table 5.7). This factor category 

occurred most frequently with the largest group of factor categories, but there was weak 

association between the factor categories in this group (Group 4, Figure 5.5). The final sig-

nificant prevalence ratios not already mentioned occurred were not using the handrail and 

being distracted (Table 5.7). Both occurred more frequently together in addition with, those 

aged 18-34, making unexpected movements (e.g., walking, reaching for fallen item, turning 

around on escalator), not using the handrail for the full journey, the escalator having only 162 

nose markings and the escalator having three flat steps before the transition in the MCA 

(Group 5, Figure 5.5). 163

5.5 Discussion 

Escalator injuries have the potential for life changing consequences with falls being 

the most common type of escalator incident reported in extant literature (Chapters 3 and 

4). Whilst the cause of escalator incidents are known to be multi-factorial (Chapters 3 and 

4), current understanding is largely limited to the analysis of narrative incidents reports. To 

advance current understanding, the present study analysed of video footage of near misses 

to identify the PIFs leading to an escalator incident (Ayres, 2019; Robinovitch et al., 2013). 

To advance current understanding, the present study analysed video footage of near misses 

to identify the PIFs leading to an escalator incident (Ayres, 2019; Robinovitch et al., 2013). 

The current research suggests that advancing understanding on escalator near misses can 

enhance knowledge of the PIFs leading to escalator incidents (i.e., falls, entrapments). Such 

findings will be discussed further in the remainder of the Discussion alongside consideration 

of the application of the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3) and the strengths and limitations in analysing 

the observational data used. 

5.5.1 Escalator use and Near Misses 

The key finding of this study is that of the 4,716 persons observed using an escalator, 

there were no observable injuries but a 3% near miss rate (i.e., the potential for fall on an 

escalator). This indicates that, whilst escalator incidents can lead to fatalities (McCann, 

2013), severe trauma (Chapter 3; Schminke et al., 2012) or minor injuries (Chapter 4), a high 

proportion of people use an escalator without an adverse event. However, field-based mod-

elling of escalator capacities suggests that escalators in railway stations can handle around 

105 persons a minute at peak times, and escalators in shopping centres handle around 92 165 

persons a minute at peak times (Bodendorf et al., 2014). Based on the results from the cur-

rent study, this could equate to around three persons per minute per escalator having a 

near miss. Whilst the current study data does not inform how many near misses would re-

sult in an escalator fall and subsequent injury, researchers on near miss reporting have 

stressed that the more near misses an organisation has the greater the likelihood of an inev-

itable incident occurring (Moore et al., 2020; Yorio & Moore, 2018). Therefore, by exploring 

those factors associated with escalator near misses it may be possible to prevent serious in-

juries occurring (Gnoni & Saleh, 2017; Gnoni et al., 2022; McSween & Moran, 2017). 

5.5.2 Factors Associated With Escalator Near Misses 

A hesitant person was found to be six times more likely to have a near miss than 

those who did not hesitate. The MCA suggested that hesitancy appeared most frequently 

with getting an item of clothing caught 5. One possible explanation for the strong association 

between hesitancy and near misses could be the location of near misses. Nearly 50% of near 

misses occurred when getting onto the escalator, where a person is presented with the 

challenge of stepping onto a moving platform (Hsu et al., 2015) accurately before the steps 

transition into stairs, which in turn may lead to hesitation. Although no escalator literature 

currently focuses on escalator use and hesitancy, gait and postural research has indicated 

hesitancy in older people can lead to more errors in stepping (St George et al., 2007), which 

in turn could impact a person’s gait, with gait variations linked to falls in older persons (Ron-

thal, 2019). Therefore, as it is those over 65 who are often reported in escalator fall injuries  

> 5A caveat of this is that there was just one person observed who had an item of clothing caught while using
> the escalator, and so this result should be interpreted with care.

166 

(Chapter 3; Lee et al., 2010), this suggests understanding the role of hesitancy in escalator 

use (embarkation and alighting) could be key to preventing future injuries. 

A person playing on the escalator was found to be five times more likely to have a 

near miss. The MCA suggested that playing on the escalator most frequently occurred with a 

person who lost their footing, using the descending escalator and there being no barrier at 

the entrance/exit of the escalator. One possible explanation for this grouping of factors 

could be that use of the descending escalator can lead to a feeling of disorientation (Cohn & 

Lasley, 1990), which could hinder a person’s sensory abilities used to maintain a stable cen-

tre of mass (Bruijn & Van Dieën, 2018) that combined with playing on the escalator could 

lead to a loss of footing and hence a near miss. This would suggest that Health and Safety 

professionals concerned with persons playing on the escalators focus on the descending es-

calator as a potential hotspot for such activity. However, as only nine persons were ob-

served playing on the escalator, caution is advised with this interpretation. 

If someone was less than 10 or over 75 years of age, they were around twice as likely 

to have a near miss as those aged 25 to 34. For those less than 10 years of age, previous in-

jury research is inconsistent with one paper suggesting high reportable incidents for this age 

group (Chi et al., 2006) but others suggesting lower frequencies of incidents (Xing et al., 

2019; Howland et al., 2012). Yet, as highlighted, those over 65 more frequently appear in es-

calator injury reports (Chapters 3 and 4; Lee et al., 2010). Often this is attributed to impair-

ments in mobility due to age-related physiological changes (Ambrose et al., 2013). The final 

regression model suggested, however, that age was independently associated with near 

misses, even when mobility issues were considered. This suggests that there are age-related 

factors that are not observable from video footage that may also impact the potential for 167 

near misses – for example, non-obvious visual problems or balance issues. The MCA sug-

gested that these two significant predictors (age and mobility issues) occurred more fre-

quently together as well as with: boarding in the wrong direction; not carrying any items; 

using the handrail for the full journey; travelling with companions; and using an escalator 

with two flat steps before transition. One possible explanation could be that the escalator 

only having two flat steps presents a limited window to accurately embark an escalator be-

fore the step separates and this task is demanding for those with mobility or age-related is-

sues. Yet, using the handrail (previously identified as a safety feature for stairs: (Maki et al., 

1998; Nemire et al., 2016) for the full escalator journey and not carrying any items (shown 

to impact of a person’s static and dynamic stability, see Roberts et al., 2018) could act bene-

ficially to offset the potential for a near miss to translate into a falling incident . Yet, caution 

is required as only one organisation had two flat steps and further research is needed to as-

certain the impact of different escalator designs on embarkation. Further it is unclear how 

traveling with a companion may influence the above scenario and there were just four in-

stances of a person boarding in the wrong direction making any such any inferences diffi-

cult. 

Not moving more than is to be expected when using an escalator (i.e., the action of 

stepping onto the escalator) meant that someone was twice as likely to have a near miss. 

Since most near misses (72%) occurred when embarking or at the first transition, a possible 

explanation is that, as coding ceased after a near miss was observed, only those factors 

leading up to the near miss were coded (McSween & Moran, 2017). This means that the in-

dividual would not have had the chance to make any unexpected movements on the escala-

tor, such as running or walking up/down the escalator steps as the issue occurred during 

this embarkation phase. Examining the results of the MCA, not making an unexpected 168 

movement occurred frequently with the largest number of HFIT-MP factor categories, albeit 

the strength of association was very weak making interpretation of these results difficult. 

Therefore, future research may need to focus on the ‘getting on’ phase of escalators to ex-

plore the biomechanics of escalator approach and embarkation to better understand what 

leads to the higher number of near misses at this phase. 

The final significant predictors not mentioned indicated if a person was not using the 

handrail or was distracted when using the escalator, they were twice as likely to have a near 

miss. The MCA suggested that these two predictors occurred frequently together among es-

calator users, together with: persons aged 18-34 years; making unexpected movements on 

an escalator (e.g., walking, retrieving a fallen item or turning around on the escalator); using 

an escalator with nose only markings and using an escalator with three flat steps before the 

transition. A possible interpretation of the results is that a younger person may feel more 

capable of moving beyond what is expected (e.g., walking) when using the escalator and so 

are more easily distracted. This, combined with a younger person feeling less of a need to 

use the handrail to maintain balance (Reeves et al., 2008) and the fact that escalator stairs 

are not of safe dimensions for walking (British Standards Institute, 2017; Jacobs, 2016), 

could mean they are more inclined to have a near miss. In addition, the demarcation lines 

on the moving tread plate and the number of flat steps may impact this relationship further, 

yet no current research examines the effect of demarcation lines and number of flat tread 

plates on escalator use. 

In summary, the results of the regression analysis and MCA show that whilst some 

factors may independently predict a near miss it is somewhat unlikely that such factors 

would occur in isolation, and so these factors can have a cumulative effect on the potential 

for a near miss to occur. Recent work by Jiao et al. (2022) shows the potential benefits of 169 

implanting a real time escalator monitoring system that can detect trigger PIFs that can lead 

to an injury resulting in an alarm being sounded and/or the stopping of the escalator to pre-

vent further injury. It is possible that the PIFs identified in this research can be used collabo-

ratively with such technology to warn health and safety professionals of impending injuries 

or perhaps to work symbiotically with the escalator by making adaptations to improve their 

journey (e.g., slowing down treadplates). However, as such research is not available, this is 

an area of potential review. 

5.5.3 Use of HFIT-MP on Escalator Recordings and Future Directions 

The use of the HFIT-MP is warranted as escalator incidents have been argued to be 

the result of several factors in tandem (Chapters 3 and 4) as opposed to one main causal 

factor (Chi et al.,2006; Xing et al., 2019; Xing et al., 2020), a stance supported in the current 

study’s results. This study identified six new factors that were included into the HFIT-MP be-

cause of new information not previously recorded through narrative incident report analysis 

(Chapters 3 and 4), thus highlighting the benefits of video data as found by Ayres (2019). 

The inclusion of these new factors did not alter the structure of the HFIT-MP from the four 

overarching themes (i.e., Individual, Task, Environment and Outcome) and the high Kappa 

results suggest a moderate to perfect reliability for the coding of the factor categories. Fur-

thermore, unlike previous research (Chapters 3 and 4) there were no ‘unknowns’ as using 

video footage meant each of the factors could be coded as either present or not based on 

what was observed (Ayres, 2019; Cocca et al., 2016). A prime illustration of this is hesitancy, 

where variability and ambiguity in narrative reports meant hesitancy could score either very 

high Kappa (Chapter 3) or very low (Chapters 4). Whereas in this study, the use of the objective 

> video data meant a clear decision could be made (supported by the high Kappa) if a person was or

170  

> was not hesitant using the escalator. However, caution is still warranted as these observations re-
> quire subjective interpretations backed up with a Cohen’s Kappa analysis to ensure consistency.

For 

example, a person’s age was estimated by coders. Whilst there was a high Kappa (.747) indi-

cating substantial agreement between coders, such an estimation approach is limited by re-

search assumptions. For example, research suggests age estimation is difficult for humans 

(Abousaleh et al., 2016) and computer-based analysis of faces may have been introduced as 

a means to overcome this issue (Hadid & Pietkainen, 2013). Such software was not available 

to the research team but could be applied in future work.The HFIT-MP was developed using 

escalator incident reports (Chapter 3). This study used the HFIT-MP more widely and applied 

it to all escalator users, whether they had a near miss or not. While this provided useful in-

sights into typical escalator use , it did present some challenges. Specifically, not all factor 

categories of the HFIT-MP were useable to code a person who had no observable incident or 

did not have a near miss. The fact that no incidents were observed in this study suggests 

that the methodology of coding everyday escalator usage is an inefficient way to observe 

escalator incidents. If the interest is in escalator incidents specifically, then it is recom-

mended that a similar approach to Robinovitch et al. (2013) could be taken in the future, 

where cameras are left in situ and footage of incidents is passed to the research team. How-

ever, just focusing on incidents alone can miss behaviours that may not have led to an inci-

dent in that instance but may have the potential to contribute to incidents (McSween & Mo-

ran, 2017). As such, analysing information on near misses – i.e., events that have the poten-

tial to lead to a falling incident – presents a novel approach for escalator injury research and 

provides a unique observation for how escalators are used when no near miss or incident is 

recorded (i.e., ‘a typical behaviour’). 

5.6 Conclusion 171 

This study indicates that escalator incidents, although serious in nature, may not be 

that prevalent in everyday use. Nevertheless, this research highlights that near misses occur 

frequently and can provide useful insights to help incident prevention. Hesitation appeared 

to be a key predictor of a near miss, with a person over six times more likely to have a near 

miss if they hesitate when using an escalator and future works should look to explore the 

causes of hesitation and how hesitation impacts incidents and how this relates to persons 

over 65 being more frequent in escalator incident reports. 172 

FOREWORD TO CHAPTER 6 

The results of Chapters 3 and 4 identified that the most frequent persons involved in 

escalator incidents are those over the age of 65. However, examination of incident reports 

(Chapter 3 and 4) and observational review of escalator users (Chapter 5) using the HFIT-MP 

do not make clear why specifically this age group has an increased risk of an escalator fall, 

nor what can be done to mitigate such incidents. To address this area of understanding, 

Chapter 6 utilised a qualitative method in line with the adopted mixed methods approach of 

the thesis. In so doing it encapsulates a Safety-III ontological perspective to proactively 

explore both safe and unsafe behaviours that may lead to an escalator incident occurring. 

Interviews are conducted with 25 persons over the age of 65 regarding their experiences of 

escalator use and potential causes of escalator injury. Reflexive thematic analysis is used on 

this data to construct themes to convey meaning as to the challenges and possible solutions 

faced by this age group when using escalators. 173 

CHAPTER: 6 A QUALITATIVE STUDY EXPLORING VIEWS AND PERCEPTIONS OF 

ESCALATOR USERS OVER THE AGE OF 65. 

6.1 Abstract 

Although common to most urban environments, there have been various reports of 

serious, and in some cases, fatal injuries to people from using escalators. Previous research 

has indicated that escalator injuries are typically the result of several contributory factors 

working in combination, with age having been identified as an important risk factor. Even 

so, it is still not clear why those over 65 years of age are more frequently recorded in 

escalator incidents reports. A semi-structured interview approach was used to explore 

attitudes toward using escalators, perceptions regarding the potential causes of incidents 

and injuries, and possible solutions with a sample of twenty-five escalator users over the 

age of 65. Data were analysed using reflexive Thematic Analysis (TA). Five key themes were 

developed: 1) The escalator user: Hesitant vs Effortless; 2) Escalators provide prominent and 

convenient use; 3) Escalators are safe to use but going down can be disconcerting; 4) 

Accurate foot placement prevents injuries; and 5) Improving escalator safety not as easy as 

it seems . Possible practical implications discussed include: 1) redesign of the environment 

to make escalators less prominent, including the use of messages to promote lift/stair use 

and the removal of distractions from escalator entrances; 2) adaptation of safety signs to 

promote risk perception, 3) physical adaptations to the escalator including reduction of 

speed or step sizes or possible substitution for travellators, and 4) the promotion of physical 

activities among older persons to reduce hesitation. This study provides an insight into 

escalator injury risk factors and possible solutions that have previously been unidentified in 

to extant research. 174 

6.2 Introduction 

Escalators are a common feature in most shopping centres and airports (DeMers et 

al., 2011; Schminke et al., 2013) and are used as means of convenient transportation 

between floors (Bodendorf, et al., 2014) with an estimated 3.5 billion global escalator 

journeys daily (Elevatori, 2018). Despite widespread adoption, past research has shown 

incidents can occur on escalators. Chapter 3 identified 403 non-fatal escalator injuries 

severe enough to be reported to Great Britain’s Health and Safety Executive (HSE) in a 1-

year period (2015 – 2016). In a follow-up paper, Chapter 4 explored less severe incidents, 

identifying 1966 minor (i.e., non-hospitalisation) escalator incidents between November 

2013 and June 2017 within six organisations in the UK. In the UK, no central repository for 

recording annual escalator injury statistics currently exists. However, in the US around 2,500 

- 6,000 non-fatal injuries are reported annually in hospitals (O'Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et 

al., 2013). Although small in number, such incidents can lead to hospitalisation (Chapter 3; 

O'Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et al., 2013;), severe lacerations (Collins, 1969) and/or 

fractures (Murphy & Moore, 1992) with 39 escalator-related deaths recorded over a 13-year 

period (1997 – 2010) in the US (McCann, 2013). 

Falls are the most frequently reported type of escalator incident, with between 77-

93% of incidents resulting from a fall (Chapters 3 and 4; Chi et al., 2006; O'Neil et al., 2008; 

Schminke et al., 2013; Xing et al., 2019). Previous aetiological research has often only sought 

to identify one main cause of escalator falls (e.g., not holding the handrail: Chi et al., 2006; 

Xing et al., 2019) or highlight the most pertinent risk factors contributing to an incident (e.g., 

those over 65 and female: O’Neil et al., 2008; Schminke et al., 2013). However, there has 

been a recent move to explore escalator incidents as multifactorial (Chapters 3 and 4; Wang 

et al., 2022). 175 

This thesis has so far developed (Chapter 3) and refined (Chapters 4 and 5) the 

Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP) organising 

Performance Influencing Factors (PIFs) (HSE, 1999) into four key themes: 1) Individual (i.e., 

age, gender); 2) Task (e.g., holding the handrail, moving on the escalator); 3) Environment 

(e.g., direction of escalator); and 4) Outcome factors (e.g., Type of incident) (Figure 6.1). 

> Figure 6.1:
> Human Factors Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public (HFIT-MP)

I successfully applied the HFIT-MP to escalator incidents that resulted in 

hospitalisation of the injured members of the public (Chapter 3), escalator incidents that did 

not result in hospitalisation but may have warranted first aid and/or incident report writing 

(Chapter 4) and falling near misses on escalators (Chapter 5). Using a Multiple 

Correspondence Analysis with hierarchical cluster analysis and biplots to aid interpretation 

these collective works found that escalator incidents are multifaceted involving multiple 

PIFs. For example, in severe falling incidents, those aged 65 and over, not traveling with 

companions, and getting onto the descending escalator were PIFs that occurred more 

frequently together (Chapter 3). 176 

Further to the research Chapters described in this thesis, wider escalator injury 

literature also suggests a trend that persons over 65 years of age are involved in over 50% of 

escalator injury incident reports (Chi et al., 2006; Howland et al., 2012; Page & Hough, 

1989). Older adults are also twice as likely to have a near miss on an escalator even though 

they only account for less than 10% of users (Chapter 5). Falls risk increases with age (Etman 

et al., 2012), with changes in cognitive and physical capabilities likely increasing the risk of a 

fall when using an escalator (Jacobs, 2016; Kenny et al., 2017; Schepers et al., 2017). 

However, the aetiology of escalator falls cannot be answered by looking at injury reports 

alone (Hanson et al., 2011). 

To address shortcomings in extant literature, the current study adopts a qualitative 

approach to explore why older users in particular may be at risk of a fall on an escalator. A 

qualitative approach enables an exploration of people’s experiences and perceptions, which 

are not observable through research techniques based on incident reports or videos (Ritchie 

& Lewis, 2003; Castleberry & Nolen, 2018). Previously Hanks (1996) explored perceptions 

and attitudes towards escalators and elevators across eight focus groups with 127 

participants aged 50-98. Hanks highlighted concerns on using escalators and lifts, in 

particular: 1) getting caught or stuck; 2) getting on or off; 3) trouble operating the 

equipment; 4) actions of other riders; 5) equipment not operating safely; and 6) what to do 

in an emergency. Whilst useful, the research by Hanks (1996) is limited as it does not 

explore, in depth, the specific issues older escalator-users may have. For example, using a 

more in-depth qualitative approach, Stave and Törner (2007) looked at causes of accidents 

in the Swedish food industry and Debnath et al. (2015) examined road construction and 

maintenance workers’ perceptions of hazards. Both studies highlighted the value of an in-177 

depth interview approach in providing an insight from a person’s perspective as to the 

reasons why an incident may happen and in informing potential interventions. 

The present study utilises an interview-based methodology to explore potential 

issues with escalator use in some depth. Building on the findings from the preceding 

research Chapters in this thesis, this study will examine why those over the age of 65 may 

be at increased risk of falls on escalators and the perceived risk factors. In doing so, the aim 

is to enable the identification of preventative strategies. The findings of the current study 

will therefore further current understanding of the suitability of the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3) to 

adequately represent the multifactorial PIFs that may contribute to a fall (or incident) when 

older adults use an escalator. 

6.3 Method 

6.3.1 Participants 

The main criteria for participation in the study was that a person had to be 65 years 

or older and to have had experience of using an escalator. Initial discussions with the 

research team and discussions with older person groups indicated it would be difficult to 

recruit only those who had fallen on an escalator and there would be benefit from speaking 

with those who had not had an incident as this would allow for potential comparisons to be 

made. For example, a recent qualitative interview study by Ellmers et al (2022) 

demonstrated how anxiety about falls can develop in people who have experienced a fall 

themselves or those merely aware of others that have, this in turn can lead to adaptive fall 

prevention behaviours such as greater focus on foot placement. Zecevic et al. (2006) 

demonstrated how public perspectives of the causes of falls differ from that of healthcare 178 

professionals and published research and it is therefore important to consider public 

perceptions when formulating preventive strategies for escalator falls. 

A purposive sampling approach was adopted whereby individuals were contacted to 

take part in the study if they met the criteria that were the focus of this study (Patton, 

2002). An internet search of local older persons’ social groups within the North Derbyshire, 

South Yorkshire, and Hampshire region was conducted and an email was sent explaining the 

outline of the research, along with a participant information sheet and a request for 

assistance from the group. In addition to this, the University of Portsmouth Ageing Network 

was approached to help facilitate contact with older persons. To aid with recruitment, a 

snowballing approach was also used whereby participants were also provided with a flyer to 

promote the research in their respective groups. Participants were invited to share this 

research with people they thought would be interested to take part asking them to contact 

the lead author directly. 

In total twenty-five interviewees were recruited (8 males; 17 females), with 

individuals aged 66 to 90 years old (mean = 73.6; SD = 7.3). Interviews were on average 28 

minutes long (range 14 to 57 minutes). Interviewees represented several UK regions; East 

Midlands ( N = 5), London ( N = 7), North East ( N = 3), North West ( N = 3), Scotland ( N = 1), 

South East ( N = 2) and South West ( N = 4). The majority ( N = 16) of those interviewed 

reported to use escalators between once a week or every day. A smaller number of 

participants said they used escalators approximately once a month ( N = 8), whilst one 

person said they would never use them now due to preference but had used them in the 

past. Of those interviewed, five indicated they had received injuries whilst using an 

escalator. Three of these injuries were due to falls. One person reported they fell when 

getting onto the escalator as they had been fatigued from traveling and had not placed their 179 

luggage securely on the escalator step. The luggage fell and the person fell trying to retrieve 

it. The second fall was reported by the person to be due to having a series of falling spells; 

they reached the top of the ascending escalator and fell backwards but chose not to report 

the injury to anyone. The third person reported they were distracted when getting onto the 

escalator leading to them falling and spraining their wrist. When asked to elaborate the 

reason for distraction their response was that they were not paying attention and knew 

they should have been. The other two injuries were cuts sustained using the escalator, one 

person had cut their hand holding the handrail on a sharp bracket for the handrail, the other 

person had been walking up the steps and cut their leg on the sharp edges of the escalator 

step. 

This diversity with respect to the frequency of use, preferences, multiple UK 

locations of participants, and experiences of escalators, provided opportunities to obtain a 

rich dataset across a sample holding suitable information power to address the aim and 

analysis strategy of this study (Malterud et al., 2016), and facilitates the transferability of 

the findings (Nowell et al., 2017). Information power is a means to guide sample size in 

qualitative studies with a focus on five elements: (i) study aim – the aim to explore escalator 

use is relatively narrow in focus, which may lend itself to smaller sample sizes; (ii) sample 

specificity –older adults represent a diverse group of people, and therefore the sample 

needed to be large enough to accommodate the potential for differing views; (iii) use of 

established theory – as there is limited qualitative research into escalator experiences the 

sample size needed to be large enough to enhance information power; (iv) quality of 

dialogue – although a narrow focus was used it became apparent as interviews ranged from 

14 minutes to over an hour in duration, a larger sample size was needed to address the 

challenge for some participants to articulate escalator experiences; and (v) analysis strategy 180 

– as a cross case analysis was used to develop common themes across participants a larger 

sample size was required to enhance information power. 

6.3.2 Materials 

An interview schedule was developed to explore individuals’ attitudes and 

perceptions towards how and why they choose to use an escalator. The topics covered 

were: 1) experiences of using escalators; 2) awareness of escalator safety features; and 3) 

causes and prevention of escalator injuries. Participants who indicated that they had been 

involved in an escalator incident ( N = 5) were given an additional question. This additional 

question focused on what happened during the incident in greater detail to explore any 

potential risk factors not identified by previous research (Chapters 3 - 5). 

6.3.3 Procedure 

Prior to the interviews, ethical approval was obtained from the University of 

Portsmouth’s ethical committee [SFEC 2019-044] ( Section 9.3 Appendix 3 ). Twenty-five 

interviews were carried out between May to June 2019, at a time and location that was 

convenient for the individual (Heath et al., 2018). For fifteen participants, this involved 

meeting the lead author at a local coffee shop, and the other ten individuals wished to 

conduct the interviews over the phone. During the interview, participants were provided 

with an additional copy of the information sheet (posted to telephone participants), and 

they were asked to read and sign a consent form indicating that they were happy to take 

part (a prepaid envelope was provided for telephone participants). Before the interview, all 

participants were assigned an ID number and informed that this is how they would be 

referred to in the data analysis (i.e., that all information provided would be anonymous). 181 

Participants were informed that they were free to withdraw from the research at any point 

without giving a reason; they were also assured of the confidentiality of their responses. In 

addition, participants were asked if they consented for the interview to be audio recorded 

for the purpose of data analysis. 

6.3.4 Data Collection 

All interviews were recorded using an Olympus VN-712PC digital audio recorder. 

Each interview was conducted by the lead author, they were then transcribed verbatim 

using Microsoft Word. 

6.3.5 Research Philosophy, Data Analysis and Trustworthiness 

As discussed in Section 2.3.2 a pragmatic approach is adopted throughout this thesis. 

A pragmatic approach is about selecting the most appropriate method to examine and 

understand specific contexts (Giacobbi et al., 2005). As this chapter is about exploring a 

person’s experiences and perceptions of escalator use and injuries, adopting a Safety-II 

perspective, a qualitative interview methodology is used. Pragmatism is a philosophical 

tradition which recognises the contextual nature of scientific inquiry, which is influenced by 

current and past social, historical, and political conditions and requires reflection and 

analysis (Giacobbi et al., 2005). These tenets align with the constructivist epistemology, 

which underpins the current qualitative research. So, whilst pragmatism and constructivism 

are not the same, they are both compatible with the idea that meaningful knowledge can be 

obtained around escalator use by exploring a person’s perspectives of their own actions and 

experiences of interacting with an escalator. For a pragmatist, it is through dialogue and 

discussion/agreement in the scientific community that the truth of facts can be tested 182 

(Giacobbi et al., 2005). As such reflexive thematic analysis (TA) was used to understand 

attitudes and perceptions to escalator use in those over the age of 65 years. According to 

Braun and Clarke (2006; 2019; 2021; 2022), reflexive TA provides the researcher with a 

flexible framework from which they are to immerse themselves in the data, think through, 

and reflect on what is being said to construct themes from their analysis, fitting with this 

study’s research philosophy. 

To ensure trustworthiness in the analysis, Braun, and Clarke’s (2006; 2019; 2021; 

2022) six stages for reflexive TA are followed with Nowell et al. (2017) providing a means of 

establishing trustworthiness for each stage of Braun and Clarke’s six stage approach. Stage 

1) familiarising one’s self with the data – this was done by conducting every aspect of the 

interviews myself, building rapport with participants and subsequently listening and 

transcribing each interview verbatim onto Microsoft Word, saving each as their own record. 

Initial thoughts were recorded in a notebook, and an Excel file was used to record possible 

themes. This process allowed me to become more familiar with participants’ experiences; 

Stage 2) generating initial codes – this was done by reading each interview repeatedly, 

producing codes as to anything that could be meaningful to escalator use/incidents (e.g., 

“foot placement”) and storing these onto the Excel file. Primarily these were semantic given 

the topic focus was escalator use/incidents. However, reflecting on the data allowed for the 

generation of latent codes such as a lack of perceived risk to escalator use; Stage 3) 

developing themes – codes were collated across all 25 interviews and organised to express 

commonality across opinions of escalator use/incidents within the Excel file. I then reflected 

on and returned to the data to ensure the themes being developed were grounded in what 

was being said using quotes to illustrate the produced theme, making notes in my notebook 183 

as a means to provide an audit trail to enhance trustworthiness of my analysis (Nowell et al., 

2017). 

Stage 4) reviewing themes and codes – this was an iterative process of reflecting, 

reviewing and refining overarching themes, mapping out any connections/overlaps between 

themes using a thematic map, and returning to the data for quotes that illustrated the 

constructed theme; Stage 5) defining and naming themes – this stage involved the 

refinement of themes, reflecting on links between themes, articulating these to other 

members of the research team and the wider shared research stakeholder group to further 

reflect and refine what could be defined as a theme. This allowed for critical oversight from 

fellow social researchers but also those who manage escalator safety, in turn this allowed 

for a credibility check of the themes, enhancing the trustworthiness of my analysis (Nowell, 

et al., 2017). This debriefing helped to challenge the constructed themes to ensure they 

conveyed meaning and were not too abstract for safety managers to understand and thus 

act upon. The construction of themes was a challenge as the topic of escalators was very 

specific, as such some of the themes may be seen as more of a domain summary rather than 

a traditional theme (Braun & Clarke, 2020) but still add to the overall understanding of 

escalator use and injuries. Finally, stage 6) producing the report – involved the writing up of 

each theme using multiple quotes from the data, and documenting my approach, thus 

producing this chapter for my thesis. This also gave me the opportunity to reflect on what 

was being said in line with existing escalator injury literature. Subsequently, presenting my 

themes to stakeholders of the wider escalator shared research program allowed me to 

understand the practical applications this analysis would have on escalator safety 

management. 184 

Overall, the above process was iterative with codes and themes being continuously 

reviewed and further refined. Themes were derived inductively (i.e., data driven) from 

the semantic concepts (i.e., explicit or surface meaning) directly communicated by 

participants, with latent (i.e., interpretative) concepts considered where possible (Braun & 

Clarke, 2022). 

It is also important to state that I as the interviewer, acting on behalf of the Health 

and Safety Executive may have influenced the responses given and the line of questioning 

used, given that I am used to investigating incidents, searching for performance influencing 

factors and breaches of legislation which may have led to an incident. This in turn may have 

influenced the themes constructed and thus is a consideration in the interpretation of the 

results generated. Yet the ability to reflect one’s own influence on the construction of 

themes is a core concept of reflexive TA (Braun & Clarke, 2019). To improve credibility, 

Nowell et al.’s (2017) guidance for the six-stage approach (Braun & Clarke, 2006; 2019; 

2021; 2022) requires the use of critical friends to challenge assumptions. In this study, I had 

already developed the HFIT-MP (Chapters 3, 4 and 5). Being mindful that my positionality 

could influence my analysis, the use of critical friends in step 5 provided the opportunity to 

challenge my assumptions and recognise new dimensions the past HFIT-MP had not 

identified, such as new PIFs. 

6.4 Results 

In total twenty-five interviews were conducted, transcribed, and analysed using the 

previous described process. Using reflexive TA, five key themes were produced encapsulat-

ing individuals’ views and experiences regarding the use of escalators and the potential fac-

tors which may contribute to escalator incidents among individuals over 65 years of age. 185 

The first theme, ‘The escalator user’ reflects that within the sample there appear to be two 

types of escalator user: the ‘hesitant user’ and the ‘effortless user’. The second theme, 

‘Prominent and convenient to use’ reflects that irrespective of the type of user, escalators 

were used as they were prominent in environments providing a convenient means of 

transport compared to lifts or stairs. The third and fourth themes cover user perceptions of 

escalator safety and their views regarding the potential causes of escalator injuries. The final 

theme captures participant views and suggestions on how escalator safety could be im-

proved. Figure 6.2 provides and illustration of the main themes and sub themes. 

Figure 6.2: 

Themes and Associated Sub themes 

6.4.1 The Escalator User: Hesitant vs Effortless 

The first theme constructed related to how participants approach escalator use: the 

Hesitant and the Effortless user. Hesitant users described some form of hesitation when 

embarking on an escalator. Exploring why this was the case, most participants described 

> The Escalator User: Hesitant vs Effortless
> Escalators Provide Prominent and Convenient use
> Escalators are Safe to use, but Going Down can be Disconcerting
> •Ageing influences accurate foot placement
> •Don't get distracted when placing your feet
> Accurate Foot Placement Prevents Injuries
> •Slow the escalator down
> •Make the steps deeper and wider for easier embarkation
> •Have more flat steps before the transition
> •Change escalators for travellators: no need to worry about transitions
> •Make escalator alternatives more prominent and frequent
> •Change users behaviour
> Improving Escalator Safety not as Easy as it Seems

186 

that hesitation resulted from identifying when to step onto the escalator, with an emphasis 

of hesitating whilst waiting for a fully flat step: 

ID1 [Line 93-96]: I do [hesitate] actually, yes. You’re just waiting for the line, I’ll wait for that 

one line, two lines, stride up four lines, so yes, slightly. 

Interviewer: What do you feel makes you hesitate? 

ID 1: Because if you were to just put your foot on the wrong bit you would literally be 

balancing on the edge of a step, wouldn’t you? 

For some Hesitant users, hesitation appears to be context specific. Contexts 

identified were: i) crowds blocking their view of the escalator step or handrail or ii) use of 

the descending escalator combined with underlying health problems such as difficulty in 

maintaining balance or poor eyesight making descending more of a challenge. 

ID 31 [Line 128-131] Going up is no problem. I just walk onto the escalator and zip up. But 

coming down I have to stand on top and get my balance correctly. 

All Hesitant users more frequently described themselves as either not active or not 

very active, with most saying their health was poor. All Hesitant users highlighted that they 

had a health issue of some degree (e.g., ‘ rheumatoid arthritis ’, ‘ osteoarthritis ‘, ‘ arthritis 

aches and pains ’, ‘ vertigo ’). Some discussed how their mobility was affected in some way 

because of health issues. The following extracts reflect the health status of this category 

best. 187 

ID 10 [Line 11]: Well, I’m restricted on quite a lot of things because I have rheumatoid 

arthritis. 

ID 24 [Line 14-16]: Not really, no [in response to how active they are]. I’m finally walking, 

because I’ve got an arthritic hip and a bad back. 

Conversely, Effortless user narratives indicated that they do not hesitate when using 

an escalator having no issues embarking, and that they find escalators easy to use. 

ID5 [Line 96-97]: No, I have no issues at all. I try to match up my sort of leading leg with the 

first step that I’m going to choose on the escalator. I have no problem at all. 

ID 25 [Line 115-124]: I find it easy. I find, to use the escalators, getting on and getting off, 

you have to have a second of concentration, when you put your foot on the escalator, and a 

second of concentration when you take your foot off. 

Effortless users more commonly described themselves as either being active or very 

active. However, like the Hesitant users, most indicated they had health issues to some 

degree, such as: ‘a weak back’, knee replacement, or osteoarthritis, for some this also 

meant their mobility was also affected as a result of these conditions. The following extracts 

best reflect the health status of effortless users. 

ID 22 [Line 11-13]: Yes, I'm healthy and I am energetic. I do a lot of gardening and walking. 

Although I don't have a bike, I have a static bike, so I do six kilometres a day on that. 188 

ID 27 [Line 22-32]: I’ve got lots of aches and pains but I’m very active. I bowl and I go to 

Zumba and I go to keep fit and I used to play badminton, but I finished that about six years 

ago. 

Of those five who reported sustaining an injury, most were Effortless users with one 

Hesitant user. Injuries included sustaining a cut to a leg from the escalator steps, a cut to 

the hand from the escalator handrail brackets and falls on the escalators due to fatigue or 

an existing balance issue. However, sustaining an injury was not reported by any of the 

participants to have impacted their use or intended use of escalators suggesting that for 

these participants, the benefits of using an escalator appear to have outweighed any 

potential or experienced risks. 

Additionally, participants were asked how often they used an escalator with both 

Effortless and Hesitant users averaging out to between 1 to 3 times a week a week. Walking 

on escalators was not frequent among either Hesitant or Effortless users. Handrail use was 

again similar with almost all Hesitant and Effortless users saying they would either use the 

handrail all the time or if they felt the need. 

6.4.2 Escalators Provide Prominent and Convenient use 

Both Hesitant and Effortless users discussed that escalators were easy to locate and 

convenient as a means of transport. This in turn appeared to increase the likelihood of using 

the escalator when navigating through an environment as opposed to looking for an 

alternative means of transport. 189 

ID 1 [Line 49-52]: It’s [escalator] usually the only way if you’re in the centre of the store. 

Finding the lift is often buried away in the walls of the building, isn’t it? For [STORE NAME], 

it’s like where is the lift? So I would use the escalator because it’s central. 

ID10 [Line 64-65]: Because nine times out of ten when you go into the stores the escalator is 

the thing you see. 

When asked about alternative means of transport such as stairs and lifts, these are 

not actively sought, thus emphasising the notion of the escalator as being convenient. Stairs 

were less readily identified as an alternative by both types of user with indications that they 

were not easy to find. Using a lift for most participants was not readily seen as a viable 

alternative means of transport. Some of the reasons provided related to the lift: i) being 

difficult to locate; ii) inducing a sense of claustrophobia; and iii) being slow in its operation, 

leading to frustration. However some users suggested that they generally had no issue with 

using lifts irrespective of them being slow or difficult to find. 

ID 14 [Line 74-77]: It’s just not that obvious where the lifts are. Yes, and in some places it’s 

quite concealed. They don’t encourage you to use the lift. 

ID 13 [Line 48-51] I can’t go in a lift. I’m claustrophobic. I can’t. I never go in a lift and I 

can’t get upstairs. I have to stop every two or three steps. So, they’re (escalators) handy for 

me. 

ID 22 [Line 204-205]: But to get the lift... and then there's a queue and you've got to wait for 

it, so that's a... I don't bother with the lift. 190 

6.4.3 Escalators are Safe to use, but Going Down can be Disconcerting 

There was a perception from both types of users that escalators are a safe means of 

transportation with descriptions emphasising an escalator to be part of daily life, thus 

potentially increasing the likelihood of it being used as a means of travel by users. 

ID 4 [Line 156-158]: I think escalators are so much part of your daily commute, your daily 

way to work, that people just sort of seem to think they’re perfectly safe. 

ID 13 [Line 226]: I just think, oh, I’d like to go upstairs and just get on and go up. I’ve never 

thought about it not being safe or anything like that. 

Responses seems to suggest that there might be an underlying complacency with 

escalator use. Specifically, most Hesitant and Effortless users did not feel that carrying an 

item on an escalator to be a risk; yet for both types of users there was an acknowledgement 

that carrying an item requires more concentration with regards to positioning the item on 

an escalator step. 

ID 26 [Line 153-155]: I don’t have any problem if it’s an item I’m carrying, because I'm used 

to all that. If it’s a wheelie, then I’ve mentioned to you that it can be difficult to get exactly 

the right position. 

ID 27 [Line 175-179]: I’m alright if I... like yesterday I had to get on it with my suitcase, it was 

only a little suitcase but just having to step on it and get your suitcase on it whether... do you 

put it on the step you’re on or do you put it on the step behind you? 191 

Moreover, both Hesitant and Effortless users described that they had used escalator 

handrails that were not in sync with the steps (i.e., traveling at differing speed). They further 

described how they would become unbalanced and needed to adjust by moving their hand 

/and or feet. Furthermore, individuals from both Hesitant and Effortless user types felt that 

the descending escalator was less safe compared to the ascending escalator. Participants 

described feeling ‘less secure’, and a fear of falling forwards. Despite these concerns 

participants were not dissuaded from opting to use escalators. There was however an 

acknowledgement by one person that an alternative would be sought for large items of 

shopping. 

6.4.4 Accurate Foot Placement Prevents Injuries 

The perceived cause of escalator injuries was explored with participants considering 

where and why escalator incidents happen; participants were shown Figure 6.2 to aid the 

visualisation of parts of the escalator. Most Hesitant and Effortless users perceived that 

injuries often occur when stepping onto an ascending or descending escalator. Some 

Hesitant and Effortless users felt that injuries could happen when getting either on or off an 

escalator. Both types of users discussed the importance of co-ordinated foot placement, 

ensuring that a user steps accurately onto a ‘full’ step, and not between two flat steps, 

before they transition into the stairs (points 2 or 4 on Figure 6.3). 

ID 9 [Line 415-420]: Well, missing the step. 

Interviewer: Okay. Is that this point one, this initial stepping onto it? 

ID 9: Yes, maybe. So your foot – I mean, this happens on fixed stairs – your foot is neither on 

one thing and then as they separate. It’s smooth and then as it separates, they fall. 192 

> Figure 6.3:
> Escalator Prompt

6.4.4.1 Aging influences Accurate Foot Placement 

Both Hesitant and Effortless users, felt that older adults were more likely to have an 

incident due to physiological-related changes, which could in turn impact on the ability to 

accurately step onto an escalator. Specifically, participants highlighted an increased frailty 

associated with advancing age and changes such as reduced response times, increased 

likelihood of losing balance, poor eyesight, and increased anxiety of using an escalator. One 

participant felt that the causes of increased frailty were the result of older adults having a 

more sedentary lifestyle, again impacting balance and response times. 

ID 24 [Line 214-218]: I think as you get older, you… speaking personally, yes I can feel, I’m 

aware, to be absolutely honest, that I’m more confused than I was when I was younger, 

about getting on an escalator. But then on the other hand, they’re brilliant, because I don’t 

want to go up the steps. 

ID 31 [Line 421-430]: Well I... yes I... I think it’s all physiological, I would think. You know, 

going with age and slowness of... of responses, response times to things. And, of course, 

balance problems too. That does affect the elderly quite a lot. 193 

6.4.4.2 Don’t get Distracted When Placing Your Feet. 

Effortless and Hesitant users indicated that distraction was the main cause of failing 

to accurately step onto an escalator. The causes of distraction, however, varied and 

included: 1) thinking about other tasks whilst using an escalator; 2) concerns over other 

passengers rushing past; 3) concerns over carrying luggage on the escalator occluding vision; 

4) user fatigue; and 5) use of mobile phones. One participant suggested that distraction is 

merely part of human nature. 

ID 25 [Line 331-334]: People, so often, in so many, you see it in farming, where there’s 

health and safety, and all that, and yet people will suddenly, “Oh, it doesn’t matter to me, 

come on, let’s get the job done,” not pay attention, and you have an accident. 

6.4.5 Improving Escalator Safety not as Easy as it Seems 

Participants made suggestions that related to changing the physical escalator, 

individual user behaviour and the wider environment. However, consensus was divergent 

with arguments for and against proposed changes. In short there was no one resounding 

change that all wished to be enacted that could improve escalator safety. Further a Hesitant 

and an Effortless user felt that it was not possible to make any changes to escalators to 

prevent injuries. 

ID 21 [Line 323-324]: I really don’t know, because, you know, you just get on it and... I can’t 

think what they could do in the way of improvements. 194 

6.4.5.1 Slow the Escalator Down 

Both Hesitant and Effortless users identified physical changes to an escalator that they 

thought would reduce the likelihood of an injury. There were mixed views towards the 

reduction in escalator speed, with some suggesting that reducing speed could prevent 

injuries with opposing views expressing that it would not improve safety. There was a view 

that a slower speed could lead to end-user frustration and that, ultimately, a balance needs 

to be struck. 

ID 6 [Line 354-356]: Between they’ve got to have a certain amount of speed to be effective, 

but you don’t want them going too fast otherwise they’re going to throw people off at the 

end. 

6.4.5.2 Make the Steps Deeper and Wider for Easier Embarkation. 

Another physical change identified was the need to increase the escalator step ‘depth’ (i.e., 

the distance between the front and back of an escalator step) to provide a larger target area 

to successfully step onto the escalator. 

ID 23 [Line 327-329]: I suppose in my mind there is an issue with the widths or the depths, yes, 

the depths of an escalator step. It seems quite, if it was a little bigger, it would be easier for 

me to get on and for me to keep, put, things on. 

Alternatively, there was a suggestion that increasing the escalator step width (i.e., 

the distance from the left to the right side of the step) would be beneficial in allowing 195 

individuals to stand side by side. A different participant highlighted that a narrower step 

may be better to prevent people from rushing past. 

6.4.5.3 Have More Flat Steps Before the Escalator Turns Into Stairs 

Two participants also suggested that increasing the number of flat steps before the 

escalator transitions into stairs may also have a beneficial impact. 

ID 12 [Line 804-810]: As I said earlier, I do like the ones that have the long run-in. Those are 

better. Because that is, whether you’re going up or down, it gives you more of a feeling that 

you can find where your feet are going; it doesn’t seem to be so much of a rush to get on those, 

so you’ve got more time. 

6.4.5.4 Change Escalators for Travellators: No Need to Worry About Transitions 

Throughout the course of the interviews most participants referred to a travellator. A 

travellator is a flat conveyer that, whilst similar to an escalator it does not transition into stairs 

but instead remains flat throughout. The most frequently described benefit of using a 

travellator is that getting onto one is deemed easier as the surface does not alter like an 

escalator. 

ID 22 [Line 349-355]: I haven't seen people looking so cautious getting on and off them as I 

have with escalators. 

Interviewer: What do you think makes people less cautious? What's your opinion on that? 196 

ID 22: Because it's still doing what you're doing anyway, you're still moving forward and 

there's nothing else thrown into the mix. They're not having to think that the steps are going 

away from me, the steps are going up and I'm going up or down. I think it's that perception. 

Other positive attributes of travellators that were mentioned across the interviews 

were: i) a person could walk up a travellator easier if they chose to; ii) a lack of disorientation 

using the descending travellators compared to a descending escalator; iii) travellators are 

wider and hence easier to use; iv) travellators are slower making them easier to embark on; 

and v) they were considered enjoyable to use. 

However an alternative view was that travellators were not such an attractive 

alternative means of transport; It was felt that the use of ‘trolleys’ used in supermarket 

contexts would make users avoid the travellator. Travellators were seen as slower, which for 

some was thought to be a benefit but others felt the slow speed would infuriate some people, 

thus leading them to walking on the travellator. Conversely others felt that travellators were 

too fast. One participant felt that travellators were more difficult to use as they would use 

the individual steps on an escalator as targets for embarkation, which they felt was not 

possible on a travellator. Another participant appeared to explore this notion further 

suggesting that a travellator encourages a person to get on with a trolley, which makes the 

action more of a challenge as there is more to think about getting themselves and the trolley 

onto the travellator. 

6.4.5.5 Make Escalator Alternatives More Prominent and Frequent 

A fifth sub-theme related to changing the wider escalator environment. Suggestions 

relating to this theme came from both Hesitant and Effortless users. The most frequently 197 

suggested change to the escalator’s environment was to have increased signage to 

alternative routes. 

ID 26 [Line 426-429]: Well that is something that could be done, so the signage of the 

alternative could be better. Or more obvious, I think. This is obviously a difficulty for 

organisations which have put lifts in later or who haven’t had very good architects, because 

they may be telling you you’ve got to go a very long way to a lift or not telling you terribly 

clearly where it is .

Other suggestions discussed across the interviews included: i) having escalators and 

lifts closer together; ii) members of staff available at the escalators to offer assistance to 

users; iii) the complete removal of all escalators ; iv) increasing the number of escalators 

could allow the inclusion of a ‘fast’ and ‘slow’ lanes; and v) changing the colour of safety 

signage on escalators to a more prominent colour, such as red, to convey potential risks. 

This latter response was interesting as not one of the 25 participants interviewed was able 

to identify the blue safety signs (i.e., mandatory instruction) that escalators are required to 

display according to escalator code (BS EN 115-1:2017 British Standards Institute, 2017). 

6.4.5.6 Change User’s Behaviour 

A sixth sub theme regarding promoting the use of escalators related to passenger 

behaviour. Both Effortless users and Hesitant users provided specific suggestions for 

changing people’s behaviour when using escalators. The most frequent suggestion was 

preventing other users from congregating at the entry/exit to the escalator due to 198 

distractions (e.g., talking to one another), possibly by using a clear/no standing zone, as this 

causes problems for other users trying to get on/off. 

ID 22 [Line 365-372]: Well I was thinking to give... to stop people from meeting their friends 

at the bottom and just starting to chat because they then don't think about the people still 

using the escalator. If you could make it a no standing zone around there. Yes, even without 

it turning into a bit of a travellator which would take up too much room but have it the same 

size but put those stripy yellow lines around it so you have to clear off after you get off it. 

Other participants suggested stopping users from rushing on the escalator past 

others and encouraging them to stand to one side to allow others to get past. Auditory 

announcements were also suggested to direct attention to the task of embarking onto the 

escalator. Other suggestions to change user behaviour included: i) nudges to get people to 

use the handrails; and ii) limiting what users can take onto the escalator, such as prams, 

which could get caught when getting off. 

6.5 Discussion 

The present study utilised an interview-based methodology to explore potential issues with 

escalator use with those over the age of 65 and subsequent identification of preventative 

strategies that could be implemented to reduce incidents. The research produced five main 

themes with several sub themes that offer an insight into the risk factors that may 

contribute to falls in persons over 65 years of age when using escalators. Building on earlier 

findings of this thesis, there are similarities with the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3) as the themes 

produced highlight: Individual (the types of escalator user: hesitant vs effortless); Task 199 

(accurate foot placement when stepping onto the escalator, carrying of items) and 

Environmental (the prominence and convenience of the escalator) PIFs (HSE, 1999) that 

appear to be influencing the selection and use of an escalator. The themes constructed as 

part of this analysis are explored in more detail in the proceeding section. 

6.5.1 Extrapolation of Constructed Themes 

The theme ‘The escalator user: Hesitant vs Effortless’, describes two distinct user 

approaches when stepping onto an escalator among those over 65: there are those who will 

hesitate before stepping onto the moving escalator steps, and those who will effortlessly get 

on with very little conscious or physical effort. In contrast, although identified by the HFIT-

MP (Chapter 3), hesitation was rarely reported as being a pertinent PIF in escalator incidents 

(Chapters 3 and 4). The observational analyses used in Chapter 5 highlighted that users who 

hesitate are over six times more likely to be involved in a falling near miss. However, the 

results of Chapter 5 did not provide insight on the causes of hesitation and the data from 

the current study suggest that hesitation may result from user’s focusing on the task of 

getting onto a full escalator step as they embark (see Hunt et al., 2023). Whilst this pause 

may feel beneficial to users to ensure they get onto a full step, hesitation in older persons 

has been identified as leading to more stepping errors (St George et al., 2007), which could 

potentially lead to an escalator fall. Additionally, ‘Hesitant’ users described themselves as 

not being very active, and often of poor health. This could mean that this group of users is 

predisposed to hesitation and might ultimately be at a higher risk of falling (Chapter 5). For 

example, low levels of physical activity in older adults has been shown to predict fall 

occurrence (Moreira et al., 2018). 200 

Irrespective of being a Hesitant or Effortless user, persons primarily chose the 

escalator due to its prominence and perceived convenience in the environment with little 

consideration given to alternative routes (the second theme). As such, this finding 

represents a PIF previously unreported in incident data (Chapters 3 and 4) or observational 

analysis (Chapter 5). Whilst the HFIT-MP considers if stairs or lifts are available as alternative 

means of transport (see Chapters 3 - 5), it does not consider the prominence and 

convenience of the escalator over these other transport options. As such, it could be that a 

new environmental PIF asks if the escalator was the first means of transportation a person 

would see when entering the environment and such modification could be included in 

future iterations of the HFIT-MP. 

The findings on the prominence and convenience of escalator placement are 

consistent with pedestrian flow research that has shown that the choice of escalators over 

stairs through transport stations (Zhang et al., 2015) and shopping centres (Zacharias & 

Tang, 2015) is dependent upon the distance (Zhang et al., 2015; Zacharias & Tang, 2015) and 

walking time between the two modes of transport (Zhang et al., 2015). Further, in previous 

research, delay (i.e., if one route was perceived to be faster) was identified as the main 

factor influencing choice (Zhang et al., 2015). This finding is consistent with comments made 

by participants in this study with the escalator being the first thing they would see and thus 

provide a faster means of travel than alternatives. An additional consideration is that 

according to wayfinding research, it has been suggested that older persons perform less 

effectively on real-world wayfinding tasks due to age-related decline in spatial and executive 

functioning (Taillade et al., 2016) and reduced physiological abilities (Zijlstra at al., 2016), 

resulting in slower movements through environments. Considering that an escalator takes 

up large amounts of floor space it becomes a very overt landmark towards which potential 201 

older users may gravitate to, instead of looking for an alternative means of transport 

(Withagen et al., 2012). 

The third theme ‘Escalators are safe to use but going down can be concerning’ 

indicated a low perception of risk associated with escalator use. Data in this theme 

suggested that even when some participants report they feel unsafe using the descending 

escalator, this would not stop them using the escalator. This low-risk perception may result 

from heuristics that are made when faced with a decision to use the escalator or not 

(Wickens et al., 2013). As very few participants had experience of being involved in an 

escalator incident, such occurrences are not salient (availability heuristic) and even those 

who did have such experiences blamed themselves for the incident. It could be inferred, 

therefore, that older persons choose an escalator as a result of conducting a cost-benefit 

analysis, where perhaps to maximise cognitive and physical capabilities, the escalator is 

selected because it offers a convenient means of transportation that is perceived to be low 

risk. 

The finding considered above suggests that it is possible that risk perception is an 

additional individual PIF to be included in future iterations of the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3-5). 

Although most persons indicated they would continue to use the escalator irrespective of a 

disconcerting feeling, it could be that this anxiety could in some way have an impact on a 

person’s ability to use an escalator. For example, Ellmers et al. (2022) highlighted that high 

levels of anxiety lead to over cautious gait or failure to plan stepping actions, which in turn 

may impact someone’s ability to successfully embark a moving surface (Hunt et al., 2023). 

The findings from the current chapter also suggest that the main perceived cause of 

escalator injuries was due to a person failing to step accurately on the moving escalator (the 

fourth theme). Previously, the HFIT-MP had identified failure to step accurately as a PIF in 202 

severe, less severe and near miss incidents (referred to as a loss of footing, Chapters 3-5). 

Further, this theme had several sub-themes associated that could impact foot placement 

that indicated that age-related decline (Hunt et al., 2023) and distraction were two reasons 

a person would fail to step accurately, both have previously been identified as PIFs in 

incidents and near-misses by the HFIT-MP (Chapters 3 – 5). Age-related physiological 

changes, such as reduced response times, poor balance and increased frailty are also risk 

factors highlighted in the wider falls’ literature (Graveson et al., 2016; Moreira et al., 2018). 

Distraction has previously been inferred in Chapter 5 to significantly influence the 

occurrence of a falling near miss (i.e., the potential to fall) when using an escalator although 

the cause of distraction was not explored in that study. The current study suggests that 

escalator user distraction may result from thinking about other tasks; other passenger 

behaviour; carrying of luggage on the escalator; fatigue or mobile phone use. Although 

additional distraction and escalator research is limited, sports literature examining penalty 

kicks and moving advertisements behind the goal line has also shown a direct negative 

effect of distraction on the performance of a penalty kick (Paterson et al., 2020). This would 

infer the need to reduce the potential for distraction at escalator entrances to ensure a 

person embarks accurately and ultimately, safely. 

The final theme constructed from the data centred on participants’ views as to 

possible changes that could be made to improve escalator safety; this had six sub-themes to 

encapsulate the most prominent suggestions. Six participants were unable to provide any 

suggested solutions, believing escalators to be as good as they can be. The themes regarding 

improving the escalator provide greater insight into potential mitigation strategies users felt 

could help to reduce injuries and is consistent with other safety literature that has 203 

previously highlighted the benefits of a qualitative approach (Debnath et al., 2015; Stave & 

Törner, 2007) 

The most common suggestion focused on the speed of escalators (the first sub-

theme). There were two opposing views, those that felt decreasing the speed of the target 

steps would make it easier to embark and as second view that reducing the speed would 

lead to frustration and possibly introduce new risk factors, such as walking on the escalator. 

Speed therefore could be considered as an additional environmental PIF that may influence 

an escalator incident occurring, as most escalator operators set their speed at 0.50m/s 

(Chapter 5; CIBSE, 2015) it could be assessed if others used a speed above or below this 

benchmark. Yet there is currently no research available which states what speed is best for 

embarkations meaning the influence of speed is an area of potential future research. 

Furthermore, according to BS EN 115-1:2017 (British Standards Institute, 2017) escalators 

are not designed to be walked on as they are not intended to be a staircase and thus do not 

meet step dimensions deemed safe for walking on stairs (Jacobs, 2016). Other suggestions 

included increasing the distance between the front and back steps of an escalator (i.e., the 

depth) or increasing the width of each step would provide a larger target area for someone 

to step on (the second sub-theme); these could form a potential new environmental PIF that 

could be included into the HFIT-MP. Typically, escalator steps are 0.38m deep (CIBSE, 2015) 

which could be used as the benchmark to assess those escalators with longer or shorter step 

depths. Yet future research is needed to assess the impact of this PIF on incidents occurring. 

Another view was that increasing the number of flat steps would mean a person does not 

need to be as concerned with the transition (the third sub-theme), a PIF already identified
