by the HFIT-MP (Chapter 5). Biomechanical studies of escalator embarkations are limited 

(Hsu et al., 2015; Hunt et al., 2023). However, Hunt et al. (2023) found that older adults 204 

(aged 60-81) are at greater risk of falls than younger adults (aged 18-40) when embarking 

moving surfaces such as escalator steps. The authors concluded that this was due to 

reduced action capabilities in older adults meaning they were unable to prospectively 

control actions when negotiating the moving surface when embarking compared to their 

younger counterparts. 

Another suggestion was for the introduction of travellators as alternatives to the 

escalator (the fourth sub-theme). Whilst this was not unanimous, resoundingly participants 

identified that on a travellator the specific targeting requirement is not needed, as the steps 

remain flat and thus do not pose a risk to users should they misplace their initial foot target. 

However, as locomotor research suggests, in changeable environments (which the moving 

escalator steps could be envisaged to be), the critical locomotor adjustments are made in 

the final phases of reaching a goal (Higuchi, 2013). Further research is required to explore 

any potential benefits of travellator use, as well as escalator speed changes and step size on 

embarkation, for which there is currently no available literature. The fifth sub-theme related 

to improving escalator safety was to ‘Make escalator alternatives more prominent and 

frequent’. Suggestions included improved signage to alternative routes and to convey safety 

messages. However, considering previous research by Zhang et al. (2015) and Zacharias and 

Tang (2015), as already discussed, signage alone may not be enough; additional elements 

were highlighted by users regarding redesigns/refurbishments of the environment to 

increase the number of, or reduce the distance to, alternatives and using staff to assist 

users. 

The final sub-theme related to the notion of changing escalator passengers’ 

behaviour. Participants identified the need to prevent escalator exits from being blocked by 

distracted users, prevent other users pushing past, or to use auditory announcements to 205 

focus attention at embarkation. The possible PIFs of ‘distraction’ and ‘collisions with other 

users’ have previously been identified using the HFIT-MP in escalator incidents (Chapters 3 

and 4) and near misses (Chapter 5). Locomotor research shows that for older adults, 

avoiding obstacles is more difficult compared to younger persons (Caetano et al., 2016). This 

suggests that blocked escalator exits or having to avoid other users may be more 

challenging for older age groups. The potential use of auditory announcements requires 

further research to explore the exact message presentation, duration, frequency, and pitch 

that would have the most benefit (Cabral & Remijn, 2019). 

6.5.2 Practical Implications 

The findings from this study have several practical implications for reducing the risk 

of having an escalator incident among older age groups. To reduce hesitation, existing fall 

prevention programmes could be enhanced by encouraging more physical activity (Ambrose 

et al., 2015, Wilkinson et al., 2018) potentially targeted escalator embarkations (Hunt et al., 

2023). Increased physical activityhas been linked to a person’s gait and walking patterns 

(Ronthal, 2019). This in turn may influence an older adult’s ability to successfully embark 

onto an escalator and reduce incidents. Such an approach should be considered as part of 

multifactorial interventions that are tailored to the individual’s risk factors (Montero-Odasso 

et al., 2022). 

Distraction from the task of stepping onto the escalator was suggested by some 

participants to be a potential factor in escalator incidents and thus injuries. One possible 

method to reduce distraction would be to remove anything such as advertisements from a 

person’s field of view when embarking on the escalator as they can distract one’s gaze away 

from the co-ordination task of getting onto a moving platform (Hsu et al., 2015; Hunt et al., 206 

2023). Similarly, the congregation of people at exits should be discouraged as this could 

cause a distraction to users disembarking. This could be achieved by using demarcation lines 

or auditory announcements as means to nudge behaviours (Sunstein, 2014), like road 

markings influencing driver speeds (Zhao et al., 2018). In addition, an interesting result was 

that participants often discussed that carrying an item required more concentration, yet 

they did not appear to consider this activity as possibly distracting. One possible solution 

could focus on limiting the number of items taken onto an escalator, which in turn could 

help prevent any possible issues with balance (Chapter 4; Schiffman et al., 2006). Further, BS 

EN 115-1:2017 (British Standards Institute, 2017) states that barriers can be used to prevent 

access of shopping trolleys and baggage carts and thus if installed could be beneficial to 

reducing incidents. 

Considering that escalators are selected as they are prominent and convenient, it 

could be suggested that a redesign of store/travel station environments with alternatives to 

escalators (i.e., lifts or stairs) placed closer to entrances (Zhang et al.,2015; Zacharias & 

Tang, 2015) to make them more prominent could encourage their use among older adults. 

However, negative perceptions about alternative methods of transport, such as using stairs 

or lifts, may hinder the benefits of any redesign. A possible solution would be to also include 

a behaviour change approach encouraging older adults to use the alternative means of 

transport. The escalator versus stairs decision context is often used in the health literature 

as a means of promoting healthier behaviours amongst passengers and to encourage stair 

use over escalators (Jennings et al., 2017). For example, Suri et al. (2014) used different 

signs that tap into heuristics (Wickens et al., 2013) and self-determination theory (Deci et 

al., 1985), the former theory recognising that some passengers will make quick decisions 

using limited resources (i.e., they are in a hurry), whereas the latter assumes that 207 

passengers will internally reflect on their choice and change their behaviours in light of this 

information in the long-term. The results of Suri and colleagues (2014) suggest that 

heuristic-based signs may be effective in the short-term to change behaviour but argue that 

they should be complemented with systematic communications to ensure a longer lasting 

effect on choice. In addition, Jennings et al.’s (2017) review of interventions to improve stair 

use highlights that any such signage requires a combination of images, text, time, and 

fitness-based motivational messages and a variety of signage sizes. Therefore, in the current 

scenario, to encourage users to take the stairs or lift, the benefits of doing so needs to 

outweigh the use of the escalator. Future research would also need to establish a cost 

benefit comparison of encouraging stair use over escalator use in terms of safety, with risk 

of stair falls in older adults well known (Jacobs, 2016) it could be that in comparison the 

escalator is a safer means of transport. 

The low-risk perception when using escalators is concerning as none of the 

participants interviewed could accurately identify the blue safety signs even though the 

escalator code (British Standards Institute, 2017) requires these as a mandatory safety 

feature to convey risk. The placement of such safety signage needs to be considered and the 

imagery used need to be examined to explore their meaning to users (Caffaro & Cavallo, 

2015, Liu & Ho, 2012). However, arguably, any signs used might need to be regularly 

updated and refreshed as Allais et al. (2017) highlighted that after a period of two weeks 

signage designed to encourage stair use became less effective, which they inferred was a 

result of passengers becoming used to them and no longer noticing them. 

To organise which intervention would be the most beneficial, governmental health 

and safety organisations promote the use of the hierarchy of controls (Health and Safety 

Executive, n.d.c; Occupational Safety and Health Administration, n.d.). The hierarchy is in 5 208 

stages, with the first being the most efficient means of risk control. Stage 1) Elimination – 

removing the hazard, in this instance it would be the removal of the escalator; 2) 

Substitution – changing the hazard; 3) Engineering controls – using equipment/ measures to 

isolate the person from the hazard; 4) Administrative controls – changing procedures, how a 

person interacts with a hazard; and 5) Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) – providing 

equipment to individuals that would reduce the impact of the hazard. Figure 6.4 organises 

the practical implications suggested by participants using the hierarchy of controls. 

Figure 6.4: 

Potential Adaptations to Improve Escalator Safety Organised by the Hierarchy of Controls.

Whilst not suggested by participants past research has indi-

cated that shoes with extended lips (Tatla, et al., 2001) or 

rubber clogs (Lim et al., 2010; Ong,et al., 2016) could lead 

to an entrapment incident. Therefore, consideration can be 

given to escalator users’ shoe types, loose clothing, or pro-

tective equipment such as hip protectors. 209 

6.5.3 Strengths and Limitations 

Previously, only one study (Hanks, 1996) has attempted to explore escalator safety 

from a qualitative methodology. This current study expands escalator injury literature, using 

interviews to go beyond the mining of statistical data sets (Chapters 3 - 5) exploring in 

greater detail the views of persons over 65 (the most frequently injured user group) regard-

ing escalator safety, contributing factors to incidents and how injuries may be prevented. 

Such an exploration is not possible using a purely quantitative method. Thus, the current 

study demonstrated the benefit of following proponents of a Safety-II approach (Hollnagel, 

2008) and adopting a qualitative method to add unique insights to the possible causes and 

mitigation strategies for escalator incidents (Creswell & Clark, 2017; Hanson et al., 2011). 

Further, the results help authenticate the overarching HFIT-MP structure as the themes cre-

ated emulated the Task, Individual and Environmental factors of the HFIT-MP with the addi-

tion of four PIFs resulting from the qualitative approach adopted. 

Throughout the interviews, it became apparent that some participants could not 

easily articulate their experiences of using escalators. Additionally, the terminology used by 

participants varied and additional clarification was sought by the researcher. This led to 

some interviews being relatively short (approximately 15 mins) while others were much 

longer (approximately 1 hour). For example, the step ‘depth’ (the distance from the front to 

the back of the step) and width of the step (the distance from side to side) would often be-

come confused by participants, and they referred to them using different terms (e.g., shoe 

size, depth, or width) and thus the researcher was required to ask participants to clarify 

what they meant. The image of the escalator helped individuals to focus and point at areas 210 

they felt were most at risk. A recommendation for future work would be to have several es-

calator pictures available for participants to help articulate their responses to questions in 

line with qualitative visual research approaches (Davison et al., 2015). 

6.5.4 Future Directions 

Current escalator research has focused on contributory factors leading to injuries 

(Chapters 3 and 4); maintenance (Bardyshev et al., 2017); modelling capacity of escalators 

(Kauffmann & Kikuchi, 2013) including trampling risks (Li et al., 2016), but has neglected to 

explore the impact of how a person steps onto the moving surface. The findings from this 

study suggest several directions for future research. First, although the current study has en-

abled the addition of four new PIFs for the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3): i) Individual: Person felt at 

risk using the escalator; ii) Environmental: If the escalator was the first means of transport 

iii) Environmental: Speed of the escalator being above/below or set at 0.5m/s, and iv) Envi-

ronmental: step depth being above below or at 0.38m, the influence of these on an incident 

is yet to substantiated. Therefore, future work could look to include these PIFs as part of a 

reporting system so that when an escalator incident occurs a uniform means to record inci-

dents is available (Wang et al., 2022). Inevitably, similar analyses could then be conducted 

on any incidents that occur akin to that used in Chapter 3 – 5 to assess the potential role 

these new PIFs may have on an incident forming. 

Further in-depth biomechanical work is required to explore embarkations. Although 

recent research by Hunt et al. (2023) demonstrates older adults have a greater potential to 

fall when embarking on to a moving surface that requires accuracy, such as the moving es-

calator surface, less is known about the impact of hesitancy, escalator speed, and step size 211 

on successful embarkations of moving surfaces. More research is required into different in-

terventions that could be used to help reduce escalator injuries. The layout of stores and the 

potential use of signage within stores to promote alternative means of transport are some 

promising areas where further research would be useful. 

6.5.5 Conclusion 

This research used a qualitative approach to expand upon the limited available litera-

ture that has examined the factors that contribute to falls on escalators among individuals 

over 65 years of age. By taking this approach, this research was able to identify risk factors 

that could not have been picked up when using quantitative data alone. The results showed 

two distinct types of escalator users: hesitant and effortless users. Compared to effortless 

users, hesitant users appear to have poorer levels of health and physical activity, and hesita-

tion has previously been linked to more escalator near misses. Both groups shared compara-

ble views on the reasons for selecting the escalator as a means of transport; namely that es-

calators are easy to locate, convenient and safe to use. Both types of users identified the 

main cause of incidents as a failure to step onto an escalator accurately and acknowledged 

that this could be harder for some individuals due to declining coordination abilities with 

age. Neither group appeared to be concerned that they may be susceptible to injuries when 

using an escalator. Several solutions that could potentially help target these risk factors are 

discussed. 212 

CHAPTER: 7 EPILOGUE 

7.1 Introduction 

This thesis presents a series of studies aimed at developing a human factors 

taxonomy for investigating incidents involving members of the public, using escalator 

contexts as an overriding focus. In this Epilogue Chapter, I will first provide a brief overview 

of the status of current knowledge at the outset of this body of work before outlining the 

key findings from each of the experimental chapters and how each chapter builds and 

refines the taxonomy for escalator incidents, acknowledging Safety-I (Read et al., 2021) and 

Safety-II (Hollnagle, 2008) ontological perspectives, whilst also highlighting how the findings 

from the thesis inform and advance knowledge about the causes and potential mitigation 

strategies for escalator incidents. Following this, literature and public safety management 

implications will be discussed, before concluding with considerations for future research. 

7.1.1 Synopsis of Safety in Public Spaces: Escalators 

Public spaces provide a means to promote physical activity (Dunton et al, 2009), 

wellbeing (Larson et al., 2016), social interaction, and economic benefits to human society 

(Goodsell, 2003). However, literature has also highlighted that some public spaces represent 

the location of accidents to people across the lifespan, with falls in particular appearing to 

be a common occurrence in public spaces for those over the age of 65 (Schepers et al., 

2017; Timsina et al., 2017). Moreover, there are features of public spaces that appear to 

increase the risk of a fall to older adults with escalators being one such example (DeMers et 

al., 2011). To ensure that persons remain safe in their use of public spaces, legislation exists 

that requires those responsible for such spaces to prevent and investigate injuries that 

occur (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). Failure to do so can lead fines and/or 213 

custodial sentence for those responsible but also there is a cost to the wider economy 

(Svedbom et al., 2013), and most importantly, the individual (Hughes et al, 2015; Sleney et 

al., 2014). This legislative requirement extends to the use of common public space features 

such as an escalator (‘The Workplace (Health, Safety and Welfare) Regulations 1992’). 

Escalator use in urban public spaces is a regular occurrence across many towns and 

cities (DeMers et al., 2011; Howland et al., 2012). Several annual escalator incidents 

(including fatalities) have been reported in the literature (McCann, 2013) and despite such 

occurrence, the reasons and causes of these incidents have received little attention in 

published academic literature (Howland et al., 2012; Pelczarski , 2018). This is of such 

concern that the Health and Safety Executive (HSE), the regulator for health and safety 

legislation, commissioned a shared research project aimed at investigating the causes of 

escalators incidents from a human factors perspective. Prior to this thesis, escalator injury 

literature had often focussed on epidemiological reviews of injuries, providing subjective 

interpretations of escalator incidents based on the frequency of incident occurrence (e.g., 

Page & Hough, 1989; Schminke et al., 2013). However, only consulting the frequency count 

of identified factors alone does little to inform a safety practitioner as to the reasons why a 

particular factor may be so relevant to the incident (Chi et al., 2006; Wang et al., 2022). 

7.1.2 The Current Thesis 

To address shortcomings in the literature as highlighted in the previous section, this 

thesis enhances current escalator injury understanding by adopting a novel human factors 

perspective (HSE, 1999; International Ergonomics Association, n.d.) to produce a new 

investigate taxonomy for incidents involving members of the public, the ‘Human Factors 

Incident Taxonomy for Members of the Public ‘(HFIT-MP). This was achieved by using a 214 

sequential mixed methods approach (Creswell & Clark, 2017; Hong et al. 2017; Sandelowski 

et al. 2006) that incorporates a Safety-III (i.e., combined Safety-I and Safety-II) ontological 

perspective (Aven, 2022; Leveson, 2020). Table 7.1 provides a summary of each of the 

chapter’s purpose, ontological perspective, data sources, main findings of the work. 215

Traditional taxonomic development approaches rely on a review of incident data 

(Mitchell et al., 2015; Hale et al., 2012), a typical Safety-I (reactive) approach (Read et al., 

2021) which is also the basis for the initial HFIT-MP development chapters (Chapters 3 & 4). 

Yet, the mixed methods approach adopted across this thesis adds rigour and reliability to 

the designed taxonomy. Specifically, each of the chapters build from one another adding to 

the development, refinement, and demonstration of the taxonomy’s application to provide 

a uniform means of investigate escalator incidents. A means called for but not observed in 

current escalator injury literature (Wang et al., 2022). 

The initial design of the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3) needed to be able to investigate 

incidents involving members of the public, for whom a safety management stance of a 

traditional organisational structure (i.e., an employer and an employee) does not apply (Ball 

& Ball-King; 2013; Ball-King, 2021). The HFIT-MP was therefore developed through a 

multidomain approach of: i) previous escalator injury research; ii) fall literature regarding 

stairways; iii) subject matter expert knowledge; and iv) review of existing human factors 

taxonomic approaches. This process resulted in an overall structure of four overarching 

factors (Figure 7.1). 218 

> Figure 7.1:
> Overarching Factors of the HFIT-MP

These overarching factors were underpinned by twenty-five PIFs that were initially 

identified as having the potential to influence an escalator incident. The resulting taxonomy 

was successfully applied (with high levels of kappa reliability scores across PIFs) to severe 

escalator incidents (Chapter 3). Consistent with wider escalator injury literature (McCann, 

2013; O’Neil, et al., 2008) falls were found to be the most common form of outcome factor. 

Moreover, the utility of the HFIT-MP taxonomy is that the aligned analysis of severe data in 

Chapter 3 provides target groups (e.g., age), actions (e.g., getting onto the escalator) and 

locations (e.g., using the descending escalator) to inform both subsequent research (e.g., 

Hunt et al., 2023) and the design of safety interventions, affording the consideration that 

escalator incidents are multifactorial rather than reliant on one specific event occurring (e.g. 

Chi et al., 2006). 

However, a limitation of the primary development of the HFIT-MP (Chapter 3, Table 

7.1) was that the taxonomy was derived from severe incident data. Based on the premise 

that less severe incidents occur and indeed may be a precursor to more severe incidents 

(McSween & Moran, 2017), it is important to highlight that attention should also be given to 

less severe and near-miss incidents to not underestimate injuries nor ignore PIFs that could 219 

lead to less severe injuries and thus further inform mitigation strategies (DeMers et al., 

2011). Therefore, following the sequential process (Creswell & Clark, 2017; Table 7.1) the 

HFIT-MP was applied to less severe incidents in Chapter 4 using data provided by HSE’s 

shared research group. Consistent with Chapter 3 and wider escalator injury literature 

(McCann, 2013; O’Neil, et al., 2008; Chi et al., 2006), falls were again the most common 

outcome factor. Further, the combined results of Chapters 3 and 4 suggest that irrespective 

of the severity of injury, escalator incidents are multifactorial, comprising of several PIFs as 

opposed to one key PIF (e.g., Chi et al., 2006). Moreover, the application of the HFIT-MP on 

less-severe incident data in Chapter 4 identified an additional four PIFs to be included, 

resulting from the increased detail of the industry incident reports. Moreover, no change 

was made to the overarching HFIT-MP structure and Kappa reliability scores remained high, 

emphasising the taxonomy’s utility to other data sets from what it was created. 

Challenges with the implementation of narrative incident reports (e.g., reliance on 

subjective interviews) in health and safety practice is well documented in the literature 

(Davies et al., 2007; Woodcock, 2008), leading to inconsistent findings, even when using the 

same incident reporting procedures within an organisation (Mitchell et al., 2015; Rossignol, 

2015). Indeed, the use of narrative incident reports are limited in scope as to what is 

recorded or not, evidenced by the high properties of unknown data sets (Chapters 3 and 4). 

To extend contemporary approaches in the wider falls literature (Robinovitch et al., 2013), 

Chapter 5 adopted an observational video coding approach of escalator users. Whilst the 

exploration of escalator incident footage has been utilised previously by Ayres (2019), their 

analysis only highlighted several examples of misuse such as sliding down handrails with no 

consideration of a multifactorial component to incidents. 220 

A further unique contribution to knowledge of Chapter 5 was the focus on escalator 

near misses (Table 7.1), an area overlooked in current escalator injury and falls literature. A 

near-miss is an event that whilst no physical injury or harm may come to the person 

involved, there was potential for harm to occur and therefore studying such instances can 

provide insight into the PIFs that may contribute to incidents (McSween & Moran, 2017), 

whilst wider safety literature has demonstrated that a high number of near misses can be 

indicative of a potential higher fatality rate (Moore et al. 2020; Yorio & Moore, 2018). 

Consistent with Chapters 3 and 4, incidents (in this case near misses) were found to be 

multifactorial. Whilst it is not clear how many near misses are needed to occur before an 

incident happens (Moore et al. 2020; Yorio & Moore, 2018), the analysis of PIFs associated 

with near misses, indicated people who hesitate when stepping onto the escalator appeared 

to be six times more likely to have a near miss (Chapter 5). This finding builds on evidence 

developed across Chapters 3 and 4, where hesitation had rarely (if at all) been identified as 

a PIF in narrative incident reports. This finding demonstrates the value of adopting an 

observational method to identify key PIFs. Hesitation was one of six new factors that were 

introduced to the HFIT-MP and whilst the overarching HFIT-MP structure remained the 

same, this finding lends support to the sequential mixed-method approach adopted in the 

thesis. Two PIFs ‘Escalator location (i.e., top, middle or bottom)’ and ‘Getting on or off the 

escalator’ were combined as the video footage provided more detailed information to 

record the location that an escalator near miss occurred. 

Unlike reviewing incident data (Chapters 3 and 4) there were no ‘unknowns’ coded 

in Chapter 5 as the use of video footage meant factors could be coded with greater accuracy 

as either present or not (Ayres, 2019; Cocca et al., 2016), a finding that was further 

substantiated with a Kappa reliability analysis. However, caution is still warranted as certain 221 

PIFs such as age were estimated. Whilst the Kappa statistic indicated substantial agreement 

(.747) of age estimation in Chapter 5, evidence suggest that can be inaccuracies during 

human estimations of age (Abousaleh et al., 2016) and therefore, there have been proposals 

that computer-based analysis of faces may offer a means to overcome such complications 

(Hadid & Pietkainen, 2013). Despite such limitation, the video coding method adopted in 

Chapter 5 allowed for the development of an invaluable Safety-II premise (Hollnagle, 2008) 

and missing piece in escalator incident literature; a baseline of how persons successfully 

(i.e., without incident) use an escalator. For example, the results indicate the handrail was 

used on 58% of occasions, yet persons not using the handrail were twice as likely to have a 

near miss. Such baseline understanding could be used to measure the impact of an 

intervention aimed at improving handrail use, such as design to encourage use (Jacobs, 

2016) that could reduce near misses and by extension, falls. 

The use of the HFIT-MP when coding video footage of people who had no observable 

incident (i.e., had no near-miss) presented a challenge as some of the HFIT-MP factors were 

no longer applicable (e.g., what stage of the journey did the incident occur? On/Transition 

1/Traveling/Transition 2/Off). This is not to say the HFIT-MP was not successful in coding all 

users, rather specific PIFs within the HFIT-MP can only be applied when an incident 

(including a near miss) has taken place. This is not a critique of the taxonomy as such, as it is 

designed for incident investigation, but highlights that when looking to incorporate a Safety-

II perspective (Hollnagel, 2008) factors that are specifically associated with an incident (i.e., 

when something goes wrong) are not applicable, a finding that will be considered further in 

Section 7.2.2 .

Extant escalator injury literature (Chapter 2, Table 2.1) and the current work 

undertaken using the HFIT-MP (Chapters, 3-4) consistently identified that the sub-group of 222 

people that most experienced escalator injuries were those over the age of 65. However, 

examination of the incident reports (Chapters 3 and 4) and observation (Chapter 5), whilst 

identifying PIFs associated with incidents/near misses does not make it clear why those over 

the age of 65 may be at increased risk of falls on escalators. To extrapolate understanding, a 

qualitative approach consisting of semi-structured interviews with 25 persons aged over 65 

was conducted to understand their experiences of escalator use, the potential causes of 

escalator injuries, and what could be done to prevent escalator incidents for this age group 

(Table 7.1). This qualitative approach adopted is advocated in mixed methods research to 

enhance understanding of initial quantitative data (Creswell & Clark, 2017) and sits within 

the Safety-II ontological perspective of proactively exploring safe and unsafe behaviours 

(Debnath et al., 2015; Hollnagel, 2008). Previously only Hanks (1996) have considered a 

qualitative approach to persons perceptions and attitudes towards escalators. However, 

their research was limited to solely identifying areas of concern for injury around the six 

following factors: 1) getting caught or stuck; 2) getting on or off; 3) trouble operating the 

equipment; 4) actions of other riders; 5) equipment not operating safely; and 6) what to do 

in an emergency, with no further exploration of these themes. 

Conducting reflexive thematic analysis (Braun & Clarke, 2006;2022), Chapter 6 

produced a potential key element of preventing an escalator injury is during the 

embarkation, with participants highlighting the perceived need to ensure that the feet are 

placed accurately onto the escalator step, irrespective of whether a person believed they 

would adopt a potentially maladaptive action such as hesitation (Chapter 5) or not. Further, 

escalators were perceived to be safe to use and were commonly used by older adults as 

they were prominent and convenient compared to alternatives (stairs of lifts). Comparing 

the results with the HFIT-MP developed across Chapters 3 – 5, similarities were observed 223 

with the constructed themes in terms of the Individual (hesitant vs effortless user – coded 

as hesitation in the HFIT-MP, risk perception of escalators – a new PIF to added to the HFIT-

MP); Task (need for accurate embarkation – coded as loss of footing in the HFIT-MP) and 

Environmental (the prominence and convenience of the escalator; the impact of escalator 

speed and the impact of escalator step depth – three new PIF to be included in the HFIT-

MP) factors that appear to be influencing the actions and use of an escalator. These results 

therefore help demonstrate the utility of the qualitative approach to add additional insight 

into the causes of escalator incidents beyond a review of incident reports alone (Creswell & 

Clark, 2017; Hanson et al., 2011). Further, the use of a Safety-II perspective helps to provide 

additional understanding as to the potential causes of escalator incidents that traditional 

Safety-I (Chapters 3-5) do not provide (Hollnagel, 2008). 

The final HFIT-MP model is provided in Figure 7.2 and illustrates all those PIFs that 

have been identified through the adopted sequential mixed methods approach in this 

thesis, which is underpinned by a Safety-III ontological perspective (i.e., the need to 

combine Safety-I and Safety-II). 224 

Figure 7.2: 

Final HFIT-MP for Investigating Escalator Incidents 225 

225 

In summary, the overall aim of the current thesis was to develop a human factors 

taxonomy for the investigation of incidents involving members of the public when using 

escalators. In so doing, this thesis advanced current knowledge and informed potential 

mitigation strategies regarding severe escalator incidents (Chapter 3), with further 

applicability to low severity incidents (Chapter 4). Further, the thesis applied the developed 

taxonomy beyond narrative incident reports to an observational data set as means to 

compensate for narrative incidents report deficiencies (Chapter 5). Finally, the thesis 

explored the experiences of those most at risk persons as identified by the taxonomy in 

Chapters 3 - 5, to explore potential undiscovered PIFs that traditional Safety-I approaches 

(i.e., Chapters 3-5) may not highlight (Chapter 6). The mixed methods approach adopted 

was beneficial to the taxonomy development as the initial quantitative work was 

complemented and enhanced by the subsequent studies as to the causes of escalator 

incidents (Creswell & Clark, 2017). The results further emulate a Safety-III (Leveson, 2020) 

ontological perspective to use both reactive (i.e., Safety-I, incident reports) and proactive 

(Safety-II) ideologies to manage safety (Aven, 2022). The next sections consider the 

implications of the assembled works and potential future directions the research could take. 

7.2 Theoretical Implications for Investigating Public Space Incidents: Escalators 

7.2.1 Escalator Incidents are Multifaceted 

In accordance with incident investigation literature (Delikhoon et al. 2022; Katsakiori 

et al., 2009; Larouzee & Le Coze, 2020; Reason, 1990; Sklet, 2004) this thesis supports the 

notion that escalator incidents should not be simply attributed to one major contributing 

factor such as “not holding the handrail” (Chi et al., 2006). Moreover, akin to Wang et al. 

(2022) the results of Chapters 3-5 provide evidence to support viewing escalator incidents 226 

226 

from a human factors systems perspective (Read et al., 2021; Salmon et al., 2017) as the 

development and utility of a new multifactorial investigative taxonomy - the HFIT-MP - has 

demonstrated that escalator incidents are resultant from a combination of PIFs centred 

around the: i) individual; ii) the task; iii) the environment; and iv) the outcome of the 

incident. For example, just being over the age of 65 will not lead to an escalator incident as 

shown by the lack of observable falls for this age group in Chapter 5. However, when 

combined with other PIFs, such as such as losing one’s footing (i.e., stumbling) when getting 

on a descending escalator and traveling alone, this combination of factors appears to 

increase the potential for a severe falling injury to occur. 

Previously, Al-Sharif (2006) proposed their own multifactorial model for escalator 

incidents ( Section 2.2.1 , Figure 2.3), which has subsequently been used as the basis for 

other escalator incident research (e.g., Wang et al., 2022). However, it is unclear how Al-

Sharif’s (2006) model was developed, nor was empirical evidence provided to substantiate 

its initial application to escalator incidents. In contrast, Chapters 3-5 of this thesis illustrate 

the HFIT-MP’s development and utility to explore escalator incidents from a multifactorial 

lens. Moreover, the incident data used in this thesis from 154 local authorities across Great 

Britain, four retail organisations, and two travel organisations (one airport and one rail 

network) is therefore far more comprehensive than previous multifactorial escalator 

research (Wang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2022; Xie & Liu, 2019), which has also tended to 

focus solely on metro station escalator incidents. 

The results of the current thesis suggest that methodological approaches, which do 

not consider the multifactorial interactions between PIFs (Howland et al., 2012; Page & 

Hough, 1989; Schminke et al., 2013) may offer an incomplete understanding of the factors 

leading to escalator incidents. Therefore, a key recommendation from this thesis is that 227 

227 

future escalator incident research should explore escalator incidents as a combination of 

several PIFs, not as the result of action/inaction of one PIF. Use of this thesis’ multifactorial 

model will allow health and safety professionals to ensure they identify appropriate control 

measures and mitigation strategies to comply with the legal duties (Health and Safety at 

Work etc. Act, 1974) and potentially reduce the risk to as low as reasonably practicable. 

7.2.2 Incorporate Safety-I, II and III Ontological Perspectives and Methods to Provide 

Greater Understanding of Escalator Incidents. 

This thesis used a sequential mixed methods approach (Creswell & Clark, 2017) to 

develop the HFIT-MP and thus explore escalator incidents: i) the review of incident data 

(Chapters 3 and 4); ii) the use of observational coding of escalator footage (Chapter 5); and 

iii) a qualitative approach, comprising semi-structured interviews with adults aged 65+ years 

(Chapter 6). The combination of approaches provides a unique approach from a human 

factors perspective as this combination encompasses Safety-I (learning from when things go 

wrong), Safety-II (learning from when things go right) and Safety-III (combination of safety-I 

and II) ontological stances (Hollnagle, 2008, Leveson, 2020). Specifically, the results of 

Chapters 3 and 4 highlight the traditional Safety-I ethos that we can understand the cause 

of escalator incidents as a by-product of reviewing incident reports (Hollnagle, 2008; 

Hollnagle et al., 2015; Read et al., 2021). This is the typical approach for developing an 

incident taxonomy (Mitchell et al., 2015; Wiegmann & Shappell, 2001; Woodcock, 2008). 

Yet this thesis complements the development of the HFIT-MP and the understanding of 

escalator incidents with both Safety-I and Safety-II approaches in Chapters 5 and 6 

(Hollnagel, 2008; Hollnagle et al., 2015). Chapter 5 provides a unique insight, coding over 

4,700 escalator users to establish a baseline of the proportion and actions taken of persons 

who use an escalator without incident (i.e., a Safety-II perspective). In Safety-I approaches 228 

228 

to escalator incidents, studies have highlighted that when people carry out other tasks such 

as carrying of luggage this could be one of the main causes of escalator injuries (Chi et al., 

2006; Xing et al., 2019). In contrast to such suggestion, Chapter 5 revealed that over 50% of 

persons carry only one item, and 38% carry more than one item, with all persons seemingly 

doing so successfully without escalator incident, indicating that a ban of carrying any item 

on the escalator may not be proportional to the risk. Thus, more nuanced understanding, 

such as carrying particularly large items that should be considered as targets for safety 

management interventions (Wang et al., 2020). Further, the use of observational data in 

Chapter 5 provided additional PIFs that were not as significant in the review of incident 

data, such as hesitation leading to a person being six times more likely to have an escalator 

near miss. Whilst hesitancy had not previously been identified as pertinent escalator injury 

literature, there is suggestion that hesitant behaviours can increase stepping errors in older 

adults (St George et al., 2007), which could be maladaptive when embarking the moving 

surface (Hsu et al, 2015; Hunt et al., 2023). Chapter 6’s qualitative approach provides 

greater insight as to why those over the age of 65 more frequently appear in the incident 

reports of Chapters 3 and 4. Respondents highlighted that successful escalator use is 

dependent on accurate foot placement onto the escalator steps, irrespective of whether 

they would adopt a maladaptive action such as hesitation (Chapter 5). Further, respondents 

provided new insights leading to the addition of new PIFs to the HFIT-MP regarding a lack of 

risk perception of escalators in general, with this mode of transport being perceived as easy 

to locate and safe to use. Neither of these PIFs are apparent in current escalator injury 

literature (Chapter 2) that is reliant on the review of incident reports (e.g., Chi et al., 2006; 

Wang et al., 2022). . 229 

229 

This thesis demonstrates that by changing from a traditional reactive (Safety-I, 

Chapters 3 and 4) approach to safety management (Hollnagel, 2008), the introduction of 

proactive methodologies (Safety-II, and Safety-III - a review of incident data, use of MCA, 

observational date and qualitative interview data: Chapters 5 and 6) can increase the 

understanding of potential hazards that can lead to an incident and thus inform mitigation 

strategies to meet legislative requirements (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). 

Specifically, PIFs have been highlighted when things go wrong (i.e., a fall) as well as PIFs 

associated with successful escalator use (i.e., ensuring accurate foot placement, lack of 

hesitation). It should be considered that such approaches could be adopted in the future to 

explore other incidents leading to the wider adoption of a Safety-III ontological stance. 

7.3 Practical Implications 

7.3.1 Application of the HFIT-MP to the Safety Management of Public Spaces: Escalators 

The HFIT-MP developed in this thesis is specifically designed with a member of the 

public in mind (Chapters 3-5) as such, the HFIT-MP has the potential to help safety 

managers in their identification of PIFs that may lead to an escalator incident. Sklet (2004) 

and Katsakiori et al. (2009) suggested that the selection of an investigation method should 

address the need for: graphical representation, facilitation of communication, and to help 

identify information gaps. As such, the HFIT-MP developed as part of this thesis does so by 

providing a unique member of the public centred incident taxonomy whose output from 

MCA provides a visual representation of those PIFs that occur most frequently together 

(Chapters 3-5). The HFIT-MP also provides a means to identify and explore PIFs that can 

influence when a member of the public is injured undertaking an activity in a public space 

(i.e., an escalator fall), providing safety practitioners with means to identify and understand 230 

230 

target groups (e.g., age), actions (e.g., carrying multiple items), and locations (e.g., 

ascending escalator) that lead to an incident. Therefore, in consideration of fulfilling one’s 

legal duty to Section 3 of the Health and Safety at Work Act (1974), it is not unreasonable 

for a HSE inspector to consider the findings of this work regarding the management of 

escalator incidents, as to whether enough is being done to ensure safety of at-risk 

populations (i.e., those over 65) when embarking an escalator in a public space (Health and 

Safety Executive, 2013). By way of example, as part of the shared research programme, the 

insights gained in this thesis were used by one of the UK’s largest airports to restructure 

passenger check in. Recognising passengers over 65 to be a pertinent risk factor for 

escalator use, the airport has moved flights that have a higher proportion of over 65 

passengers to a lower floor, meaning they must traverse fewer escalators for their 

departures, thus reducing the potential for an incident to occur. Whilst it is not the 

responsibility of the HSE to tell persons how to manage the risks in their organisation, this 

example shows how the information gained in this thesis can reduce the risk to at risk 

populations when no such traditional employee-employer relationship exists. 

The PIFs identified by the HFIT-MP can also be used in a proactive means to alert 

safety managers of potential at-risk escalator users. For example, using a combination of 

artificial intelligence and real-time escalator video footage, research has begun to develop 

automatic escalator monitoring systems that can look for and identify specific PIFs (Jiao et 

al. 2022; Osipov et al., 2022). However, these advancements are limited by previous 

literature that has failed to identify the pertinent PIFs that are associated with escalator 

injuries. For example, based on previous escalator incident literature (Chi et al., 2006; Wang 

et al., 2020, Xing et al., 2019), the system developed by Jiao et al. (2022) focussed on the 

three PIFs of: i) loss of balance; ii) not holding the handrail; and iii) carrying large items. 231 

231 

Whilst not holding the handrail was identified as a PIF for escalator falls (Chapters 3 and 4), 

this was in addition to other PIFs occurring more frequently together and thus should not be 

considered in isolation. Moreover, Chapter 3 highlighted that hesitant users are six times 

more likely to have a near miss, whereas with lack of handrail-use, a person is only twice as 

likely to have a near miss. Therefore, this thesis highlights PIFs and combinations of PIFs that 

a more pertinent to escalator incidents occurring, and this should be considered as part of 

any automated shut down / alarm system in regard to the management of safety. 

7.3.2 Review of Escalator Standards 

There are several standards available that are designed to help escalator 

operators/owners meet their legislative requirements and improve the safety of escalator 

use (Filippone et al., 2012). The current focus of these standards is centred around the 

construction, installation, and maintenance of escalators; for example, an emergency stop 

button with a red actuator for emergency situations shall be provided at each landing of the 

escalator (PD ISO/TS 8103-6:2017, British Standards 2017). Whilst the inclusion of an 

emergency stop button meets the legislative requirement Regulation 19 of The Workplace 

(Health, Safety and Welfare) Regulations (1992), there is limited reference in the current 

escalator standards to interventions aimed at passenger behaviours beyond the 

requirement to include safety signs to inform passengers of safe conduct (British Standards 

2018) or stating the use of shopping trolleys and baggage cars is unsafe and should be 

prevented (British Standards Institute, 2017; 2021). 

There is a current development of a Global Essentials Safety Requirements (GESR) 

(British Standards 2018) that uses information from independent research to inform safe 

escalator use. The findings from this thesis could be used to inform an update to GESR. For 

example, Chapters 3-5 indicate that escalator injuries and near misses frequently involve 232 

232 

those over the age of 65, whilst Chapter 6 demonstrated a low-risk perception with this age 

group of escalator use, even though there is a recognition of the challenges to embark the 

moving steps. A potential consideration for future inclusions to the GESR could be to specify 

the construction of escalators away from store/travel station entrances. Preference instead 

could be given to alternatives (i.e., stairs or lifts) recognising users tend to select the closest 

means of transport when entering a store/travel station (Zhang et al., 2015; Zacharias & 

Tang, 2015). Chapter 6 established that alternatives are hard to locate and thus not actively 

searched for by older adults when the escalator offers a more inviting means of moving 

between floors. Yet such considerations would need balancing as research suggests higher 

rates of fatalities when using lifts compared to escalators (McCann, 2013), albeit 

transportation comparisons are limited (McCann, 2013; Howland et al., 2012) and future 

works would be required to establish which mode of transport is the safer option. 

Additionally, the inclusion of safety signs whilst beneficial to convey the risk of 

escalator use (British Standards Institute, 2017; Chew et al., 2021) may require review in 

terms of their usability as although small in terms of representation of the estimated 3.5 

billion daily escalator users (Elevatori, 2018), none of the 25 persons interviewed in Chapter 

6 reporting as having seen the mandatory blue safety signs British Standards Institute, 

2017). Therefore, another potential suggestion for the GSER could be to consider the need 

to have signs regularly updated and refreshed. Previous research on signs to encourage stair 

use has shown the effectiveness diminishes after a two-week period with passengers no 

longer reporting seeing the signs (Allais et al., 2017), however a balance would need to be 

struck as refreshing safety signs every two weeks would be a significant financial burden. 

Future research could seek to establish the effectiveness of escalator safety signs to reduce 

risk allowing safety managers to make the case for a return on investment of updating the 233 

233 

signs more regularly. In a similar vein, the current standards convey that: i) small children 

shall be held firmly; ii) dogs shall be carried; iii) use the handrail; and iv) push chairs are not 

permitted. Yet it would appear the greatest risk is the embarkation of the moving steps. 

Therefore, signage could instead be altered to provide users with warning of ensuring they 

board the step accurately onto the moving platform (Chapter 6). However, signage should 

not be the only mitigation measures considered. Recent work by Hunt et al. (2023) 

established that successful embarkation of the moving surface is influenced by a person’s 

action capabilities and the environmental demand with older persons at risk of falling when 

embarking moving surfaces that demand accuracy. Therefore, any updated of the GSER 

standard should consider signage as only one part of suite of interventions recognising this 

thesis main finding that escalator incidents are multifactorial and thus require a 

multifactorial intervention. 

7.4 Considerations for Future Research 

7.4.1 Application of the HFIT-MP Beyond Escalators 

Whilst the HFIT-MP has been successfully applied to escalator incidents, future 

research should look to assess its applicability to other public space environments where 

there is a legal duty (in Great Britain at least) to ensure any hazards to members of the 

public are identified and the risks of such hazards are reduced to as reasonably practicable 

(Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). For example, previous research has highlighted 

incidents where members of the public have been injured using amusement park rides 

(Woodcock, 2008), swimming pools (Peden & Franklin, 2020; Taylor et al., 2020) playground 

equipment (Keays & Skinner, 2012) or crowded environments such as festivals (Haslam & 

Filingeri, 2019). Akin to escalator incidents in these scenarios, there is lack of an employer-234 

234 

employee relationship for which current legislation and safety management systems and 

resources are typically designed (Ball & Ball-King; 2013; Ball-King, 2021). It is likely that some 

of the PIFs - for example, if the ascending or descending escalator was used - will not be 

applicable to playground equipment. Rather it is the overarching factors of: i) individual; ii) 

the task; iii) the environment; and iv) the outcome of the incident that could form the basis 

for future investigative works. Therefore, by applying the HFIT-MP in these circumstances it 

may be a means to bridge the gap when managing risks to members of the public. Doing so 

would better enable health and safety managers to meet their legislative requirements 

(Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974), acting as an effective means to investigate 

incidents for members of the public. 

7.4.2 Biomechanical Inquiry Into Escalator use 

Whilst this research provides identification of those PIFs that influence escalator 

incidents, questions remain as to why and how these PIFs may be influencing an escalator 

incident. For example, Chapters 3-6 highlight that the embarkation phase is a crucial 

element of using the escalator successfully and for older adults (more frequently appearing 

in incident reports), there is a need to ensure they establish successful foot placement when 

doing so. Yet the biomechanics of embarking an escalator is limited to two studies (Hsu et 

al., 2015; Hunt et al., 2023). The more recent work by Hunt et al. (2023), is a progressive 

step forward for understanding how a person uses an escalator. Hunt et al’s (2023) work 

indicated that when embarking moving surfaces, older adults used different stepping 

behaviours to younger adults to reconcile their reduced physical capabilities. As the older 

adult demographic has been identified as at risk of incident (Chapters 3-5), it could be 

suggested that PIFs which may influence this group should be explored. For example, 235 

235 

Chapter 6 highlighted the perceived need for accurate foot placement onto the escalator 

steps. One possible solution could be to explore the impact of using stairway interventions 

such as increasing the contrast edging of the step (Bjelica et al., 2021; Foster et al., 2014; 

Harper et al. 2022) or increasing step illumination (Hamel et al. 2005; Thomas et al., 2020) 

to improve stepping and foot clearance. However, what is not clear is what impact the 

moving stairs will have on these interventions compared to a typical stationary staircase. 

Alternatively in Chapter 6 there was a suggestion that altering the escalator’s speed 

or step dimensions may allow for successful embarkations, presently most escalators speeds 

are on average set a 0.50 m.s −1 (CIBSE, 2015; Chapter 5) and steps are 0.38m deep (CIBSE, 

2015), yet it is not clear if this is the most suitable speed or step depth for escalator 

embarkations, with no current research available to assess would be the most suitable step 

speed or depth for successful escalator embarkation. Through the steering committee of the 

HSE escalators shared research programme, it was suggested that one of the retail 

organisations had reduced the speed of their escalators to 0.40 m.s −1 , in so doing they had 

seen a decrease in the number of incidents. However, it was not possible to substantiate 

this in the observational work (Chapter 5) due to permissioning of setting up cameras in this 

store’s environment. None had explored the possibility of step depth as this was not a PIF 

the stakeholder group had considered to be influential. Future research is necessary to 

explore the impact escalator speed and step depth has on successful embarkations. 

7.5 Conclusion 

This thesis aimed to develop a human factors taxonomy for incidents involving 

members of the public that can be used to help organisations investigate incidents and thus 

meet legislative requirements (Health and Safety at Work etc. Act, 1974). This was achieved 

using a sequential mixed methods approach (Hong et al. 2017; Sandelowski et al. 2006), 236 

236 

whilst adopting a Safety-III ontological perspective (Aven, 2022; Leveson, 2020), recognising 

that traditional taxonomies have been developed with a traditional organisation structure in 

mind (HSE, 1999; Kyriakidis et al., 2018) and that multiple approaches (i.e. Safety-I and 

Safety-II) are required to increase knowledge on how to improve safety (Dolan et al., 2022; 

Klockner et al., 2021). 

The work concludes that escalator incidents involving members of the public should 

be considered as multifactorial with the current thesis providing a uniform means to identify 

and explore multiple PIFs that may lead to an escalator incident. Such analyses can be used 

to inform mitigation measures such as automated monitoring systems that can respond in 

the event certain combinations of PIFs being identified (Jiao et al., 2022; Osipov et al., 

2022). In addition, the results of the current work can be adopted into the GESR standard 

(PD ISO/TS 8103-6:2017, British Standards 2017) for safer escalator use in terms of the 

importance of citing escalators in consideration with alternative means of transport (i.e., 

lifts or stairs) or the utility of safety signs to convey messages of risk. 

Further, the presented work suggests the need to consider the investigation of 

incidents from a Safety-III perspective. Through a combination of, learning when things go 

wrong (Safety-I: Chapters 3-5) and when things go right (Safety-II: Chapters 5-6), it can be 

possible to increase understanding about the potential causes and mitigation strategies of 

incidents beyond the traditional review of incident reports (e.g., Chi et al., 2006). In future 

works, the presented taxonomy has the potential to be adopted to other public space 

environments such as children’s play parks (Brussoni et al., 2015) or swimming pools (Peden 

& Franklin, 2020; Taylor et al., 2020). The design of the investigative taxonomy is such that it 

encapsulates incidents where the traditional employee-employer relationship does not 

exist, such as a child playing at a park (Ball & Ball-King; 2013; Ball-King, 2021). The 237 

237 

application of this multifactorial model would allow for a wider understanding of such 

incidents and by applying it to different scenarios, would cement the taxonomy’s utility in 

investigating incidents involving members of the public beyond escalators. Furthermore, the 

presented works highlight a need for further in-depth biomechanical research for escalators 

that focus on embarkation behaviours and what mitigation strategies (e.g., reduction in 

escalator speed) would have on successful embarkations. 238 

238
